O_{a},O_{b}\in\mathcal{A}
with maximal eigenvectors
\ket{\psi_{A}},\ket{\psi_{B}}
such that for any
i\neq a
and
j\neq b

\displaystyle\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{a}\ket{\psi_{A}}>\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{i}\ket{\psi_{A}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bra{\psi_{B}}O_{b}\ket{\psi_{B}}>\bra{\psi_{B}}O_{j}\ket{\psi_{B}}.

(3.14)

Theorem 3.2.1
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
. For any policy
\pi
and action set of traceless observables
\mathcal{A}
that obeys condition (
3.14
) there exists an environment
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
such that,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]\geq C_{\mathcal{A}}\sqrt{T},

(3.15)

where
C_{\mathcal{A}}>0
is a constant that depends on the action set.

Proof 3.2.2
.

Choose two operators
O_{a},O_{b}\in\mathcal{A}
that obey the condition in (
3.14
) with maximal eigenvectors
\ket{\psi_{A}}
and
\ket{\psi_{B}}
, respectively. Define the following environments:

\displaystyle\rho:=\frac{1-\Delta}{d}\mathbb{I}+\Delta\ket{\psi_{A}}\!\bra{\psi_{A}},\quad\rho^{\prime}:=\frac{1-\Delta}{d}\mathbb{I}+\Delta\ket{\psi_{B}}\!\bra{\psi_{B}},

(3.16)

for some constant
0\leq\Delta\leq\frac{1}{2}
to be defined later. Note that
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}\geq 0
. Define

\displaystyle c=\min\left\{\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{a}\ket{\psi_{A}}-\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{i}\ket{\psi_{A}},\bra{\psi_{B}}O_{b}\ket{\psi_{B}}-\bra{\psi_{B}}O_{j}\ket{\psi_{B}}\right\},

(3.17)

for
i\neq a
and
j\neq b
. Using the expression for the regret in (
2.22
), we can compute the regret for
\rho
as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\EX_{\rho,\pi}(T_{i}(T))\Delta\left(\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{a}\ket{\psi_{A}}-\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{i}\ket{\psi_{A}}\right)

(3.18)

where we have used that the observables are traceless and

\displaystyle\max_{O_{i}\in\mathcal{A}}\Tr(\rho O_{i})=\Tr(\rho O_{a})=\Delta\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{a}\ket{\psi_{A}}.

(3.19)

Note that for
i=a
the sub-optimality gaps in (
2.21
) are zero. Thus, using Condition (
3.14
) we can bound the regret as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]=\sum_{i\neq a}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{i}(T)]\Delta\left(\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{a}\ket{\psi_{A}}-\bra{\psi_{A}}O_{i}\ket{\psi_{A}}\right)\geq\Delta c\sum_{i\neq a}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{i}(T)],

(3.20)

where
c>0
. Using that
T=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{i}(T)]
and Markov inequality we have,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]\geq\Delta c\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[T-T_{a}(T)\right]\geq\frac{cn\Delta}{2}P_{\rho,\pi}\left(T_{a}(T)\leq\frac{T}{2}\right).

(3.21)

Similarly, the regret for
\rho^{\prime}
can be bounded as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)\right]\geq\Delta\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[T_{a}(T)]\left(\bra{\psi_{B}}O_{b}\ket{\psi_{B}}-\bra{\psi_{B}}O_{i}\ket{\psi_{B}}\right),

(3.22)

where we have taken into account just the term with
i=a
. Using Condition (
3.14
) and Markov inequality we have,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)\right]\geq\frac{cT\Delta}{2}P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left(T_{a}(T)>\frac{T}{2}\right).

(3.23)

Thus, combining (
3.21
) and (
3.23
),

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)\right]\geq\frac{cT\Delta}{2}\left(P_{\rho,\pi}\left(T_{a}(T)\leq\frac{n}{2}\right)+P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left(T_{a}(T)>\frac{T}{2}\right)\right).

(3.24)

Using Lemma
3.1.1
we can bound the above expression as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)\right]\geq\frac{cT\Delta}{4}\exp\left(-D(P_{\rho,\pi}\|P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi})\right).

(3.25)

Using Lemma
3.1.4
combined with the data-processing inequality, we can bound the Kullback-divergence as,

\displaystyle D(P_{\rho,\pi}\|P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi})=

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{k}\EX_{\rho,\pi}(T_{a}(T))D\big(P_{\rho}(\cdot|a)\big\|P_{\rho^{\prime}}(\cdot|a)\big))\leq D(\rho\|\rho^{\prime})\sum_{a=1}^{k}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{a}(T)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle TD(\rho\|\rho^{\prime}).

(3.26)

where
D(\rho\|\rho^{\prime})=\Tr\rho\log\rho-\Tr\rho\log\rho^{\prime}
is the relative entropy between
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
.
Now define
f(\Delta)=D(\rho\|\rho^{\prime})
for the corresponding expressions of
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
and note that
f(0)=0
since for
\Delta=0
,
\rho=\rho^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}(0)=0
using the convexity of the quantum relative entropy. Thus, using Taylor’s theorem, we can express
f(\Delta)
as,

\displaystyle f(\Delta)=\frac{\Delta^{2}}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(\chi),

(3.27)

for some
\chi\in[0,\Delta]
. Define,

\displaystyle c_{f}=\max f^{\prime\prime}(\chi)\quad\text{for}\quad\chi\in\left[0,\frac{1}{2}\right].

(3.28)

Note that
f^{\prime\prime}(\chi)
is well defined since the states
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
have full support, and then
f(\chi)
is a smooth function. Thus using that
f^{\prime\prime}(\chi)\leq c_{f}
for
\Delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
we can plug the above expression into (
3.26
) and using (
3.25
) we have,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)\right]\geq\frac{cT\Delta}{4}\exp\left(-\frac{T\Delta^{2}}{2}c_{f}\right).

(3.29)

Finally, if we choose
\Delta=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{T}}
,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)\right]\geq\frac{c}{8}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{8}c_{f}\right)\sqrt{T},

(3.30)

and the result follows using

\displaystyle 2\max\left\{\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right],\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)\right]\right\}\geq\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)\right]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)\right].

(3.31)

3.2.1
Pauli observables

In this section, we analyze the case of Pauli observables to show how the number of available actions affects the regret bound. If we consider a
d=2^{m}-
dimensional (
m
qubits) Hilbert space, there are
d^{2}
different Pauli observables and they can be expressed as the
m
-fold tensor product of the
2\times 2
Pauli matrices. Let
\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{d^{2}}
denote all the possible Pauli observables. Each
\sigma_{i}
can be expressed as
\sigma_{i}=\Pi^{+}_{i}-\Pi^{-}_{i}
, where
\Pi^{+}_{i},\Pi^{-}_{i}
are projectors associated to the
+1
and
-1
subspaces and they describe the 2 possible outcomes when we perform measurements using Pauli observables.

Theorem 3.2.3
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
. For any policy
\pi
and action set of observables
\mathcal{A}
comprised of
k
distinct length-
m
strings of single-qubit Pauli observables for
d=2^{m}
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an environment
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
such that

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]\geq\frac{3}{100}\sqrt{(k-1)T},

(3.32)

for
T\geq 2(k-1)
.

Proof 3.2.4
.

The case
k=1
is trivial since
R_{T}=0
always. Suppose
k>1
and let
0\leq\Delta\leq\frac{1}{3}
be a constant to be chosen later. Pick
\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{A}
and define the following environment,

\displaystyle\rho:=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{d}+\frac{\Delta}{d}\sigma_{1}.

(3.33)

Define

\displaystyle l:=\argmin_{j>1}\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[T_{j}(n)\right]

(3.34)

as the index for the least expected picked observable different from
\sigma_{1}
. Define a second environment as,

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}:=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{d}+\frac{\Delta}{d}\sigma_{1}+\frac{2\Delta}{d}\sigma_{l}.

(3.35)

Note that
\rho\geq 0
and
\rho^{\prime}\geq 0
since
0\leq\Delta\leq\frac{1}{3}
.
In order to compute the sub-optimality gaps (
2.21
) we compute the following quantities for
\sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\Tr(\rho\sigma_{i})=\frac{\Delta}{d}\Tr(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{i})=\begin{cases}\Delta\quad\text{if}\quad i=1.\\
0\quad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.36)

\displaystyle\Tr(\rho^{\prime}\sigma_{i})=\frac{\Delta}{d}\left(\Tr(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{i})+2\Tr(\sigma_{l}\sigma_{i})\right)=\begin{cases}2\Delta\quad i=l.\\
\Delta\quad i=1.\\
0\quad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(3.37)

For
\rho
the sub-optimality (
2.21
) gaps are
\Delta_{i}=\Delta
for
i\neq 1
and
\Delta_{i}=0
for
i=1
. Thus we can compute the regret as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]=\sum_{a=1}^{k}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{a}(T)]\Delta_{a}=\Delta\sum_{a\neq 1}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{a}(T)]

(3.38)

Using Markov’s inequality and
T=\sum_{i=a}^{k}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{a}(T)]
,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]=\Delta\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[T-T_{1}(T)\right]\geq\frac{n\Delta}{2}P_{\rho,\pi}\left(T_{1}(T)\leq\frac{n}{2}\right).

(3.39)

For
\rho^{\prime}
we bound the regret for the term
i=1
, using that the sub-optimality gap is
\Delta_{1}=\Delta
and Markov’s inequality we have,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)]=\sum_{a=1}^{k}\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[T_{a}(T)]\Delta_{a}\geq\Delta\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[T_{1}(T)]\geq\frac{T\Delta}{2}P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\left(T_{1}(T)>\frac{T}{2}\right).

(3.40)

Combining (
3.39
),(
3.40
) and the Bretagnolle-Huber inequality (
3.1.1
) we have,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)]\geq\frac{T\Delta}{4}\exp\left(-D(P_{\rho,\pi}\|P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi})\right).

(3.41)

Note that the probabilities of the rewards are just Bernoulli distributions since the observables
\sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
have two outcomes, +1 and -1. Using Lemma
3.1.4
we can decompose the relative entropy as

\displaystyle D(P_{\rho,\pi}\|P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi})=\sum_{a=1}^{k}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{a}(T)]D\big(P_{\rho}(\cdot|a)\big\|P_{\rho^{\prime}}(\cdot|a)\big).

(3.42)

Note that
P_{\rho}(\cdot|a),P_{\rho^{\prime}}(\cdot|a)
are not equal only when
a=l
, so
D\big(P_{\rho}(\cdot|a)\big\|P_{\rho^{\prime}}(\cdot|a)\big)=0
for
a\neq l
and,

\displaystyle D(P_{\rho,\pi},P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi})=\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{l}(T)]D\big(P_{\rho}(\cdot|l)\big\|P_{\rho^{\prime}}(\cdot|l)\big).

(3.43)

Using (
3.36
) and (
3.37
) we have

\displaystyle P_{\rho}(1|l)=\frac{1}{2},\quad P_{\rho^{\prime}}(1|l)=\frac{1}{2}+\Delta.

(3.44)

Then we can compute the Kullback–Leibler divergence as,

\displaystyle D\big(P_{\rho}(\cdot|l)\big\|P_{\rho^{\prime}}(\cdot|l)\big)=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}+\Delta}+\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}-\Delta}=\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{1}{1-4\Delta^{2}}.

(3.45)

Note that using the definition of
l

(
3.34
)
it follows that

\displaystyle T=\sum_{i=1}^{k}E_{\rho,\pi}[T_{i}(T)]\geq\sum_{i=2}^{k}E_{\rho,\pi}[T_{i}(T)]\newline
\geq(k-1)E_{\rho,\pi}[T_{l}(T)].

(3.46)

Thus, it holds that,

\displaystyle E_{\rho,\pi}[T_{l}(T)]\leq\frac{T}{k-1}.

(3.47)

Combining (
3.41
),(
3.43
),(
3.45
) and (
3.47
) we have,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)]\geq\frac{T\Delta}{4}\exp\left(-\frac{T}{2(k-1)}\log\frac{1}{1-4\Delta^{2}}\right).

(3.48)

Finally choosing
\Delta=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-e^{-\frac{k-1}{T}}}
and using that
e^{-x}\leq(e^{-1}-1)x+1
for
0\leq x\leq 1
we arrive to the result,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho^{\prime},\pi)]\geq\frac{e^{-1/2}\sqrt{1-e^{-1}}}{8}\sqrt{(k-1)n}.

(3.49)

The condition
T\geq 2(k-1)
suffices to have
0\leq\Delta\leq\frac{1}{3}
. The theorem follows using
\frac{e^{-1/2}\sqrt{1-e^{-1}}}{8}>\frac{3}{50}
.

3.2.2
Pure state environment

In the previous setting, we considered mixed-state environments to construct worst-case instances that led to the
\sqrt{T}
lower bound on regret. A natural question that arises is whether a similar behavior persists in the case of pure-state environments. That is, does the fundamental exploration-exploitation tradeoff still impose the same scaling on regret, or do pure states with discrete action sets allow for a more efficient learning strategy? To investigate this, we focus on a simplified yet representative scenario: 1-qubit pure-state environments, where the action set consists of the Pauli observables.

Theorem 3.2.5
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
. For any policy
\pi
and action set
\mathcal{A}
containing the Pauli observables for 1-qubit, there exists an environment
|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{*}
such that

\EX_{\psi,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi,\pi)]\geq\frac{3}{200}\sqrt{T}.

Proof 3.2.6
.

Let
\Delta\in[0,1]
and define the following the following two pure-states environments,

\displaystyle|\psi_{\pm}\rangle:=c_{\pm}\left(1+\frac{1\pm\Delta}{\sqrt{2}}|0\rangle+\frac{1+\Delta}{\sqrt{2}}|1\rangle\right),

(3.50)

where
c_{\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left(1+\frac{1\pm\Delta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{1\pm\Delta}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^{2}}}
.
Let
|0\rangle,|+\rangle,|\psi_{y}\rangle
be the eigenvector of the Pauli observables
\sigma_{z},\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}
respectively and define
x_{\pm}=\frac{1\pm\Delta}{\sqrt{2}}
. Then, compute the following quantities

\displaystyle p^{x}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=|\langle+|\psi_{\pm}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{(1+2x_{\pm})^{2}}{2\left((1+x_{\pm})^{2}+x_{\pm}^{2}\right)},\quad p^{z}_{\pm}=|\langle 0|\psi_{\pm}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{(1+x_{\pm})^{2}}{(1+x_{\pm})^{2}+x_{\pm}^{2}},

(3.51)

\displaystyle p^{y}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=|\langle\psi_{y}|\psi_{\pm}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}.

Note that the expectation values for each action on the environments can be computed as,

\displaystyle\Tr\left(\sigma_{i}|\psi_{\pm}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{\pm}|\right)=2p^{i}_{\pm}-1,

(3.52)

for
i=x,y,z
. Note that
\Tr\left(\sigma_{y}|\psi_{\pm}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{\pm}|\right)=0
,

\Tr\left(\sigma_{x}|\psi_{+}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{+}|\right)\geq\Tr\left(\sigma_{z}|\psi_{+}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{+}|\right)\geq
0 and

\Tr\left(\sigma_{z}|\psi_{-}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{-}|\right)\geq\Tr\left(\sigma_{x}|\psi_{-}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{-}|\right)\geq 0
for
\Delta\in[0,1]
. Thus, for the environment
|\psi_{+}\rangle
the optimal action is given by
\sigma_{x}
and for
|\psi_{-}\rangle
by
\sigma_{z}
. Let
\Delta^{\pm}_{i}
denote the sub-optimality gap for the
i
-th action in the environment
\psi_{\pm}
respectively. Using (
3.51
) and (
3.52
) we can compute the sub-optimality gaps as,

\displaystyle\Delta^{+}_{x}=0,\quad\Delta^{+}_{z}=\frac{2+\Delta}{(1+\Delta)^{2}+\sqrt{2}(1+\Delta)+1}\Delta,\quad\Delta^{+}_{y}=\frac{(\Delta+1)(1+\sqrt{2}+\Delta)}{\Delta^{2}+(2+\sqrt{2})\Delta+2+\sqrt{2}}

(3.53)

\displaystyle\Delta^{-}_{x}=\frac{2-\Delta}{(1-\Delta)^{2}+\sqrt{2}(1-\Delta)+1}\Delta,\quad\Delta^{-}_{z}=0,\quad\Delta^{-}_{y}=\frac{-\sqrt{2}\Delta+1+\sqrt{2}}{\Delta^{2}-(\sqrt{2}+2)\Delta+\sqrt{2}+2}.

(3.54)

\begin{overpic}[figures/gapspure.pdf]
\put(98.0,38.0){$\Delta^{+}_{z}$}
\put(98.0,33.0){$\Delta^{-}_{x}$}
\put(98.0,27.0){$\Delta^{+}_{y}$}
\put(98.0,21.0){$\Delta^{-}_{y}$}
\put(98.0,15.0){$\frac{3\Delta}{5+2\sqrt{2}}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 3.1
:
Sub-optimality gaps for the environments
\psi_{+},\psi_{-}
and the lower bound
\frac{3}{5+2\sqrt{(}2)}\Delta
in the range
\Delta\in[0,1]
.

Analysing the behaviour of
\Delta^{+}_{z},\Delta^{+}_{y},\Delta^{-}_{x},\Delta^{-}_{y}
for
\Delta\in[0,1]
we see that all these quantities are lower bounded by
\frac{3}{5+2\sqrt{(}2)}\Delta
. In order to simplify the expression we will use that
\frac{3}{5+2\sqrt{(}2)}\Delta>\frac{3}{10}\Delta
and take
\frac{3}{10}\Delta
as the lower bound.

We start analysing the regret for
\psi_{+}
. Using the Equations (
3.53
) for the sub-optimality gaps and the bound
\Delta^{+}_{z},\Delta^{+}_{y}\geq\frac{3}{10}\Delta
we have

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{+},\pi)]=\sum_{i=x,y,z}\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[T_{i}(T)]\Delta^{+}_{i}\geq\frac{3}{10}\Delta\sum_{i=y,z}\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}(T_{i}(T)).

(3.55)

Now using
T=\sum_{i=x,y,z}\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[T_{i}(T)]
and applying Markov’s inequality we have

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[R_{n}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{+},\pi)]\geq\frac{3}{10}\Delta\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[n-T_{x}(n)]\geq\frac{3T\Delta}{20}P_{\psi_{+},\pi}\left(T_{x}(T)\leq\frac{T}{2}\right).

(3.56)

In order to bound the regret for the environment
\psi_{-}
, we bound just the first term, use Markov inequality, and the bound
\Delta^{-}_{x}
in order to obtain

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi_{-},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{-},\pi)]=\sum_{i=x,y,z}\EX_{\psi_{-},\pi}[T_{i}(T)]\Delta^{-}_{i}\geq\Delta^{-}_{x}\EX_{\psi_{-},\pi}[T_{i}(T)]\geq\frac{3T\Delta}{20}P_{\psi_{-},\pi}\left(T_{x}(T)>\frac{T}{2}\right).

(3.57)

Combining (
3.56
) and (
3.57
)

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{+},\pi)]

\displaystyle+\EX_{\psi_{-},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{-},\pi)]

(3.58)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3T\Delta}{20}\left(P_{\psi_{+},\pi}\left(T_{x}(T)\leq\frac{T}{2}\right)+P_{\psi_{-},\pi}\left(T_{x}(T)>\frac{T}{2}\right)\right).

(3.59)

Applying Lemma
3.1.2
together with Lemma
3.1.5
we obtain

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{+},\pi)]+\EX_{\psi_{-},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{-},\pi)]\geq\frac{3n\Delta}{40}\exp\left(-TD_{\frac{1}{2}}(\psi_{+}\|\psi_{-})\right),

(3.60)

where
D_{\frac{1}{2}}(\psi_{+}\|\psi_{-})=-\log|\langle\psi_{+}|\psi_{-}\rangle|^{2}
. The overlap between the two environments can be computed as

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{+}|\psi_{-}\rangle=2+\sqrt{2}-\Delta^{2},

(3.61)

and we use it to give the following upper bound

\displaystyle-\log|\langle\psi_{+}|\psi_{-}\rangle|=\log\frac{1}{2+\sqrt{2}-\Delta^{2}}\leq\frac{\Delta^{2}-1-\sqrt{2}}{2+\sqrt{2}-\Delta^{2}}\leq\frac{\Delta^{2}}{2+\sqrt{2}-\Delta^{2}}\leq\frac{\Delta^{2}}{1+\sqrt{2}},

(3.62)

where the last inequality follows from
\frac{1}{2+\sqrt{2}-\Delta^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{2}}
for
\Delta\in[0,1]
. Thus, plugging (
3.62
) into (
3.60
) we have

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{+},\pi)]+\EX_{\psi_{-},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{-},\pi)]\geq\frac{3T\Delta}{40}\exp\left(-\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{2}}T\Delta^{2}\right).

(3.63)

Finally, if we choose
\Delta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}
,

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi_{+},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{+},\pi)]+\EX_{\psi_{-},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi_{-},\pi)]\geq\frac{3}{40}\exp\left(-\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{2}}\right)\sqrt{T},

(3.64)

and the result follows using
\frac{3}{40}\exp\left(-\frac{2}{1+\sqrt{2}}\right)\geq\frac{3}{100}
.

3.2.3
Problem dependent lower bound: one qubit environment and rank-1 projectors

The final lower bound we study for discrete action sets has a different flavor from the previous ones. Until now, we have focused on worst-case bounds to establish minimax regret guarantees. In contrast, this last bound aims to provide an explicit expression for the constant in Theorem
3.2.1
, which depends on the action set. This results in a
problem-dependent bound
that quantifies the difficulty of the problem as a function of the elements in the action set. To make the analysis more concrete, we restrict our study to environments consisting of
one-qubit quantum states
, with the action set given by a collection of rank-1 projectors

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\left\{\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{k}\right\},

(3.65)

where each
\Pi_{i}
is a rank-1 matrix satisfying
\Pi_{i}^{2}=\Pi_{i}
, or
\Pi\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{*}
.

In order to prove the main result we will need the computation of the relative entropy between two quantum states given by the following Lemma.

Lemma 3.2.7
.

Let
\rho=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{2}+\frac{\Delta}{2}\sigma_{x}
and
\rho^{\prime}=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{2}+\frac{\Delta}{2}\sigma_{z}
be two one-qubit density matrices. Then, their quantum relative entropy can be computed as,

\displaystyle D(\rho\|\rho^{\prime})=\frac{\Delta}{2}\log\left(\frac{1+\Delta}{1-\Delta}\right).

(3.66)

Proof 3.2.8
.

Let
\Pi^{+}_{i},\Pi^{-}_{i}
be the projectors for the
i=x,z
Pauli matrix into the subspaces of eigenvalue
+1
and
-1
respectively. Then we can express the density matrices as,

\displaystyle\rho=\frac{1+\Delta}{2}\Pi^{+}_{x}+\frac{1-\Delta}{2}\Pi^{-}_{x},\quad\rho^{\prime}=\frac{1+\Delta}{2}\Pi^{+}_{z}+\frac{1-\Delta}{2}\Pi^{-}_{z},

(3.67)

where we have used that
\sigma_{i}=\Pi^{+}_{i}-\Pi^{-}_{i}
. Then using that
\mathbb{I}=\Pi^{+}_{i}+\Pi^{-}_{i}
,
\Tr(\sigma_{i})=0
and
\Tr(\sigma_{x}\sigma_{z})=0
we can compute the following identity

\displaystyle\Tr(\Pi^{+}_{i}\Pi^{-}_{j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{if }i\neq j\\
0\quad\text{if }i=j.\end{array}\right.

Then, using (
3.67
) and (
3.2.8
),

\displaystyle\Tr\rho\log\rho=\frac{1+\Delta}{2}\log\left(\frac{1+\Delta}{2}\right)+\frac{1-\Delta}{2}\log\left(\frac{1-\Delta}{2}\right),

(3.70)

\displaystyle\Tr\rho\log\rho^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1+\Delta}{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(\frac{1-\Delta}{2}\right).

(3.71)

The result follows from the definition of the relative entropy and rearranging the above terms.

Theorem 3.2.9
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
. For any policy
\pi
and finite action set of observables
\mathcal{A}
of rank-1 projectors, there exists a one-qubit environment
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{2}
such that,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]\geq\frac{\sqrt{1-c}-(1-c)}{30}\sqrt{T},

(3.72)

where
c=\Tr(\Pi_{a}\Pi_{b})
and
(\Pi_{a},\Pi_{b})=\argmin_{\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}\max\big\{\Tr(\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}),\Tr\big(\Pi_{i}(\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{j})\big)\big\}
.

Note that that
\sqrt{1-c}\geq 1-c
since
c\in[0,1]
, and the constant is thus positive and only vanishes for
c\in\{0,1\}
. The value
c=1
corresponds to the case where the action set has all elements equal, thus the regret always vanishes. The case
c=1
corresponds to the case where the action set contains the projector
\Pi
and the orthogonal
\mathbb{I}-\Pi
, which correspond to the same POVM but with the reverse outcomes, thus they are the same measurement. The intermediate cases
c\in(0,1)
recover the
\sqrt{T}
behaviour but the constant on the regret depends on the overlap of all projectors contained in the action set.

Proof 3.2.10
.

Let
k=|\mathcal{A}|<\infty
denote the number of elements in
\mathcal{A}
. We study the case
k\geq 2
since the case
k=1
is trivial. We will use the Bloch sphere representation of the actions, i.e, for each
\Pi_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
we decompose it as

\displaystyle\Pi_{i}=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=x,y,z}A_{i,j}\sigma_{j},\quad i\in[k],

(3.73)

where
A_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a normalized unit vector (
\|A_{i}\|_{2}=1
) since
\Pi_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{*}
and
\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}
and
\sigma_{z}
are the qubit Pauli matrices. Let
\mathcal{A}_{r}=\left\{A_{1},...,A_{k}\right\}
be the set of Bloch vectors associated to the
k
elements of
\mathcal{A}
. Let
\Delta\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
be a constant to be chosen later. Pick
A_{a},A_{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{r}
such that they are the two directions with the smallest inner product, that is

\displaystyle(A_{a},A_{b})

\displaystyle=\argmin_{A_{i},A_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{r}}|A_{i}\cdot A_{j}|=\argmin_{\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}\left|\Tr(\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j})-\Tr\big(\Pi_{i}(I-\Pi_{j})\big)\right|

(3.74)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}\max\big\{\Tr(\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j})-\Tr\big(\Pi_{i}(I-\Pi_{j})\big),\Tr\big(\Pi_{i}(I-\Pi_{j})\big)-\Tr(\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j})\big\}

(3.75)

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\Pi_{i},\Pi_{j}\in\mathcal{A}}\max\big\{\Tr(\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j}),\Tr\big(\Pi_{i}(I-\Pi_{j})\big)\big\}\,,

(3.76)

where in the last step we used that
\Tr(\Pi_{i}\Pi_{j})+\Tr\big(\Pi_{i}(\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{j})\big)=1
.
Since
\mathcal{A}
contains at least two independent directions,
\mathbb{r}_{a},\mathbb{r}_{b}
determine a plane. Now rotate
A_{a},A_{b}
symmetrically in this plane until we find two orthogonal directions
A^{\prime}_{a}
and
A^{\prime}_{b}
that obey the following conditions:

•

|A^{\prime}_{a}|=|A^{\prime}_{b}|=1
and
\langle A^{\prime}_{a},A^{\prime}_{b}\rangle=0
(unit orthogonal vectors).

•

\langle A_{a},A^{\prime}_{a}\rangle=\langle A_{b},A^{\prime}_{b}\rangle
(symmetrical rotation).

•

A_{a}\times A_{b}=A^{\prime}_{a}\times A^{\prime}_{b}
(they remain in the same plane).

•

\max_{A_{i}\in\mathcal{A}}\langle A^{\prime}_{a},A_{i}\rangle=\langle A^{\prime}_{a},A_{a}\rangle
,
\max_{A_{i}\in\mathcal{A}}\langle A^{\prime}_{b},A_{i}\rangle=\langle A^{\prime}_{b},A_{b}\rangle
(closest directions that obey the above conditions).

\begin{overpic}[figures/vectors.pdf]
\put(7.0,58.0){\color[rgb]{0,0,1} $A_{a}$}
\put(89.0,58.0){\color[rgb]{0,0,1} $A_{b}$}
\put(1.0,51.0){\color[rgb]{1,0,0} $A^{\prime}_{a}$}
\put(96.0,51.0){\color[rgb]{1,0,0} $A^{\prime}_{b}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 3.2
:
Scheme for the choice of the vectors
A^{\prime}_{a}
and
A^{\prime}_{b}
.

Using the directions
A^{\prime}_{a}
and
A^{\prime}_{b}
define the following environments
\rho_{a},\rho_{b}

\displaystyle\rho_{a}:=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{2}+\frac{\Delta}{2}A^{\prime}_{a}\cdot\sigma\quad\rho_{b}:=\frac{I}{2}+\frac{\Delta}{2}A^{\prime}_{b}\cdot\sigma.

(3.77)

Note that
\rho\geq 0
and
\rho_{b}\geq 0
. In order to calculate the sub-optimality gaps (
2.21
) for
\rho
and
\rho_{b}
we will use an orthonormal system of coordinates defined by the directions
\mathcal{C}_{a,b}=\left\{A^{\prime}_{a},A^{\prime}_{b},A^{\prime}_{a}\times A^{\prime}_{b}\right\}
. Using the above conditions for
A^{\prime}_{a}
and
A^{\prime}_{b}
we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{a}_{i}=\Tr(\rho\Pi_{i})=\Delta(p-A_{ia}),\quad\Delta^{b}_{i}=\Tr(\rho_{b}\Pi_{b})=\Delta(p-A_{ib})\quad\text{for }i=1,...,k,

(3.78)

where
p=\langle A_{a},A^{\prime}_{a}\rangle
and
r_{ia},r_{ib}
are the first and second coordinates of
A_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
in the coordinate system
\mathcal{C}_{a,b}
respectively. This last fact is because we defined
\mathcal{C}_{a,b}
to be a orthonormal set of coordinates. Note that by the construction of
A^{\prime}_{a}
and
A^{\prime}_{b}
we have

\displaystyle p=\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{4}-\frac{\theta}{2}\right),

(3.79)

where
\theta
is defined via
\cos\theta=\langle A_{a},A_{b}\rangle
. Let
\Pi_{a},\Pi_{b}
be the projectors associated to
\mathbb{r}_{a},\mathbb{r}_{b}
, then using the trigonometric identity for
\cos\left(\frac{a}{2}\right)
we have
\Tr(\Pi_{a}\Pi_{b})=\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)
. Thus, using the trigonometric identity for
\cos(a+b)
we have

\displaystyle p=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\sqrt{\Tr(\Pi_{a}\Pi_{b}})+\sqrt{1-\Tr(\Pi_{a}\Pi_{b}})\right).

(3.80)

Now define the following subsets of indices of
[k]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{a}=\left\{i\colon A^{2}_{ia}\geq\frac{1}{2}\text{ for }A_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{r}\right\}\quad\mathcal{N}_{a}^{C}=\left\{i\colon A^{2}_{ia}<\frac{1}{2}\text{ for }A_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{r}\right\}.

(3.81)

Note that this sets are complementary,
\mathcal{N}_{a}\cap\mathcal{N}_{a}^{C}=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{N}_{a}\cup\mathcal{N}_{a}^{C}=[k]
. Using the expression for the regret (
2.22
) and the sub-optimality gap (
3.78
), the regret for
\rho
can be bounded as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{a},\pi)]

\displaystyle=\Delta\sum_{i=1}^{k}(p-A_{ia})\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[T_{i}(T)]

(3.82)

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}^{C}}(p-A_{ia})\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[T_{i}(T)]

(3.83)

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}^{C}}\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[T_{i}(T)],

(3.84)

where we have used that for
i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}^{C}
, we have
A_{ia}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
. Using that
T=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\EX_{\pi,\rho_{a}}(T_{i}(T)),

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{a},\pi)]\geq\Delta\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}\left[T-\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}}T_{i}(T)\right].

(3.85)

Applying Markov’s inequality to the above expression we have,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{a},\pi)]\geq\frac{T\Delta}{2}\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)P_{\rho_{a},\pi}\left(T-\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}}T_{i}(T)\geq\frac{T}{2}\right).

(3.86)

Rearranging all the terms,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{a},\pi)]\geq\frac{T\Delta}{2}\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)P_{\rho_{a},\pi}\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}}T_{i}(T)\leq\frac{T}{2}\right).

(3.87)

In order to bound the regret for
\rho_{b}
note that if
i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}
then
A^{2}_{ib}\leq\frac{1}{2}
since
\|A_{i}\|=1
. Using the same tricks as before,

\displaystyle\EX[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{b},\pi)]

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}}(p-A_{ib})\EX_{\pi,\rho_{b}}[T_{i}(T)]

(3.88)

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}}\EX_{\pi,\rho_{b}}[T_{i}(T)].

(3.89)

Using again Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{b},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{b},\pi)]\geq\frac{T\Delta}{2}\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)P_{\rho_{b},\pi}\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}}T_{i}(T)>\frac{T}{2}\right).

(3.90)

Thus, combining (
3.87
) and (
3.90
),

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{a},\pi)]+\EX_{\rho_{b},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{b},\pi)]\geq

\displaystyle\frac{T\Delta}{2}\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\left(P_{\rho_{a},\pi}\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}}T_{i}(T)\leq\frac{T}{2}\right)+P_{\rho_{b},\pi}\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{a}}T_{i}(T)>\frac{T}{2}\right)\right).

(3.91)

Using Lemma
3.1.1
, we can bound the above expression as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{a},\pi)]+\EX_{\rho_{b},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{b},\pi)]\geq\frac{T\Delta}{4}\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\exp\left(-D(P_{\pi,\rho_{a}}\|P_{\pi,\rho_{b}})\right).

(3.92)

Using Lemma
3.1.4
combined with the data-processing inequality, we can bound the Kullback–Leibler divergence as,

\displaystyle D(P_{\pi,\rho_{a}}\|P_{\pi,\rho_{b}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\EX_{\pi,\rho_{a}}[T_{i}(T)]D\big(P_{\rho_{a}}(\cdot|i)\big\|P_{\rho_{b}}(\cdot|i)\big))

(3.93)

\displaystyle\leq D(\rho_{a}\|\rho_{b})\sum_{i=1}^{k}\EX_{\rho,\pi}[T_{i}(T)]=TD(\rho_{a}\|\rho_{b}).

(3.94)

Using that the relative entropy
D(\rho_{a}\|\rho_{b})
is unitarily invariant and
{A}^{\prime}_{a},{A}^{\prime}_{b}
are orthogonal, we can use the computation of the relative entropy given by Lemma
3.2.7
. Thus we can lower bound (
3.92
) as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{a},\pi)]+\EX_{\rho_{b},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{b},\pi)]\geq\frac{T\Delta}{4}\left(p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{T\Delta}{2}\log\left(\frac{1+\Delta}{1-\Delta}\right)\right).

(3.95)

Choosing
\Delta=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{T}}
we have the following upper bound,

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{1+\Delta}{1-\Delta}\right)\leq 2\log(3)\Delta\quad\text{for }\Delta\in[0,1/2],

(3.96)

where we have used that
\log\left(\frac{1+x}{1-x}\right)
is convex and finite for
x\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
. Thus, choosing
\Delta=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{T}}
we have

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho_{a},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{a},\pi)]+\EX_{\rho_{b},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho_{b},\pi)]\geq\frac{p-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}{8\cdot 3^{1/4}}\sqrt{T},

(3.97)

and the result follows using the expression for
p
(
3.80
) and
15>8\cdot 3^{1/4}\sqrt{2}
. From (
3.80
) is easy to check that
p\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
, thus the above lower bound is positive.

3.3
Continuous lower bounds

We now turn to the problem of establishing minimax regret lower bounds for the general bandit setting introduced in Section
2.2.2
.

3.3.1
Rank-1 projection measurements for mixed state environments

In the following theorem, we consider a bandit with a mixed state environment whose action set is the set of all rank-1 projections, i.e.,
\mc A=\mc S_{d}^{*}
where
d\in\N
is the dimension of the Hilbert space. When we play the action
|\fii\>\!\<\fii|\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*}
on the environment state
\rho
, the probability of reward 1 is
\<\fii|\rho|\fii\>
and the probability of reward 0 is
1-\<\fii|\rho|\fii\>
. The sub-optimality gap is
\Delta_{\fii}:=\lambda_{\rm max}(\rho)-\<\fii|\rho|\fii\>
for the environment state
\rho
upon playing the action
|\fii\>\!\langle\fii|
.

Theorem 3.3.1
.

Let
T,\,d\in\N
. For any policy
\pi
and action set of observables
\mathcal{A}
containing all rank-1 projections, i.e.,
\mc A=\mc S_{d}^{*}
, there exists an environment
\rho\in\mc S_{d}
such that

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho,\pi)]\geq C\sqrt{T}

(3.98)

for some constant
C>0
.

Proof 3.3.2
.

Let us fix a policy
\pi
and an orthonormal basis
\{|n\>\}_{n=0}^{d-1}
for
\C^{d}
and define
|\psi\>:=d^{-1/2}(|0\>+\cdots+|d-1\>)
and the sets

\displaystyle\mc N_{1}

\displaystyle:=\left\{|\eta\>\!\<\eta|\in\mc S_{d}^{*}\,\middle|\,|\<0|\eta\>|^{2}<\frac{3}{4}+\frac{1}{4d}\right\},

(3.99)

\displaystyle\mc N_{2}

\displaystyle:=\left\{|\eta\>\!\<\eta|\in\mc S_{d}^{*}\,\middle|\,|\<\psi|\eta\>|^{2}<\frac{3}{4}+\frac{1}{4d}\right\}.

(3.100)

Let us show that these sets are disjoint. First note that
P(|0\>\!\<0|,|\psi\>\!\<\psi|)=\sqrt{1-1/d}
where
P=\sqrt{1-F^{2}}
is the purified distance where, in turn,
F
is the fidelity. Assume that
|\eta\>\!\<\eta|\in\mc N_{1}
, so that, using the triangle inequality for the purified distance,

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{d}}

\displaystyle=P(|0\>\!\<0|,|\psi\>\!\<\psi|)\leq P(|0\>\!\<0|,|\eta\>\!\<\eta|)+P(|\eta\>\!\<\eta|,|\psi\>\!\<\psi|)

(3.101)

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{d}}+P(|\eta\>\!\<\eta|,|\psi\>\!\<\psi|)

(3.102)

where we have used the definition of
\mc N_{1}
in the final inequality. Thus,
P(|\eta\>\!\<\eta|,|\psi\>\!\<\psi|)>(1/2)\sqrt{1-1/d}
which is easily seen to imply
|\eta\>\!\<\eta|\in\mc N_{2}^{c}
. Thus,
\mc N_{1}\cap\mc N_{2}=\emptyset
.

Let us now define

\displaystyle\rho:=\frac{1-\Delta}{d}\mathbb{I}+\Delta|0\>\!\<0|\,,

(3.103)

where
\Delta\in[0,1]
is a constant to be determined later. For this environment, the sub-optimality gap is

\displaystyle\Delta_{\eta}=\Delta(1-|\<0|\eta\>|^{2}).

(3.104)

We may now evaluate the decomposition of the regret for general bandits (
2.27
)

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho,\pi)]

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\int_{\mc N_{1}^{c}}(1-|\<0|\eta\>|^{2})\,d\gamma_{\rho,\pi}(|\eta\>\!\<\eta|)\geq\Delta\frac{d-1}{4d}\gamma_{\rho,\pi}(\mc N_{1}^{c}).

(3.105)

Let us define another state

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}:=\frac{1-\Delta}{d}\mathbb{I}+\Delta|\psi\>\!\<\psi|\,,

(3.106)

where
|\psi\>
is the unit vector defined earlier. Similarly as above, we find

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho^{\prime},\pi)]

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\int_{\mc N_{2}^{c}}(1-|\<\psi|\eta\>|^{2})\,d\gamma_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}(|\eta\>\!\<\eta|)\geq\Delta\frac{d-1}{4d}\gamma_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}(\mc N_{2}^{c})

(3.107)

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\frac{d-1}{4d}\gamma_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}(\mc N_{1})

(3.108)

where the final inequality follows from
\mc N_{1}\subseteq\mc N_{2}^{c}
; recall that
\mc N_{1}
and
\mc N_{2}
are disjoint. Recalling that
(1/T)\gamma_{\rho,\pi}
and
(1/T)\gamma_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}
are probability measures and using Lemma
3.1.1
, we now obtain

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho,\pi)]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho^{\prime},\pi)]

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\frac{d-1}{4d}T\left(\frac{1}{T}\gamma_{\rho,\pi}(\mc N_{1}^{c})+\frac{1}{T}\gamma_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}(\mc N_{1})\right)

(3.109)

\displaystyle\geq\Delta\frac{d-1}{4d}T\exp{\left(-D\left(\frac{1}{T}\gamma_{\rho,\pi}\middle\|\frac{1}{T}\gamma_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\right)\right)}.

(3.110)

Recall that, e.g.,
\gamma_{\rho,\pi}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(P_{\rho,\pi}^{(t)}(\cdot,0)+P_{\rho,\pi}^{(t)}(\cdot,1)\big)
. Defining, for all
t=1,\ldots,\,T
, the (measurable) function
f_{t}:(\mc A\times\{0,1\})^{T}\to\mc A
through

\displaystyle f_{t}\big((|\eta_{s}\>\!\<\eta_{s}|,x_{s})_{s=1}^{T}\big)=|\eta_{t}\>\!\<\eta_{t}|,

(3.111)

we now see that
\gamma_{\rho,\pi}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}P_{\rho,\pi}\circ f_{t}^{-1}
where we view
P_{\rho,\pi}
as a measure. Using the joint convexity of the Kullback-Leibler divergence and the data processing inequality, we finally get

\displaystyle D\left(\frac{1}{T}\gamma_{\rho,\pi}\middle\|\frac{1}{n}\gamma_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}D(P_{\rho,\pi}\circ f_{t}^{-1}\|P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}\circ f_{t}^{-1})

(3.112)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}D(P_{\rho,\pi}\|P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi})=D(P_{\rho,\pi}\|P_{\rho^{\prime},\pi})

(3.113)

\displaystyle\leq TD(\rho\|\rho^{\prime})

(3.114)

where the final inequality follows from Lemma
3.1.5
(for
\alpha=1
). Combining this with (
3.109
) gives us

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho,\pi)]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho^{\prime},\pi)]\geq\Delta\frac{d-1}{4d}T\exp{\big(-TD(\rho\|\rho^{\prime})\big)}.

(3.115)

Whenever
\Delta\leq 1/2
, we may follow the end of the proof of Theorem
3.2.1
and obtain
c>0
such that
D(\rho\|\rho^{\prime})\leq(c/2)\Delta^{2}
. Fixing
\Delta=1/\sqrt{T}
, we now have

\displaystyle\max\{\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho,\pi)],\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho^{\prime},\pi)]\}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\big(\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho,\pi)]+\EX_{\rho^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mc A,\rho^{\prime},\pi)]\big)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d-1}{8d}\sqrt{T}e^{-c/2}.

(3.116)

3.3.2
PSMAQB lower bound

In the discrete case, we observed that the
\sqrt{T}
scaling of regret remains unchanged regardless of whether the environment is mixed or pure. The proof techniques were similar, as they relied on the Bretagnolle-Huber inequality (or its modified version in Lemma
3.1.2
). Given this, one might expect that the same behavior holds for general bandits and that the proof would closely follow that of Theorem
3.3.1
.

As in the previous section, we consider an action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*}
and an environment represented by a pure state
\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*}
. This is the PSMAQB setting that we defined in
2.2.4
. However, applying the previous techniques in this setting results in only a constant lower bound. To illustrate this, consider the following example.

We first fix the reference state
\ket{0}
in some basis and define two possible environments

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}:=\ket{0},\quad\ket{\psi^{\prime}}:=\cos(\Delta)\ket{0}+\sin(\Delta)\ket{1},

(3.117)

where
\Delta\in[0,2\pi)
is a constant. We then select a subset of the action set,
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*}
, consisting of states that are closer to
\ket{\psi}
than to
\ket{\psi^{\prime}}
, formally defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}:=\left\{\ket{v}\!\bra{v}:F(v,\psi)\geq F(v,\psi^{\prime})\right\}.

(3.118)

Following similar steps as in the proof of Theorem
3.3.1
and using Lemma
3.1.2
(with
\alpha=1/2
), we obtain the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\psi,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi,\pi)]+\mathbb{E}_{\psi^{\prime},\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi^{\prime},\pi)]\geq\frac{T\sin^{2}(\Delta)}{8}\exp\left(-T\frac{\sin^{2}(\Delta)}{1-\sin^{2}(\Delta)}\right).

(3.119)

Note that the bound
\alpha=1/2
is tighter than
\alpha=1
. By choosing
\sin^{2}(\Delta)\sim\frac{1}{T}
, this results in a constant lower bound. The reason for this lower bound stems from the fact that the sub-optimality gap for the environment
\ket{\psi}
, given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\eta}=1-|\langle\psi|\eta\rangle|^{2},

(3.120)

for elements in the complement set
\mathcal{N}^{C}
, behaves as
\Delta_{\eta}\sim\sin^{2}(\Delta)
. This coincides with the divergence

\displaystyle D_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\psi\|\psi^{\prime}\right)\sim\sin^{2}(\Delta).

(3.121)

In all our previous results, the relationship between these two quantities was quadratic, which allowed us, by choosing appropriate parameters, to achieve the usual
\sqrt{T}
regret scaling.

The natural question that follows is whether a more refined calculation can recover the standard
\sqrt{T}
lower bound, or if there is something fundamental in this setting that enables breaking the square-root barrier.

In order to establish a non-trivial lower bound in this setting, we use the key insight that a policy for PSMAQB can be understood as a sequence of state tomography procedures. This follows from the fact that the regret can be expressed as

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi,\pi)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{t}|\psi\rangle\right),

(3.122)

where
\Pi_{t}=\ket{\psi_{t}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}}\in\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*}
is the action selected at time step
t\in[T]
.

Minimizing the regret means choosing
\ket{\psi_{t}}
so that it is close to
\ket{\psi}
in terms of infidelity distance. Since the expected fidelity of these tomography procedures is directly related to the regret, existing upper bounds on tomography fidelity also provide a lower bound for the expected regret of the policy. Our lower bound proof relies on the fact that individual actions of a strategy at time
t\in[T]
can be viewed as quantum state tomography algorithms using
t
copies of the state. A relation between the optimal fidelity achievable by these algorithms and the regret of the strategy allows us to convert the fidelity upper bound from
[
51
]
to a regret lower bound. We use measure-theoretic tools to adapt the proof from
[
51
]
to a more general case where the tomography can output arbitrary distribution of states. In the following we provide the necessary tools to connect a quantum state tomography protocol with our model and a regret lower bound.

Average fidelity bound for pure state tomography

In its most general form, a tomography procedure takes
n
copies of an unknown state
\Pi=|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*}
and performs a joint measurement on the state
\Pi^{\otimes n}
. This is captured in the following definition. Let
(\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*},\Sigma)
be a
\sigma
-algebra. A
tomography scheme
is a POVM
\mathcal{T}:\Sigma\to\End(\hil^{\otimes n})
such that
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*})=\Pi^{+}_{n}
, where
\Pi^{+}_{n}
is the symmetrization operator on
\hil^{\otimes n}
. For any
\rho\in\End(\hil^{\otimes n})
, this POVM gives rise to a complex-valued measure

P_{\mathcal{T},\rho}(A)=\Tr(\mathcal{T}(A)\rho)

(3.123)

for
A\in\Sigma
.
P_{\mathcal{T},\rho}
becomes a probability measure if
\rho
satisfies
\rho\geq 0,\ \Pi^{+}_{n}\rho=\rho\Pi^{+}_{n}=\rho
, and
\Tr\rho=1
. Given
n
copies of
\Pi
, the tomography scheme produces the distribution
P_{\mathcal{T},\Pi^{\otimes n}}
of the predicted states. Note that
\Pi^{\otimes n}
satisfies the properties above, so
P_{\mathcal{T},\Pi^{\otimes n}}
is indeed a probability distribution. The fidelity of this distribution is given by

F(\mathcal{T},\Pi)=\int\Tr(\Pi\sigma)dP_{\mathcal{T},\Pi^{\otimes n}}(\sigma).

(3.124)

Finally, the average fidelity of the tomography scheme is defined as

F(\mathcal{T})=\int F(\mathcal{T},|\psi\>\<\psi|)d\psi,

(3.125)

where the integration is taken with respect to the normalized uniform measure over all pure states. In the following,
\int d\psi
will always imply this measure. We will provide a lower bound on
F(\mathcal{T})
in terms of
d
and
n
, following the proof technique from
[
51
]
. In
[
51
]
, the proof is only presented for tomography schemes producing a finite number of predictions. For our definition, we will require more general measure-theoretic tools. Before we introduce the upper bound on the fidelity, we will need two auxiliary lemmas about the nature of the measure
P_{\mathcal{T},\rho}
. The proofs of these Lemmas are left to Appendix
A
.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma)
be a
\sigma
-algebra, and let
O:\Sigma\to\End(\widetilde{\hil})
be a POVM with values acting on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\widetilde{\hil}
with
\operatorname{dim}\thil=\tdim
s.t.
O(\Omega)\leq\mathbb{I}
, where
\mathbb{I}
is the identity operator. Further, let
P_{O,\sigma}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{C}
be a complex-valued measure, defined for any
\sigma\in\End(\widetilde{\hil})
as

P_{O,\sigma}(A)=\Tr[O(A)\sigma].

(3.126)

Then, there exists a set of functions
\{f_{\sigma}\}
indexed by
\sigma\in\End\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
that are linear w.r.t.
\sigma
for all
\omega
and that satisfy

f_{\sigma}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\forall A\in\Sigma\ \ P_{O,\sigma}(A)=\int_{A}f_{\sigma}(\omega)dP_{O,\id}(\omega).

(3.127)

We purposefully formulated this Lemma with slightly more general objects than the ones used in the definition of tomography. That is,
\Omega
does not need to be
\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*}
, and
\widetilde{\hil}
does not need to be the n-th power
\hil^{\otimes n}
, although we will focus on this case.

Note that for
\sigma\geq 0
, the measure
P_{O,\sigma}
is finite and nonnegative, but nonnegativity (and even real-valuedness) do not hold for a general
\sigma\in\End(\widetilde{\hil})
.
By our definition of
f_{\sigma}(\omega)
, it can be written as

f_{\sigma}(\omega)=\Tr\left[K(\omega)\sigma\right],\quad\text{where}\ K(\omega)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\text{dim}(\tilde{\mathcal{H}})}f_{|i\>\<j|}(\omega)|j\>\<i|.

(3.128)

As the following lemma demonstrates,
K(\omega)\geq 0
for
P_{O,\id}
-almost every
\omega
:

Lemma 3.3.4
.

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mu)
be a measurable space and
V:\Omega\to\End(\thil)
be a measurable operator-valued function with values acting on a finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\thil
such that

\forall A\in\Sigma\quad\int_{A}V(\omega)d\mu(\omega)\geq 0.

(3.129)

Then,
V(\omega)\geq 0

\mu
-almost everywhere.

Now we can apply this analysis to the POVM corresponding to our tomography scheme, and get the desired upper bound on the fidelity.

Theorem 3.3.5
.

For any tomography scheme
\mathcal{T}
utilizing
n
copies of the input state, the average fidelity is bounded by

F(\mathcal{T})\leq\frac{n+1}{n+d}.

(3.130)

Proof 3.3.6
.

We will introduce the density
K(\omega)
from (
3.128
) for our tomography scheme
\mathcal{T}
and the corresponding measure
P_{\mathcal{T},\sigma}
. Lemma
3.3.3
allows us to introduce for any
\sigma\in\End(\hil^{\otimes n})
the density
f_{\sigma}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
s.t.

\forall A\in\Sigma\ \ P_{\mathcal{T},\sigma}(A)=\int_{A}f_{\sigma}(\omega)dP_{\mathcal{T},\mathbbm{1}}(\omega).

(3.131)

This density can be written as
f_{\sigma}(\omega)=\Tr\left(K(\omega)\sigma\right)

for some
K(\omega)\in\End(\hil^{\otimes n})
.
K(\omega)
can be considered as the operator-valued density of
\mathcal{T}
w.r.t.
P_{\mathcal{T},\id}
:

\forall A\in\Sigma\quad\mathcal{T}(A)=\int_{A}K(\omega)dP_{\mathcal{T},\id}(\omega).

(3.132)

Since
\mathcal{T}(A)\geq 0
, it follows by Lemma
3.3.4
that
K(\omega)\geq 0
for
P_{\mathcal{T},\id}
-almost all
\omega
. Furthermore, as
\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*})=\Pi^{+}_{n}
, we have that for all
A\in\Sigma
,

\mathcal{T}(A)\leq\Pi^{+}_{n}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{T}(A)\Pi^{+}_{n}=\Pi^{+}_{n}\mathcal{T}(A)=\mathcal{T}(A)
. This means that
\tilde{K}(\omega)=\Pi^{+}_{n}K(\omega)\Pi^{+}_{n}
would also satisfy (
3.132
). In the following, we will without loss of generality assume that

K(\omega)=\Pi^{+}_{n}K(\omega)=K(\omega)\Pi^{+}_{n}.

(3.133)

With these tools at hand, we are ready to adapt the proof from
[
51
]
to the general case of POVM tomography schemes. We begin by rewriting the expression (
3.124
) for average fidelity:

\displaystyle F(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=\int d\psi\int dP_{\mathcal{T},(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes n}}(\sigma)\Tr(\sigma\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})

(3.134)

\displaystyle=\int d\psi\int dP_{\mathcal{T},\mathbb{I}}(\sigma)\Tr(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}\sigma)\Tr\left(K(\sigma)(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes n}\right).

(3.135)

Since fidelity is nonnegative and its average is bounded by 1, we can change the order of integration. Following
[
51
]
, we introduce notation

\sigma_{n}(k)=\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes\sigma\otimes\mathbb{I}^{\otimes(n-k)}\in\hil^{\otimes n}.

(3.136)

The product of traces in (
3.135
) can be rewritten in the following manner:

F(\mathcal{T})=\int dP_{\mathcal{T},\id}(\sigma)\int d\psi\Tr\left((K(\sigma)\otimes\mathbb{I})(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes(n+1)}\sigma_{n+1}(n+1)\right).

(3.137)

We can now take the inner integral in closed form. As shown in
[
51
, Eq. (4)]
,

\int d\psi(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes n}=\frac{\Pi^{+}_{n}}{D_{n}},

(3.138)

where
D_{n}=\binom{n+d-1}{d}
. Another useful result in this paper is
[
51
, Eq. (8)]
:

\Tr_{n+1}\left(\Pi^{+}_{n+1}\sigma_{n+1}(n+1)\right)=\frac{1}{n+1}\Pi^{+}_{n}\left(\mathbb{I}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sigma_{n}(k)\right),

(3.139)

where
\Tr_{n+1}:\End(\hil^{\otimes(n+1)})\to\End(\hil^{\otimes n})
is the partial trace on the
(n+1)
-st copy of the system. These expressions allow us to rewrite (
3.137
) as follows:

\displaystyle F(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{D_{n+1}}\int dP_{\mathcal{T},\id}(\sigma)\Tr\left((K(\sigma)\otimes\mathbb{I})\Pi^{+}_{n+1}\sigma_{n+1}(n+1)\right)

(3.140)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{D_{n+1}}\int dP_{\mathcal{T},\id}(\sigma)\Tr\left(K(\sigma)\Tr_{n+1}\left(\Pi^{+}_{n+1}\sigma_{n+1}(n+1)\right)\right)

(3.141)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n+1)D_{n+1}}\int dP_{\mathcal{T},\id}(\sigma)\Tr\left(K(\sigma)\left(\mathbb{I}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sigma_{n}(k)\right)\right).

(3.142)

Finally,
\sigma_{n}(k)\leq\mathbb{I}
, so
\Tr(K(\sigma)\sigma_{n}(k))\leq\Tr(K(\sigma))
, and we can bound the above as

F(\mathcal{T})\leq\frac{1}{D_{n+1}}\int dP_{\mathcal{T},\id}(\sigma)\Tr\left(K(\sigma)\right)=\frac{\Tr\Pi^{+}_{n}}{D_{n+1}}=\frac{D_{n}}{D_{n+1}}=\frac{n+1}{n+d}.

(3.143)

Bandit policy as a sequence of tomographies

Given the fidelity bound in Theorem
3.3.5
we are ready to apply it to our multi-armed quantum bandit problem. In the following theorem, we reduce the PSMAQB setting to a sequence of quantum state tomography protocols and apply the mentioned fidelity bound.

Theorem 3.3.7
.

Let
T,\,d\in\N
. For any policy
\pi
and action set of observables
\mathcal{A}
containing all rank-1 projections, i.e.,
\mc A=\mc S_{d}^{*}
, there exists an environment
\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}\in\mc S^{*}_{d}
such that

\displaystyle\int d\psi\EX_{\psi,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi,\pi)\right]\geq(d-1)\log\left(\frac{T}{d+1}\right).

(3.144)

Proof 3.3.8
.

Given a policy
\pi
, we can introduce a POVM
E_{t}:(\Sigma\times\{0,1\})^{\times t}\to\End(\hil^{\otimes t})
such that

P^{t}_{\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi},\pi}(A_{1},X_{1},\dotsc,A_{t},X_{t})=\Tr\left((\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes t}E_{t}(A_{1},X_{1},\dotsc,A_{t},X_{t})\right),

(3.145)

where
P^{t}_{\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi},\pi}
is the probability measure defined by (
2.2.2
), but only for actions and rewards until step
t
. The construction of this POVM is presented in the proof of Lemma
3.1.5
.
We will also define the coordinate mapping

\Psi_{t}(\Pi_{1},X_{1},\dotsc,\Pi_{t},X_{t})=\Pi_{t},

(3.146)

where
\Pi_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
are actions and
r_{i}\in\{0,1\}
are rewards of the PSMAQB. Now we can for each step
t
define a tomography scheme
\mathcal{T}_{t}=E_{t}\circ\Psi_{t}^{-1}
as the pushforward POVM from
E_{t}
to the space
(\mathcal{A},\Sigma)
. Informally, this tomography scheme takes
t
copies of the state, runs the policy
\pi
on them, and outputs the
t
-th action of the policy as the predicted state. For
A\in\Sigma
, we can rewrite the tomography’s distribution on predictions as

P_{\mathcal{T},(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes t}}(A)=\Tr\left(\mathcal{T}_{t}(A)(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes t}\right)=\Tr\left(E_{t}(\Psi_{t}^{-1}(A))(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes t}\right)=\left(P^{t}_{\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi},\pi}\circ\Psi^{-1}\right)(A).

(3.147)

Then, the fidelity of
\mathcal{T}_{t}
on the input
\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle F(\mathcal{T}_{t},\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})

\displaystyle=\int\<\psi|\rho|\psi\>dP_{\mathcal{T}_{t},(\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi})^{\otimes t}}(\rho)

(3.148)

\displaystyle=\int\<\psi|\Psi_{t}(\Pi_{1},X_{1},\dotsc,\Pi_{t},X_{t})|\psi\>dP^{t}_{\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi},\pi}(\Pi_{1},X_{1},\dotsc,\Pi_{t},X_{t})

(3.149)

\displaystyle=\EX_{\psi,\pi}\left[\<\psi|\Pi_{t}|\psi\>\right].

(3.150)

Using the bound for average tomography fidelity on
\mathcal{T}_{t}
from Theorem
3.3.5
, we can now bound the average regret of
\pi
:

\displaystyle\int\EX_{\psi,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\psi,\pi)\right]d\psi

\displaystyle=T-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\int\ \EX_{\psi,\pi}\left[\<\psi|\Pi_{t}|\psi\>\right]d\psi

(3.151)

\displaystyle=T-\sum_{t=1}^{T}F(\mathcal{T}_{t})\geq\sum_{t=1}^{T}1-\frac{t+1}{t+d}

(3.152)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{d-1}{t+d}\geq(d-1)\log\left(\frac{T}{d+1}\right),

(3.153)

where the first equality follows from the form of the regret (
3.122
) and the last inequality follows from bounding the sum by below with the integral of the function
f(t)=1/(t+d)
.

This result is averaged over all pure states, meaning that at least one state reaches the bound. Given the symmetries of the problem, we can intuitively expect that no particular subset of states is significantly harder to learn than others for any given policy.

Given this result, one may observe that since we employed a tomography-based approach without accounting for the exploration–exploitation constraint, the bound obtained is not tight. In particular, tomography guarantees at least a logarithmic contribution, as the infidelity scales as
1-F=\Omega(1/t)
with
t
copies, leading to a cumulative contribution of order
\log(T)
. However, as we will show in the next chapter this bound is indeed almost tight.

Chapter 4
Algorithms and regret upper bounds

This chapter builds on the algorithms developed by the author in
[
71
,
74
,
73
]
. We begin by reviewing some of the most well-known algorithms based on upper confidence bounds, or
UCB
-type strategies. These methods can be readily applied to the MAQB settings introduced in the previous section and already yield a regret scaling of order
\sqrt{T}
. We then present a simple Explore-Then-Commit strategy for the PSMAQB setting, grounded in a quantum state estimation algorithm. After this warm-up, we move into the core technical contribution of the chapter: the design and theoretical analysis of algorithms for linear bandits with vanishing noise. To study these settings, we introduce a novel technique that tightly controls the eigenvalues of the design matrix, leading to new proofs of regret bounds in the linear stochastic bandit setting. These techniques will allow us to establish a polylogarithmic upper bound for the PSMAQB setting.

4.1
Optimism in the face of uncertainty principle

Two of the most widely used algorithms for solving bandit problems are UCB (Upper Confidence Bounds)
[
17
]
and Thompson Sampling
[
104
,
10
]
. In this thesis, we focus on UCB-type algorithms, which are based on the principle of optimism in the face of uncertainty (OFU). This principle is a natural and effective approach to designing algorithms for many stochastic online learning problems. It suggests that, when faced with uncertainty, the learner should act as if the most favorable plausible scenario is true, encouraging exploration of potentially optimal choices. In the context of bandit problems, this translates into selecting actions that balance exploration and exploitation by considering optimistic estimates of expected rewards.

Given a set of actions
\mathcal{A}
and a set of possible environments
\Gamma
, the algorithm follows a simple yet effective strategy. At each time step
t\in[T]
, it uses past observations of rewards and actions to construct a confidence set
\mathcal{C}_{t-1}\subseteq\Gamma
that contains the unknown environment with high probability. The next action is then selected based on the optimism in the face of uncertainty (OFU) principle:

\displaystyle a_{t}=\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\max_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{t-1}}\langle\theta^{\prime},a\rangle,

(4.1)

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is a well-defined inner product between elements in the action set and the environment set
\Gamma
(for linear bandits, we consider real vector spaces). The key challenge in applying the OFU principle lies in constructing confidence sets
\mathcal{C}_{t-1}
that are small enough, so we choose actions close to the optimal one, while still containing the true environment with high probability.

The UCB-type algorithms often require certain assumptions on the reward distribution, such as finite support or bounded variance. The reason for this is that constructing confidence regions relies on concentration inequalities, which provide probabilistic guarantees on how much the observed rewards deviate from their expected values. In particular, we focus on rewards that are sub-Gaussian with a finite sub-Gaussian parameter. This assumption ensures that the reward distribution satisfies strong tail bounds, making it possible to derive meaningful confidence sets. To formalize this in the multi-armed quantum bandit setting, we state the following lemma, which will be used repeatedly throughout our analysis.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Consider a general or discrete multi-armed quantum bandit problem
(\mathcal{A},\Gamma)
with environment
\rho\in\Gamma
such that, for any
O\in\mathcal{A}
,
\|O\|<\infty
. Then if
O_{A_{t}}\in\mathcal{A}
is the observable selected at round
t\in[T]
, the rewards are of the form

\displaystyle X_{t}=\Tr\left(\rho O_{A_{t}}\right)+\epsilon_{t},

(4.2)

where the statistical noise
\epsilon_{t}
is
\eta
-subgaussian given
X_{1},O_{A_{1}},...,X_{t-1},O_{A_{t-1}},O_{A_{t}}
with

\displaystyle\eta\leq\max_{O\in\mathcal{A}}\|O\|.

(4.3)

Proof 4.1.2
.

We define
\epsilon_{t}
simply as

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t}:=X_{t}-\Tr\left(\rho O_{A_{t}}\right),

(4.4)

and the form (
4.2
) follows. Given
\lambda_{\max}(O)
and
\lambda_{\min}(O)
we have that
\lambda_{\min}({O_{t}})\leq X_{t}\leq\lambda_{\max}(O_{t})
and
\lambda_{\max}(O_{t})-\lambda_{\min}(O_{t})\leq 2\|O_{A_{t}}\|
. Then we can apply Hoeffding Lemma
[
52
, Equation 4.16]
and

\displaystyle\EX[e^{\lambda(X_{t}-\Tr(\rho O_{A_{t}})}]\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}\|O_{A_{t}}\|^{2}}{2}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}(\max_{O\in\mathcal{A}}\|O\|)^{2}}{2}\right)

(4.5)

And the result follows from the definition of subgaussian parameter.

4.1.1
LinUCB
algorithm for multi-armed quantum bandits

In this section, we review one of the most well-known algorithms for linear stochastic bandits, called
LinUCB
(linear upper confidence bound) or
LinRel
(linear reinforcement learning). We will just call it
LinUCB
. The first algorithm for linear stochastic bandits was studied in
[
6
]
, and the first one based on the optimistic principle in
[
16
]
where the authors considered a finite action set. The version of the
LinUCB
algorithm that we introduce is based on
[
42
,
88
,
4
]
, which extended the setting to allow an infinite action set. This algorithm follows the optimism in the face of uncertainty principle to construct confidence sets and select actions accordingly. In the following, we present the algorithm and its main properties following these references before discussing its application to our model.

Recall that in the classical linear model, the rewards are sampled as

X_{t}=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle+\epsilon_{t},

(4.6)

where
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the unknown parameter,
A_{t}\in\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the chosen action, and
\epsilon_{t}
is conditionally sub-Gaussian noise. The
LinUCB
algorithm minimizes regret by constructing a confidence ellipsoid for the unknown parameter
\theta
and selecting the action in
\mathcal{A}
that maximizes the estimated reward within this confidence region.

The confidence region is built using the least-squares estimator

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{t}=\argmin_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\left(X_{s}-\langle\theta^{\prime},A_{s}\rangle\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\right),

(4.7)

where
X_{s}
is the reward received at round
s
,
A_{s}
is the action selected from
\mathcal{A}
, and
\lambda\geq 0
is a regularization parameter ensuring the function has a unique minimum.

Solving this minimization problem yields the closed-form estimator

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{t}=V_{t}^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{t}A_{s}X_{s},

(4.8)

where
V_{t}
, known as the
design matrix
, is defined as

\displaystyle V_{t}:=\lambda\mathbb{I}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}A_{s}A_{s}^{\mathsf{T}}.

(4.9)

By construction,
V_{t}
is positive definite and induces the norm
\|x\|^{2}_{V_{t}}=\langle x,V_{t}x\rangle
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Using the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{t}
, we can construct a confidence region
\mathcal{C}_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
at each round
t
. The following theorem provides the exact form of this confidence region.

Theorem 4.1.3

(Theorem 20.5 in
[
67
]
)
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
(A_{t})^{\infty}_{t=1}
be the actions of linear stochastic bandit selected by some policy with corresponding rewards
(X_{t})^{\infty}_{t=1}
given by
X_{t}=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle+\epsilon_{t}
, where
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the unknown parameter and
\epsilon_{t}
is
\eta
-subgaussian. Let
L,m\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\|A_{t}\|_{2}
and
\|\theta\|_{2}\leq m
. Then we can define the following confidence region

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{t}:=\{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\theta^{\prime}-\hat{\theta}_{t}\|^{2}_{V_{t}}\leq\beta_{t,\delta}\},

(4.10)

where
\hat{\theta}_{t}
is defined as in (
4.8
),
V_{t}
in (
4.9
) for some
\lambda>0
, and

\displaystyle\beta_{t,\delta}=\left(\eta\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{\delta}+d\log\left(\frac{d\lambda+tL^{2}}{d\lambda}\right)}+\eta\sqrt{\lambda}\right)^{2}.

(4.11)

Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left(\forall s\in[t]:\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{s}\right)\geq 1-\delta.

(4.12)

We note that the confidence region given in Theorem
4.1.3
is an ellipsoid centered at the least squares estimator and aligned with the principal axes of the eigenvectors of the design matrix. A sketch of this region for a particular case in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is shown in Figure
4.1
. We will provide a proof of the confidence region
\mathcal{C}_{t}
in Section
4.5.1
for a modified version of the Lemma.

Given the confidence region (
4.188
), the
LinUCB
algorithm proceeds as follows: at each round
t\in\{1,\dots,T\}
, it constructs the confidence region
\mathcal{C}_{t}
, selects an optimistic estimate
\tilde{\theta}_{t}
, and chooses the action that maximizes the estimated reward:

(A_{t},\tilde{\theta}_{t})=\argmax_{A\in\mathcal{A},\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{t-1}}\langle\theta^{\prime},A\rangle.

(4.13)

The algorithm then selects
A_{t}
and observes the corresponding reward. The vector
\tilde{\theta}_{t}
can be interpreted as the optimistic estimate of the parameter used by the algorithm. The complete pseudo-code is provided in Algorithm
1
.

\begin{overpic}[figures/eigenvalue_scaling.png]
\put(20.0,50.0){$\mathbb{S}^{1}$}
\put(50.0,43.0){$A_{t}$}
\put(74.0,49.0){$\hat{\theta}_{t-1}$}
\put(30.0,20.0){$V_{t}=\lambda I+\sum_{s=1}^{t}A_{s}A_{s}^{\text{T}}$}
\put(80.0,65.0){\rotatebox{-60.0}{$\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{t-1})}}$}}
\put(80.0,18.0){\rotatebox{30.0}{$\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{\lambda_{\max}(V_{t-1})}}$}}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.1:
Sketch of a linear bandit with
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, where
\hat{\theta}_{t-1}
is the least squares (
4.8
) centered around the confidence region defined in Theorem
4.1.3
. The orientation of the ellipsoid is defined by the eigenvectors of
V_{t}
along its principal axis and the lengths are given by the eigenvalues.

Algorithm 1

LinUCB

1:

for

t=1,2,\ldots

do

2:

(A_{t},\tilde{\theta}_{t})=\argmax_{A\in\mathcal{A},\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{t-1}}\langle\theta,A\rangle
;

3:

Select
A_{t}
and observe reward
X_{t}
;

4:

Update
\mathcal{C}_{t}
;

5:

end

for

Regret analysis

With the tools introduced above, we are now ready to present the regret bound achieved by
LinUCB
. While the proof follows the same steps as in
[
67
, Chapter 19]
, we reproduce it here for completeness. Understanding this proof will help illustrate the limitations of standard approaches and justify why we later introduce a different algorithm that achieves better regret scaling in certain multi-armed quantum bandit settings. First of all we state the elliptical potential lemma, which is a standard tool for analyzing regrets in online learning problems especially for linear stochastic bandits
[
34
]
.

Lemma 4.1.4

(Elliptical potential lemma, Lemma 19.4 in
[
67
]
)
.

Let
a_{1},...,a_{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a sequence of vectors with
\|a_{t}\|_{2}\leq L<\infty
for all
t\in[T]
,
V_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
positive definite and
V_{t}=V_{0}+\sum_{s\leq t}a_{s}a_{s}^{\mathsf{T}}
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\min\{1,\|a_{t}\|^{2}_{V^{-1}_{t-1}}\}\leq 2\log\left(\frac{\det V_{T}}{\det V_{0}}\right)\leq 2d\log\left(\frac{\Tr(V_{0})+TL^{2}}{d\det(V_{0})^{1/d}}\right).

(4.14)

The following regret bound is derived using the above lemma along with the probabilistic guarantees of
\mathcal{C}_{t}
from Theorem
4.1.3
. We will use the normalization of the regret
\sup_{a,b\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\theta,a-b\rangle\leq 1
.

Theorem 4.1.5

(Theorem 19.2 in
[
67
]
)
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
be the finite time horizon. Under the assumptions of Theorem
4.1.3
for
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
the regret (
2.34
) of
LinUCB
satisfies with probability at least
1-\delta

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\theta,\pi)\leq\sqrt{8dT\beta_{T,\delta}\log\left(\frac{d\lambda+TL^{2}}{d\lambda}\right)},

(4.15)

where
\beta_{T}
is defined as in (
4.11
). In particular the expected regret of
LinUCB
with
\delta=1/T
is bounded by

\displaystyle\EX_{\theta,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\theta,\pi)]\leq Cd\eta\sqrt{T}\log(T),

(4.16)

where
C>0
is a suitably large universal constant, and
\eta
is the subgaussian parameter of the rewards.

Proof 4.1.6
.

Let
A^{*}=\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\theta,a\rangle
be the optimal action. We will prove the following for all
t\in[T]
under the assumption that
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{t}
and apply that this holds with probability at least
1-\delta
using Theorem
4.1.3
. Then using the action selection rule of the algorithm (
4.13
) and the assumption
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{t}
and we have

\displaystyle\langle\theta,A^{*}\rangle\leq\langle\tilde{\theta}_{t},A_{t}\rangle,

(4.17)

where
\tilde{\theta}_{t}
is the optimistic parameter selected by the algorithm at time step
t
. Using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the above inequality and again the assumption
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{t}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\theta,A^{*}-A_{t}\rangle\leq\|A_{t}\|_{V^{-1}_{t-1}}\|\tilde{\theta}_{t}-\theta\|_{V_{t-1}}\leq 2\|A_{t}\|_{V^{-1}_{t-1}}\sqrt{\beta_{t,\delta}}.

(4.18)

Then using the normalization
\sup_{a,b\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\theta,a-b\rangle\leq 1
and
\beta_{T}\geq\max\{1,\beta_{t}\}
we have

\displaystyle\langle\theta,A^{*}-A_{t}\rangle\leq 2\sqrt{\beta_{T,\delta}}\min\{1,\|A_{t}\|_{V^{-1}_{t-1}}\},

(4.19)

Then finally applying Cauchy-Schwarz inequality into the regret expression we have

\displaystyle R_{T}\left(\mathcal{A},\theta,\pi\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\theta,A^{*}-A_{t}\rangle\leq\sqrt{T\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\theta,A^{*}-A_{t}\rangle^{2}}

(4.20)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{T\beta_{T,\delta}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\min\{1,\|A_{t}\|_{V^{-1}_{t-1}}\}}.

(4.21)

The result follows applying the elliptical potential Lemma
4.1.4
.

Given the regret bound for
LinUCB
stated in Theorem
4.1.5
we can use the connection between linear stochastic bandits and multi-armed quantum bandits from Section
2.3
in order to get a minimax regret upper bound for the MAQB model. The following theorem shows how the
LinUCB
can be applied to the multi-armed quantum bandit problem and the scaling of the regret.

Theorem 4.1.7
.

Let
d,T\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider a general or discrete multi-armed quantum bandit described by
(\mathcal{A},\Gamma)
where
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{d}
is the action set and
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{d}
is the set of potential environments. Consider the normalization of the action set
\|O\|\leq 1
for any
O\in\mathcal{A}
. Then the
LinUCB
algorithm
1
, associated to policy
\pi
, can be applied to the general or discrete multi-armed bandit problem such that for any
\rho\in\Gamma
the expected regret is bounded by

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]\leq Cd^{2}\sqrt{T}\log(T),

(4.22)

for some constant
C>0
that depends on the action set.

Proof 4.1.8
.

Considering a set
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d^{2}}
of orthonormal Hermitian matrices, we can parametrize
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
and any
O_{a}\in\mathcal{A}
as
\rho=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{2}}\theta_{i}\sigma_{i}
,
O_{a}=\sum_{i=1}^{d^{2}}A_{a,i}\sigma_{i}
where
\theta_{i}=\Tr(\rho\sigma_{i})
and
A_{a,i}=\Tr(O_{a}\sigma_{i})
. Note that
\theta,A_{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
. We fix the normalization of the basis to
\Tr(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j})=1
, thus since
\Tr(\rho^{2})\leq 1
we have
\|\theta\|_{2}\leq 1
and
\|A_{a}\|_{2}\leq\|O_{a}\|_{2}=\Tr(O^{2}_{a})
. We also have
\Tr(\rho O_{a})=\langle\theta,A_{a}\rangle
and the rewards are of the form

\displaystyle X_{t}=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle+\epsilon_{t},

(4.23)

where
\epsilon_{t}
is
\eta
-subgaussian with
\eta\leq\max_{O_{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\|O\|=1
by Lemma
4.1.1
. Note that the regret can be rewritten as

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\max_{O_{A}\in\mathcal{A}}\langle\theta,A\rangle-\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle.

(4.24)

Then we can use the regret bound of Theorem
4.1.3
and Theorem
4.1.5
under the linear stochastic bandit given by the considered vector parametrization.

4.1.2
UCB
and
Phased Elimination
algorithms for discrete multi-armed quantum bandits

The previous algorithm can be applied to both discrete and general multi-armed quantum bandits. From Theorem
4.1.5
, we see that the regret scales with the dimension of the Hilbert space. However, in cases where the action set is discrete and consists of only a few observables, it is natural to ask whether we can achieve a regret bound that depends on the number of observables instead. The key idea is that when the number of observables is small, we can estimate their mean rewards individually and apply the optimistic principle by treating each action independently, rather than relying on a high-dimensional parameter estimation.

This approach is precisely the foundation of one of the earliest multi-armed bandit algorithms, the
Upper Confidence Bound
(
UCB
) algorithm, first introduced in
[
65
]
. We will see that under certain conditions on the number of actions and the dimension of the Hilbert space,
UCB
can provide a tighter regret bound than
LinUCB
. To understand this, we will first review the
UCB
algorithm and introduce a simplified version of the multi-armed bandit problem.

Multi-armed stochastic bandit

A
multi-armed stochastic bandit
consists of a set of probability distributions (the environment)
\nu=(P_{a}:a\in\mathcal{A})
, where
\mathcal{A}
is the set of available actions with cardinality
k=|\mathcal{A}|
. Each action
i\in[k]
has an associated mean reward
\mu_{i}
. At each round
t\in[n]
, a learner selects an action
A_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
and observes a reward
X_{t}\sim P_{A_{t}}
. The expected regret is defined as

\displaystyle R_{T}(\nu,\pi)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\max_{i\in[k]}\mu_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi}[X_{t}],

(4.25)

where
\pi
denotes the policy and
\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi}
is the reward distribution induced by the interaction of the policy
\pi
with the environment
\nu
. The
sub-optimality gap
for each action
a\in[k]
is given by
\Delta_{a}=\max_{i\in[k]}\mu_{i}-\mu_{a}
, which measures how much worse the action
a
is compared to the optimal one.

This model can be seen as a special case of a linear bandit problem with the following structure:

•

The action set is discrete, consisting of
k
elements, so that
\mathcal{A}=\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
, where
e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
are the standard basis vectors.

•

The unknown parameter is the reward vector
\theta=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

•

The reward noise is given by
\epsilon_{t}=X_{t}-\mu_{A_{t}}
, where
X_{t}\sim P_{A_{t}}
.

Thus, if we consider each action independently, a discrete multi-armed quantum bandit can also be viewed as a multi-armed stochastic bandit.

UCB algorithm

The core idea of the
UCB
algorithm is to estimate the mean reward of each action while ensuring that, with high probability, these estimates are optimistic. This approach follows the optimistic principle described in (
4.1
), where the algorithm selects actions based on the upper confidence bound of their estimated rewards. Unlike the linear bandit setting, where the confidence region
\mathcal{C}_{t}
is an ellipsoid, here it is shaped along
k
independent directions. Each direction corresponds to an action, and its length is determined by an upper bound on the estimated mean reward.

The version of
UCB
that we describe is based on the following concentration bound for subgaussian random variables. If
(Z_{t})_{t=1}^{T}
is a sequence of independent
\eta
-subgaussian random variables with mean
\mu
and empirical mean
\hat{\mu}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}Z_{t}
, then for any
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left(\mu\geq\hat{\mu}+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{T}}\right)\leq\delta.

(4.26)

This bound states that, with high probability, the true mean
\mu
is close to the empirical mean
\hat{\mu}
, up to a confidence radius that decreases as more samples are collected.

Using this bound, the
UCB
algorithm estimates the reward of each action optimistically following (
4.1
). Let
T
be the total number of rounds, and let
(X_{t})_{t=1}^{T}
be the sequence of observed
\eta
-subgaussian rewards in a multi-armed stochastic bandit. For each action
a\in[k]
, we define its empirical mean as

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{a}(T)=\frac{1}{T_{a}(T)}\sum_{t=1}^{T}X_{t}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t}=a\},

(4.27)

where
A_{t}\in[k]
is the action selected at round
t
, and
T_{a}(T)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbbm{1}\{A_{t}=a\}
represents the number of times action
a
has been chosen up to round
T
. At each round
t
, the
UCB
algorithm assigns an upper confidence estimate
\tilde{\mu}_{a}(t)
to each action
a\in[k]
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{a}(t)=\begin{cases}\infty,&\text{if }T_{a}(t-1)=0,\\
\hat{\mu}_{a}(t-1)+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{T_{a}(t-1)}},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(4.28)

The assignment of
\infty
ensures that each action is selected at least once in the first
k
rounds. Then, the action selected at round
t
is the one with the highest upper confidence estimate:

\displaystyle A_{t}=\argmax_{a\in[k]}\tilde{\mu}_{a}(t).

(4.29)

This strategy balances exploration and exploitation by favoring actions that have either shown high empirical rewards or have been played infrequently and have thus a large uncertainty. The pseudocode of the
UCB
algorithm is presented in Algorithm
2
.

Algorithm 2

UCB

1:

for

t=1,2,\ldots,T

do

2:

Choose action
A_{t}=\argmax_{i\in[k]}\begin{cases}\infty\quad\text{if}\quad T_{i}(t-1)=0.\\
\hat{\mu}_{i}(t-1)+\sqrt{\frac{2\eta^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{T_{i}(t-1)}}\quad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

3:

Observe reward
X_{t}
and update confidence bounds;

4:

end

for

The following result on the regret scaling of the
UCB
algorithm for discrete multi-armed quantum bandits follows from Lemma
4.1.1
and the regret analysis of
UCB
for stochastic bandits in
[
67
, Theorem 7.2]
. Applying
UCB
to a discrete multi-armed quantum bandit problem
(\mathcal{A},\Gamma)
is straightforward: we fix an environment
\rho\in\Gamma
and define the corresponding action set as

\displaystyle\nu=\left(P_{\rho}(\cdot|a):O_{a}\in\mathcal{A}\right).

(4.30)

Then we can treat each action independently without need of estimating the unknown
\rho\in\Gamma
.

Theorem 4.1.9
.

Let
d,T\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider a discrete multi-armed quantum bandit described by
(\mathcal{A},\Gamma)
, where
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{d}
is the action set and
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{d}
is the set of potential environments. Consider the normalization of the action set
\|O\|\leq 1
for any
O\in\mathcal{A}
. Let
k=|\mathcal{A}|
be the number of available actions. Then, the
UCB
algorithm
2
, following policy
\pi
, can be applied to this setting with
\delta=\frac{1}{T^{2}}
, ensuring that for any
\rho\in\Gamma
, the expected regret is bounded by

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]\leq 8\sqrt{Tk\log(T)}+\sum_{a=1}^{k}\Delta_{a},

(4.31)

where
\Delta_{a}=\max_{O\in\mathcal{A}}\Tr(\rho O)-\Tr(\rho O_{a})
for
a\in[k]
.

Next, we compare the regret scaling of
UCB
with that of
LinUCB
for discrete multi-armed quantum bandits. Let
k
be the number of observables and
d
the dimension of the Hilbert space. Theorem
4.1.9
shows that when
k\leq d^{4}
, the regret scaling of
UCB
is more favorable than that of
LinUCB
(see Theorem (
4.1.7
)). However, it is important to note that when applying
UCB
to discrete multi-armed quantum bandits, the algorithm does not exploit the underlying linear structure of the rewards.

Phased Elimination algorithm

One can further refine the dependence of the regret on the dimension for
LinUCB
in the discrete multi-armed quantum bandit setting. A variant of
LinUCB
, known as
Phased Elimination
(see
[
67
, Chapter 22]
), leverages optimal design techniques and assumes a finite number of actions. This algorithm, stated in Algorithm
3
, can be applied to discrete multi-armed quantum bandits under the assumptions of Theorem
4.1.7
, requiring the action set
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
to be finite.

The key idea behind phased elimination is to structure the learning process into successive phases, where in each phase, the algorithm maintains a set of promising actions and gradually eliminates suboptimal ones based on collected data. Instead of updating estimates after every round, the algorithm gathers observations over a phase and then uses an optimal design criterion (such as G-optimality
[
67
, Chapter 21]
) to determine the next set of actions to explore.

The analysis of Theorem
[
67
, Theorem 22.1]
, together with the observations from the proof of Theorem
4.1.7
, leads directly to the following result.

Theorem 4.1.10
.

Let
d,T\in\mathbb{N}
, and consider a discrete multi-armed quantum bandit described by
(\mathcal{A},\Gamma)
where
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{d}
is the action set and
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{d}
is the set of potential environments. Consider the normalization of the action set
\|O\|
for any
O
in
\mathcal{A}
. Let
k=|\mathcal{A}|
be the number of available actions, Consider the normalization of the action set
\sup_{O_{a},O_{b}\in\mathcal{A}}\Tr(\rho(O_{a}-O_{b}))\leq 1
for any
\rho\in\Gamma
. Then, the
Phased Elimination
algorithm
3
associated to policy
\pi
, with
\delta=\frac{1}{T}
, can be applied to the discrete multi-armed bandit problem and for some universal constant
C>0
and for all
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
the expected regret is bounded by

\displaystyle\EX_{\rho,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\rho,\pi)]\leq Cd\sqrt{T\log(Tk)}.

(4.32)

Note that the scaling is better than
LinUCB
since the dimensional factor is
d
instead of
d^{2}
as long as the number of observables
k
is not exponential in the dimension of the Hilbert space.

Algorithm 3

Phased Elimination

1:

Set
l=1
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\mathcal{A}
.

2:

Let
t_{l}=t
be the current time step
t
and find
\pi_{l}:\mathcal{A}_{l}\rightarrow[0,1]
with
\mathtt{support}(\pi_{l})\leq\frac{d(d+1)}{2}
that maximises

\log\det\left(\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi(a)aa^{T}\right)\;\;\text{subject to }\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}\pi(a)=1

3:

Let
\epsilon_{l}=\frac{1}{2^{l}}
and
T_{l}(a)=\left\lceil\frac{2d\pi_{l}(\mathbb{a})}{\epsilon^{2}_{l}}\log\left(\frac{kl(l+1)}{\delta}\right)\right\rceil
and
T_{l}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}T_{l}(\mathbb{a})

4:

Choose each action
a\in\mathcal{A}_{l}
exactly
T_{l}({a})
times

5:

Calculate the empirical estimate:

\hat{\theta}_{l}=V^{-1}_{l}\sum_{t=t_{l}}^{t_{l}+T_{l}}A_{t}X_{t}\;\;\text{with }V_{l}=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}T_{l}(a)aa^{T}

6:

Eliminate low rewarding arms:

\mathcal{A}_{l+1}=\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{l}:\max_{b\in\mathcal{A}_{l}}\;\hat{\theta}_{l}\cdot(b-a)\leq 2\epsilon_{l}\}

7:

l=l+1
and
Goto Step 2

4.2
Explore–then–commit tomography for the PSMAQB

After reviewing well-known algorithms for linear stochastic bandits, we found that they achieve a regret scaling of
\sqrt{T}
in the time horizon
T
