. This matches the lower bounds established in Chapter
3
, except in the case of the PSMAQB setting where the action set consists of rank-1 projectors and the environment is a pure quantum state. Recall that in this setting, the regret is given by

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{d},\psi,\pi)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle,

(4.33)

where
\rho=|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|
is the unknown pure quantum state, and
\Pi_{A_{t}}
is the rank-1 projector chosen from the action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{S}^{*}_{d}
, which consists of all rank-1 projectors.

From Theorem
3.3.7
, the minimax lower bound for this setting is
\Omega(\log T)
, which does not match the
O(\sqrt{T})
regret of existing algorithms. This lower bound was derived by exploiting the connection between state tomography and this specific bandit setting. This raises the natural question of whether quantum state tomography techniques can also be used to improve the upper bound on regret.

A straightforward approach is to apply an
explore-then-commit
strategy. In this method, a fraction of the time horizon
T
is dedicated solely to estimating the unknown quantum state. Once the estimation is sufficiently accurate, the algorithm commits to a single action for the remaining rounds based on that estimate. The algorithm that we propose is based on the
projected least squares
(PLS) method given in
[
49
]
, but any other single copy state tomography algorithm can be adapted (as long as we can map the outcome measurements to outcomes of rank-1 projectors). Now, we briefly review the PLS methond and explain how to apply it for the regret analysis.

Least-squares quantum state tomography algorithm

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
an unknown quantum state that we want to estimate, and
\left\{M_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{m}
the elements of a POVM used to measure
\rho
. Suppose we perform measurements over
T
rounds, and let
t_{i}
be the number of times we observe the outcome
i
associated with the element
M_{i}
from the POVM. Since the probabilities of each outcome
i
are given by Born’s rule
\Tr(\rho M_{i})
, the least squares estimator for
\rho
is defined as

\displaystyle L_{T}=\argmin_{X\in\mathcal{O}_{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\frac{t_{i}}{T}-\Tr\left(M_{i}X\right)\right)^{2}.

(4.34)

The estimator
L_{T}
is not necessarily a physical state, so the next step is to project it onto the space of physical states

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{T}=\argmin_{\tilde{\rho}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}}\|L_{T}-\tilde{\rho}\|_{2},

(4.35)

where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
denotes the Frobenius norm. In
[
49
]
, this estimator is analyzed for structured POVMs, Pauli observables, and Pauli basis measurements. In our case, we use Pauli observables. Fix
d=2^{n}
, for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\left\{\sigma_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{d^{2}}
be the set of all possible tensor products of the
2\times 2
Pauli matrices. These matrices form a basis of the form (
2.35
). For Pauli observables, the least squares estimator (
4.34
) takes the form

\displaystyle L_{T}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d^{2}}\left(\frac{t_{i}^{+}-t_{i}^{-}}{T/d^{2}}\right)\sigma_{i},

(4.36)

where
t_{i}^{\pm}
are the empirical frequencies associated with the two-outcome POVM
\Pi_{i}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{I}\pm\sigma_{i})
, and each observable is measured
T/d^{2}
times. The convergence analysis in
[
49
]
leads to the following theorem.

Theorem 4.2.1

(Theorem 1 in
[
49
]
)
.

Let
\rho
be a quantum state in a
d
-dimensional Hilbert space, and fix a number of samples
T\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, using Pauli measurements, the PLS estimator
\hat{\rho}_{T}
(
4.35
) satisfies

\displaystyle\Pr\left(\|\hat{\rho}_{T}-\rho\|_{1}\leq\epsilon\right)\geq 1-e^{-\frac{T\epsilon^{2}}{43d^{2}r^{2}}}\quad\text{for}\quad\epsilon\in[0,1],

(4.37)

where
r=\min\left\{\mathrm{rank}(\rho),\mathrm{rank}(\hat{\rho}_{T})\right\}
.

Explore-Then-Commit algorithm

After reviewing the PLS estimation method we explain how we use it to construct an algorithm for our pure state problem and how to analyse the regret. Recall that we have considered an action set that contains all rank-1 projectors. For that reason we consider the algorithm for one qubit states (
d=2
) since Pauli observables for one qubit can be measured using rank-1 projectors. The pseudo-code for the algorithm can be found in Algorithm
4
. If
T
is the number of rounds, fix the error
\epsilon^{2}=\frac{172\log(T)}{\sqrt{T}}
and assume
\frac{172\log(T)}{\sqrt{T}}\leq 1
in order to have
\epsilon\in[0,1]
. The condition
\frac{172\log(T)}{\sqrt{T}}\leq 1
is achieved by
T\geq 8\cdot 10^{6}
. Then the algorithm (policy
\pi
) works as follows.

•

During the first
\sqrt{T}
rounds we perform the Pauli observables measurements, and calculate the estimator
\hat{\rho}_{\sqrt{T}}
(
4.35
) for
\rho
. In our setting this using the MAQB framework the two-outcome POVM
\Pi_{i}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{I}\pm\sigma_{i})
simply corresponds to the rank-1 projector observable
\Pi^{+}_{i}
. The frequencies can be written as

\displaystyle t^{+}_{i}=\sum_{t=1}^{\sqrt{T}}\mathbbm{1}\{X_{t}=1,A_{t}=\Pi^{+}_{i}\},\quad\Pi^{+}_{i}=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{i}.

(4.38)

Then using Theorem
4.2.1
we have,

\displaystyle P_{\rho,\pi}\left(\|\hat{\rho}_{\sqrt{T}}-\rho\|_{1}\leq\epsilon\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{T},

(4.39)

where we have used
r=1
since
\mathrm{rank}(\rho)=1
and
\epsilon^{2}=\frac{172\log(T)}{\sqrt{T}}
. In order to ensure that the estimator is pure we project the estimator into the rank-1 subspace in a
\epsilon-
ball as follows,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}=\argmin_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2}}\|\hat{\rho}_{\sqrt{T}}-\rho\|_{1}\quad\text{such that}\quad\|\hat{\rho}_{\sqrt{T}}-\rho\|_{1}\leq\epsilon.

(4.40)

Recall that for the above equation there is at least one solution with probability greater than
1-1/T
since
\rho
is rank-1 by assumption. Note that using (
4.39
) we have,

\displaystyle P_{\rho,\pi}\left(\|\hat{\rho}-\rho\|_{1}\leq 2\epsilon\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{T}.

(4.41)

•

For the remaining rounds we perform the measurement using
\hat{\rho}=|\hat{\psi}\rangle\!\langle\hat{\psi}|=\hat{\Pi}
as the rank-1 projector from the action set.

Algorithm 4

Bandit PLS

1:

for

i=1,2,\ldots,d^{2}

do

2:

for

j=1,2,\ldots,\lceil\sqrt{T}\rceil/d^{2}

do

3:

Measure
\rho
with
\sigma_{i}
;

4:

Update outcomes
t_{i}^{\pm}
;

5:

end

for

6:

end

for

7:

Compute
L_{T}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d^{2}}\left(\frac{t_{i}^{+}-t_{i}^{-}}{T/d^{2}}\right)\sigma_{i}
;

8:

Compute
\hat{\rho}_{\sqrt{T}}=\argmin_{\tilde{\rho}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}}\|L_{T}-\tilde{\rho}\|_{2}
;

9:

Fix
\epsilon^{2}=\frac{43d^{2}\log(T)}{\sqrt{T}}

10:

Compute
\hat{\rho}=\argmin_{\rho\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{d}}\|\hat{\rho}_{\sqrt{T}}-\rho\|_{1}\quad\text{such that}\quad\|\hat{\rho}_{\sqrt{T}}-\rho\|_{1}\leq\epsilon

11:

for

t=\lceil\sqrt{T}\rceil+1,...,n

do

12:

Measure
\rho
with
\hat{\rho}=\hat{\Pi}
;

13:

end

for

Theorem 4.2.2
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
denote the time horizon, assume
\frac{172\log(T)}{\sqrt{T}}\leq 1

\left(T\geq 8\cdot 10^{6}\right)
. Consider the PSMAQB setting with
d=2
(qubits). Then, for all environments
\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2}
the
Bandit PLS
algorithm
4
associated to policy
\pi
achieves the expected regret

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi)]=O\left(\sqrt{T}\log(T)\right).

(4.42)

Proof 4.2.3
.

First we can rewrite the regret in terms trace distance as,

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi):=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\big\||\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|-\Pi_{A_{t}}\big\|^{2}_{1}\,,

(4.43)

and then we will take the expectation value. During the first
\sqrt{T}
rounds the algorithm performs the Pauli measurements using rank-1 projectors and builds the estimator
\hat{\rho}
using Equation
(
4.40
)
. Using Equation (
4.41
) we have the probabilistic error bound

\displaystyle P_{\psi,\pi}\left(\||\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|-\hat{\rho}\|_{1}\leq 2\epsilon\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{T},

(4.44)

where
\epsilon^{2}=\frac{172\log(T)}{\sqrt{T}}
. Then, with probability greater than
1-\frac{1}{T}
, the regret can be bounded as,

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi)\leq\sqrt{T}+(T-\sqrt{T})\frac{172\log(T)}{\sqrt{T}}=O(\sqrt{T}\log(T)),

(4.45)

where the first term comes from the trivial bound
\frac{1}{2}\||\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|-\Pi_{A_{t}}\|_{1}\leq 1
for the first
\sqrt{T}
rounds and the second term from the bound of Equation (
4.44
) for the remaining rounds where
\Pi_{A_{t}}
is the estimator (
4.40
). Let
C
be the hidden constant in
O(\sqrt{T}\log(T))
, and
G
the probabilistic event where
R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi)\leq C\sqrt{T}\log(T)
and
G^{C}
its complement. Note that
P_{\psi,\pi}(G^{C})\leq 1/T
. Then we can calculate the expected regret as,

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi)]=\EX_{\psi,\pi}[\mathbbm{1}\left\{G\right\}R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi)]+\EX_{\psi,\pi}[\mathbbm{1}\left\{G^{C}\right\}R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi)].

(4.46)

For the first term we use the bound (
4.45
) given by the event G. For the second term we use the trivial bound
R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi)\leq T
combined with
P_{\psi,\pi}(G_{i}^{C})\leq 1/T
. Thus,

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2},\psi,\pi)]\leq C\sqrt{T}\log(T)+TP_{\psi,\pi}(G_{i}^{C})\leq C\sqrt{T}\log(T)+1=O(\sqrt{T}\log(T)).

(4.47)

4.3
Beyond the Square Root Barrier

From Section
4.1
, we see that standard
UCB
-type algorithms achieve an
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{T})
regret bound across all MAQB settings almost matching all minimax lower bounds studied in Chapter
3
. However, this remains suboptimal compared to the
\Omega(\log T)
lower bound we established for the PSMAQB setting. Since the
UCB
algorithms we used are general-purpose, they apply to a broad range of settings but do not exploit the specific structure of the quantum problem. A key observation is that the environment is given by a pure state
|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{d}
, which suggests the possibility of exploiting its lower-dimensional structure to design a more efficient algorithm. In Section
4.2
, we applied this idea to construct a simple explore-then-commit algorithm. While this approach still yields an
O(\sqrt{T})
regret bound, it only involves a single round of adaptivity, and one might argue that a more adaptive algorithm could achieve a better scaling. The first question that we ask ourselves is why
LinUCB
achieves the square root scaling. From the proof in Theorem
4.1.5
we see that the square root behavior is introduced when we apply Cauchy-Schwarz as

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathcal{A},\theta,\pi)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\theta,A^{*}-A_{t}\rangle\leq\sqrt{T\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\theta,A^{*}-A_{t}\rangle^{2}}.

(4.48)

Then the term
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\theta,A^{*}-A_{t}\rangle^{2}
can be argued to behave logarithmically in
T
using the optimistic principle and the elliptical potenital lemma
4.1.4
. This procedure always introduces a
\sqrt{T}
and for that reason we need to develop a new technique to upper bound the cumulative regret based on a tight control of the instantaneous regret
\langle\theta,A^{*}-A_{t}\rangle
at each time step
t\in[T]
. In order to design the algorithm we will take advantage of the underlying structure of the problem, specifically that the problem is constrained to the Bloch sphere.

One of the key features of this problem is its underlying geometry. For qubits, we can represent any pure state projector
|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|\in\mathcal{S}_{2}^{*}
using the Pauli basis
\sigma=(\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y},\sigma_{z})
as follows

\displaystyle|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|=\frac{\mathbb{I}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}r\cdot\sigma,

(4.49)

where
r\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a unit vector. This suggests that we can study the problem as a classical linear stochastic bandit with an action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, a
(d-1)
-dimensional sphere, and an unknown parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Existing regret lower bounds for linear bandits, such as those in
[
88
]
and
[
67
, Chapter 24, Theorem 24.2]
, assume that
\theta
lies within the unit ball and give a
\Omega(\sqrt{T})
scaling. These bounds do not apply here because the worst-case instances they construct involve unknown vectors far from the surface of the ball. However, when the noise is finite and
\sigma
-subgaussian, we adapt a lower bound for logistic bandits from
[
7
]
and establish a minimax regret lower bound of
\Omega(\sigma d\sqrt{T})
, as shown in Appendix
B.1
. We denote
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
a normal distribution of mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma
.

Theorem 4.3.1
.

For any policy
\pi
of a stochastic linear bandit with action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x\|_{2}=1\}
, reward noise given by
\epsilon_{t}=\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
where
\sigma>0
, then there exists an unknown parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|\theta\|_{2}=1
and

\displaystyle\EX_{\theta,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)]\geq\frac{1}{100}{\sigma}d\sqrt{T},

(4.50)

for
T\geq\frac{1}{6400}d^{2}{\sigma}^{2}
.

The proof is a simplified proof of the lower bound found in
[
7
]
but we decided to not include it in the main text since the focus of this Chapter is on algorithms. This result shows that for linear bandits with a constant subgaussian parameter (or variance), the
\sqrt{T}
regret scaling remains optimal, even when the problem is constrained to a sphere. One may try to now apply the above lower bound to the MAQB problem with pure state, however the bound does not apply. In order to see this we have to look more closely to the quantum reward model. Recall that the action is given by a rank-1 projector
\Pi_{A_{t}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}^{*}
and the reward is sampled by measuring the two outcome POVM
\{\Pi_{A_{t}},\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{A_{t}}\}
on the unknown state. The reward is given by

\displaystyle X_{t}=\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle+\epsilon_{t},

(4.51)

where
X_{t}\in\{0,1\}
is Bernoulli distributed and
|\psi\rangle
is the environment. We note that the variance of the reward behaves as

\displaystyle\VX[\epsilon_{t}]=\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle(1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle),

(4.52)

which means that it is not constant and actually vanishes linearly as we select actions close to the optimal one. Although the lower bound in Theorem
4.3.1
assumes a different noise model, it already suggests that non-zero reward variance is necessary to achieve the typical
\sqrt{T}
regret scaling. This becomes clear when considering the trivial case where the variance is always zero: in such a scenario, the environment could be learned using only
d^{2}
actions, and the regret would no longer grow with
T
. While the subgaussian parameter also vanishes when actions are chosen close to the unknown environment, its behavior is more complicated. From
[
32
]
we have that the exact
\sigma
-subgaussian parameter of
X_{t}
is given by

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}=\begin{cases}0\quad\text{for}\quad\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle\in\{0,1\}\\
\frac{1}{4}\quad\text{for}\quad\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\\
\frac{1-2\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle}{2\ln\left(\frac{1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle}{\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle}\right)}\quad\text{for}\quad\langle\psi|\Pi_{A_{t}}|\psi\rangle\neq 0,1,\frac{1}{2}.\end{cases}

(4.53)

In Figure
4.2
, we observe that both the variance and the subgaussian parameter of
X_{t}
vanish when selecting actions close to the environment. However, the key difference lies in their rates of decay: the variance decreases linearly, whereas the subgaussian parameter decays more slowly. This observation motivates our focus on the vanishing variance, rather than the subgaussian parameter, when designing the algorithm for the PSMAQB setting.

Figure 4.2:
Plot of the variance and subgaussian parameter of a Bernoulli random variable
X\in\{0,1\}
where
\mathrm{Pr}(X=1)=x
.

We could also try to implement the extra structure given by (
4.52
) into the proof of Theorem
4.3.1
, but this leads to a constant lower bound. Specifically, we encounter the problem that these calculations rely on the computation of the
KL
divergences
D_{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta},\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}})
for two “close” unknown parameters
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
and it is not possible to give a non-trivial bound since their variance is arbitrarily close to
0
when we select actions near the unknown parameters. We can easily see this fact for example if we consider Gaussian distributed rewards and use the divergence decomposition lemma that leads to

\displaystyle D_{KL}(\mathbb{P}_{\theta},\mathbb{P}_{\theta^{\prime}})=\EX_{\theta}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}D_{KL}\left(\mathcal{N}(\langle\theta,a_{t}\rangle,1-\langle\theta,a_{t}\rangle^{2}),\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(\langle\theta^{\prime},a_{t}\rangle,1-\langle\theta^{\prime},a_{t}\rangle^{2})\right)\right],

(4.54)

where
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
is a Gaussian probability distribution with mean
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
and variance
\sigma^{2}\geq 0
. The proof requires to upper bound of the above divergence but a simple computation shows that the divergence
D_{KL}
for the reward distributions cannot be upper bounded since the variances can go arbitrarily close to
0
and we only get the trivial bound
D_{KL}\leq\infty
. Recall that in Section
3.3.2
we also encountered the same issue of constant lower bound when trying to derive a minimmax lower bound for the PSMAQB setting using the techniques we developed for the other MAQB lower bounds. Specifically when computing
D_{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\psi\|\psi^{\prime}\right)
we found that the tradeoff with closeness of the environments and the time horizon
T
(
3.121
) leads to the constant bound. From these observations we conclude that the second structure that we want to exploit is the vanishing variance of the reward.

4.4
Controlling the eigenvalues of the design matrix

From the variance expression in (
4.52
) and the subgaussian parameter in (
4.53
), we see that the statistical noise is not independent of the chosen actions and the environment. In fact, there is a well-defined dependence. This setting, where the noise depends on the actions, is known as
heteroscedastic noise
, and was first studied for linear bandits in
[
61
]
. There, the authors considered a model in which the noise is
\sigma(a_{t})
-subgaussian, where
\sigma(a_{t})
is a
link function
that maps actions
a_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
to real numbers, i.e.,
\sigma:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. The key difference in our model is that the noise also depends on the unknown environment.

One of the techniques introduced in
[
61
]
is to replace the online least-squares estimator (
4.7
)—used, for instance, in the
LinUCB
algorithm—with a weighted version. The main idea is to bias the estimator towards actions with lower noise, using the inverse of the link function as a weight. In our case, we also will need such a weighted estimator, since the noise depends on the unknown environment. We note that the setting studied in
[
61
]
does not provide an improvement over the regret square root barrier since they focus on general class of linear bandits. However, our setting is more concrete, which will allow us to design a simpler strategy while still providing theoretical guarantees on the regret. Although
[
61
]
focuses on link functions that capture the subgaussian parameter, the same ideas can be applied when we have a link function for the variance.

Thus, we introduce an estimator for the noise subgaussian parameter (or variance) at time step
t\in[T]
as

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}:\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\times\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0},

(4.55)

where
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=(A_{1},X_{1},\dots,A_{t-1},X_{t-1})
contains the history of past actions and rewards, and is independent of the unknown environment. For simplicity, we will often omit the dependence on past actions and rewards and simply write
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}(A)
for
A\in\mathcal{A}
. We will refer to
4.55
as the
noise estimator
.
The weighted version of the linear least square estimator for linear bandits is defined with the following minimization problem

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{t}:=\argmin_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A_{s})}\left(X_{s}-\langle\theta^{\prime},A_{s}\rangle\right)^{2}+\lambda\|\theta^{\prime}\|_{2},

(4.56)

where
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a regularization term. The analytical solution is

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{t}=V_{t}^{-1}(\lambda)\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A)}A_{s}X_{s},

(4.57)

where

\displaystyle V_{t}(\lambda)=\lambda\mathbb{I}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A_{s})}A_{s}A^{\mathsf{T}}_{s},

(4.58)

is the
weighted design matrix
.

Note.
The above quantities have been defined in the context of a linear bandit problem rather than the PSMAQB setting. However, recall from (
4.49
) that there exists a one-to-one correspondence between the action set and environment of the qubit PSMAQB problem and the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Therefore, although we will focus on linear bandits in the following, we will later make the explicit connection to the PSMAQB problem.

In the next sections, we will introduce a method that allows us to control the eigenvalues of the design matrix at any time. Combined with the use of weighted estimators, this will enable us to break the square-root barrier. Before presenting the general case, we first illustrate the technique in a simplified setting: a linear bandit on the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. This will help us see how the method works in practice. Later we will consider the general case.

4.4.1
Controlling the eigenvalues of the design matrix in the circle

We now have the tool we want to use to solve our problem, but how should we use it? As mentioned earlier, we aim to design strategies that follow the optimistic principle, which in the case of
LinUCB
typically takes the form given in (
4.1
). As previously discussed, the action selection rule derived from this principle leads to a
\sqrt{T}
regret in the analysis. For this reason, we explore an alternative way to implement the optimistic principle. To illustrate our idea, we begin by studying the setting where the action set is the circle
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{1}
and the unknown parameter also lies on the circle, i.e.,
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. The optimistic principle tells us to select action accordingly to

\displaystyle A_{t}\in\argmax_{A\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}\max_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{t-1}}\langle\theta^{\prime},A\rangle=\mathcal{C}_{t-1}\cap\mathbb{S}^{1},

(4.59)

where
\mathcal{C}_{t-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a confidence region centered in the weighted estimator (
4.57
) with the same form as
LinUCB
i.e

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{t-1}=\{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\|\theta^{\prime}-\hat{\theta}_{t-1}\|^{2}_{V_{t-1}}\leq\tilde{\beta}_{t-1}\},

(4.60)

where
\{\tilde{\beta}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}
is some sequence that defines a well-behaved confidence region (we will work out the specific details later). Using the geometry of the problem we have that for
A_{t}
choosen as in (
4.59
) we can reduce the task of bounding the regret to a geometry problem since

\displaystyle\max_{A\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}\langle\theta,A\rangle-\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle=1-\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\theta-A_{t},\theta-A_{t}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-A_{t}\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.61)

Moreover using that
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t}(\lambda))\mathbb{I}\leq V_{t}(\lambda)
and
a_{t}\in\mathcal{C}_{t-1}
we can compute the distance (
4.61
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-A_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\tilde{\beta}_{t-1}}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{t-1}(\lambda))}.

(4.62)

Thus, to bound the individual terms in the regret, it is enough to understand the behavior of the minimum eigenvalue of the design matrix. For standard
LinUCB
, it is known that the regret scales as
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{T})
, which suggests that
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t}(\lambda))\sim\sqrt{t}
. In fact, in
[
19
]
, the authors show (under mild assumptions) that any strategy minimizing optimally the regret for linear bandits with continuous and smooth action sets must satisfy
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t}(\lambda))=\Omega(\sqrt{t})
for constant noise models.

Since our goal is to go beyond the square-root scaling, this relation is not convenient, and we are motivated to look for a more general condition. We note that
\lambda_{\max}(V_{t}(\lambda))
captures the direction that has been exploited the most, as it relates to the shortest principal axis of the ellipsoid
\mathcal{C}_{t}
. For the unweighted design matrix (
4.9
) with normalized actions
A_{t}
, we have
\Tr(V_{t}(\lambda))=\lambda+t
, so
\lambda_{\max}(V_{t}(\lambda))\sim t
, which suggests the relation

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(V_{t}(\lambda))=\Omega\left(\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(V_{t}(\lambda))}\right),

(4.63)

for any strategy over continuous smooth action sets. Can we prove this relation for the weighted version of
LinUCB
? It turns out that even in the unweighted case, this is still an open problem in
[
19
]
.

In our case, we design an alternative strategy tailored to our setting that ensures this ratio holds. The idea is to select actions by projecting the extremal points of the longest axis of the ellipsoid
\mathcal{C}_{t}
onto
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, as illustrated in Figure
4.3
. This particular choice helps “stabilize” the ellipsoid and guarantees the ratio (
4.63
). Furthermore, we aim to define a general selection rule such that the ratio (
4.63
) holds independently of both the algorithm’s randomness and the noise model. This is formalized in the following theorem

\begin{overpic}[figures/cartoon_intro2.png]
\put(13.0,40.0){ $A^{+}_{t}$}
\put(68.0,15.0){ $A^{-}_{t}$}
\put(70.0,28.0){ $\theta$}
\put(55.0,43.0){ $\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{t-1}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.3:
Scheme for the choice of actions
A_{t}^{+},A_{t}^{-}
as an altertative to
LinUCB
. The actions are selected as the projections of the extremal points across the largest axis of the confidence region centered around a weighted least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{t}
of the unknown parameter
\theta
. This choice is sufficient to increase the minimum eigenvalue of
V_{t}
such that the relation
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})=\Omega(\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(V_{t})})
is satisfied. Moreover, the actions
A_{t}^{+}
and
A_{t}^{-}
are sufficiently close to
\theta
to keep the regret small.

Theorem 4.4.1
.

Let
\{c_{t}\}_{t=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{S}^{1}
be a sequence of normalized vectors and
\omega:\text{P}^{2}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
a function such that

\displaystyle\omega(X)\leq C\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(X)},

(4.64)

for a constant
C>0
and any
X\in\text{P}^{2}_{+}
. Let
\lambda_{0}\geq 4\sqrt{2}C+2
, and define a sequence of matrices

\{V_{t}\}_{t=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
as

\displaystyle V_{0}:=\lambda_{0}\mathbb{I}_{2\times 2},\quad V_{t+1}:=V_{t}+\omega(V_{t})\left(a^{+}_{t+1}(a^{+}_{t+1})^{\mathsf{T}}+a^{-}_{t+1}(a^{-}_{t+1})^{\mathsf{T}}\right),

(4.65)

where
a^{+}_{t+1},a^{-}_{t+1}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
are defined as

\displaystyle a^{+}_{t+1}:=\frac{c_{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})}}v_{t,\min}}{\sqrt{1+\frac{2\langle c_{t},v_{t,\min}\rangle}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})}}},\quad a^{-}_{t+1}:=\frac{c_{t}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})}}v_{t,\min}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2\langle c_{t},v_{t,\min}\rangle}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})}}},

(4.66)

and
v_{\min,t}
is the normalized eigenvector corresponding to the minimum eigenvalue of
V_{t}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})\geq\sqrt{2\lambda_{\max}(V_{t})}\quad\text{for all}\quad t\geq 0.

(4.67)

Proof 4.4.2
.

To simplify the notation in the proof we define

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t}:=\lambda_{\min}(V_{t}),\quad\lambda_{\max,t}:=\lambda_{\max}(V_{t}),

(4.68)

with corresponding normalized eigenvectors
v_{t,\min},v_{t,\max}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
. We will also need the following properties for positive semidefinite matrices
A,B\in\text{P}^{d}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(A+B)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\min}(A)+\lambda_{\min}(B),

(4.69)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(A+B)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max}(A)+\lambda_{\max}(B).

We are going to prove the result by induction. At time step
t=0
we have
\lambda_{\min,t}=\lambda_{\max,t}=\lambda_{0}
and the inequality
\lambda_{0}\geq\sqrt{2\lambda_{0}}
holds since
\lambda_{0}\geq 2
.
Then at time step
t\geq 1
we can express
V_{t}
as

\displaystyle V_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{t,\min}&0\\
0&\lambda_{t,\max}\end{pmatrix},

(4.70)

using the basis
\{v_{t,\min},v_{t,\max}\}
. Then we assume that the inequality
\lambda_{t,\min}\geq\sqrt{2\lambda_{\max,t}}
is satisfied and we want to check the same inequality at time step
t+1
. We express the vectors that update
V_{t+1}
(
4.65
) on the same basis as

\displaystyle a^{+}_{t+1}=\begin{pmatrix}\langle a^{+}_{t+1},v_{t,\min}\rangle\\
\langle a^{+}_{t+1},v_{t,\max}\rangle\end{pmatrix}\quad a^{-}_{t+1}=\begin{pmatrix}\langle a^{-}_{t+1},v_{t,\min}\rangle\\
\langle a^{-}_{t+1},v_{t,\max}\rangle\end{pmatrix},

(4.71)

and define the following quantities,

\displaystyle x_{t}

\displaystyle:=\langle a^{+}_{t+1},v_{t,\min}\rangle^{2}+\langle a^{-}_{t+1},v_{t,\min}\rangle^{2},

(4.72)

\displaystyle y_{t}

\displaystyle:=\langle a^{+}_{t+1},v_{t,\max}\rangle^{2}+\langle a^{-}_{t+1},v_{t,\max}\rangle^{2},

(4.73)

\displaystyle z_{t}

\displaystyle:=\langle a^{+}_{t+1},v_{t,\min}\rangle\langle a^{+}_{t+1},v_{t,\max}\rangle+\langle a^{-}_{t+1},v_{t,\min}\rangle\langle a^{-}_{t+1},v_{t,\max}\rangle.

(4.74)

Using that
a^{+}_{t+1},a^{-}_{t+1}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
we get the following relation

\displaystyle y_{t}=2-x_{t}.

(4.75)

Thus the perturbation at time step
t+1
can be written as

\displaystyle a^{+}_{t+1}(a^{+}_{t+1})^{\mathsf{T}}+a^{-}_{t+1}(a^{-}_{t+1})^{\mathsf{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{t}&z_{t}\\
z_{t}&y_{t}\end{pmatrix},

(4.76)

and
V_{t+1}
as

\displaystyle V_{t+1}=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{t,\min}+\omega(V_{t})x_{t}&\omega(V_{t})z_{t}\\
\omega(V_{t})z_{t}&\lambda_{t,\max}+\omega(V_{t})(2-x_{t})\end{pmatrix}.

(4.77)

In order to analyze the eigenvalue of
V_{t+1}
we have to control the overlap
\langle v_{t,\min},c_{t}\rangle
, so we define the following variable

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}:=\langle v_{t,\min},c_{t}\rangle\in[-1,1],

(4.78)

and using that

\displaystyle\langle v_{t,\min},a^{+}_{t+1}\rangle=\frac{\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lmin}}}{\sqrt{1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lmin}}\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\lmin}}},\quad\langle v_{t,\min},a^{-}_{t+1}\rangle=\frac{\alpha_{t}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lmin}}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lmin}}\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\lmin}}}

(4.79)

we can express
x_{t},z_{t}
in terms of
\alpha_{t}
as

\displaystyle x_{t}(\alpha_{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\big(\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lmin}}\big)^{2}}{1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lmin}}\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\lmin}}+\frac{\big(\alpha_{t}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lmin}}\big)^{2}}{1-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lmin}}\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\lmin}},

(4.80)

\displaystyle z_{t}(\alpha_{t})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lmin}}}{1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lmin}}\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\lmin}}+\frac{\alpha_{t}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lmin}}}{1-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lmin}}\alpha_{t}+\frac{1}{\lmin}}\right)\sqrt{1-\alpha_{t}^{2}}.

(4.81)

Then we can directly compute the minimum eigenvalue of
V_{t+1}
as

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t+1}(\alpha_{t})=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{\max,t}+\lmin}{2}+\omega(V_{t})

(4.82)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\lambda_{\max,t}-\lambda_{\min,t}+2\omega(V_{t})(1-x_{t}(\alpha_{t})))^{2}+4\omega^{2}(V_{t})z_{t}^{2}(\alpha_{t})}.

(4.83)

Now we define the difference of eigenvalues at time step
t
,

\displaystyle\phi_{t}:=\lambda_{\max,t}-\lambda_{\min,t},

(4.84)

and analyze two different regimes for the induction to hold at time step
t+1
.

Case 1
:
\phi_{t}\geq 2\omega(V_{t})

For this case we want to justify that
\lambda_{\min,t+1}(\alpha_{t})\geq\lambda_{\min,t+1}(0)
for
\alpha_{t}\in[-1,1]
, and later prove the induction using this lower bound. Defining the following

\displaystyle f(\alpha_{t})

\displaystyle:=(\phi_{t}+2\omega(V_{t})(1-x(\alpha_{t})))^{2}+4\omega^{2}(V_{t})z^{2}(\alpha_{t})

(4.85)

\displaystyle=\phi^{2}_{t}+4\omega(V_{t})\big(\phi_{t}(1-x(\alpha_{t}))+\omega(V_{t})(1-x(\alpha_{t}))^{2}+\omega(V_{t})z^{2}(\alpha_{t})\big),

(4.86)

and comparing with the exact minimum eigenvalue (
4.82
) we see that it suffices to prove that
f(\alpha_{t})
achieves a maximum at
\alpha_{t}=0
in the range
\alpha_{t}\in[-1,1]
in order to have
\lambda_{\min,t+1}(\alpha_{t})\geq\lambda_{\min,t+1}(0)
for all
\alpha_{t}\in[-1,1]
. A direct computation shows that

\displaystyle f(\alpha_{t})=\phi_{t}^{2}+4\omega(V_{t})g(\alpha_{t}),

(4.87)

\displaystyle g(\alpha_{t}):=\frac{2\big(\lmin-1\big)\lmin\phi_{t}\alpha_{t}^{2}+(1-\lmin^{2})\phi_{t}-(1-\lmin)^{2}\omega(V_{t})}{4\lmin\alpha_{t}^{2}-\big(1+\lmin\big)^{2}}.

(4.88)

We have that
g(\alpha_{t})
is of the form
g(x)=\frac{ax^{2}+b}{cx^{2}+d}
which has an unique maximum at
x=0
if
ad-bc<0
. Then identifying the coefficients we have to check that

\displaystyle p:=2(1-\lmin)\lmin(1+\lmin)^{2}\phi_{t}-4\lmin(1-\lmin^{2})\phi_{t}+4\lmin(1-\lmin)^{2}\omega(V_{t})<0.

(4.89)

Using that
\lambda_{\min,t}\geq 2
and
\omega(V_{t})\leq\frac{\phi_{t}}{2}
we can bound the above as

\displaystyle p\leq\phi_{t}\left(2(1-\lmin)\lmin(1+\lmin)^{2}-4\lmin(1-\lmin^{2})+2\lmin(1-\lmin)^{2}\right).

(4.90)

Finally summing all the terms we get,

\displaystyle p\leq-2\phi_{t}\left(\lambda_{\min,t}^{2}(1-\lambda_{\min,t})^{2}\right)\leq 0,

(4.91)

where the inequality follows from
\phi_{t}\geq 0
. Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t+1}(\alpha_{t})\geq\lambda_{\min,t+1}(0).

(4.92)

Using that

\displaystyle x_{t}(0)=\frac{2}{1+\lambda_{\min,t}},\quad z_{t}(0)=0,

(4.93)

and
\phi_{t}\geq 2\omega(V_{t})
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t+1}(0)=\lambda_{\min,t}+\frac{2\omega(V_{t})}{1+\lambda_{\min,t}}.

(4.94)

Thus, using (
4.92
)(
4.94
) and

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{t+1})=\lambda_{\min,t+1}(\alpha_{t})+\lambda_{\max,t+1}(\alpha_{t})=\lambda_{\max,t}+\lambda_{\min,t}+2\omega(V_{t}),

(4.95)

we can upper bound the maximum eigenvalue as

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,t+1}(\alpha_{t})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\max,t}+\lambda_{\min,t}-\lambda_{\min,t+1}(\alpha_{t})+2\omega(V_{t})

(4.96)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max,t}+\lambda_{\min,t}-\lambda_{\min,t+1}(0)+2\omega(V_{t})

(4.97)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\max,t}+2\omega(V_{t})\frac{\lambda_{\min,t}}{1+\lambda_{\min,t}}.

(4.98)

Finally in order to check the induction step at
t+1
,
\lambda_{\min,t+1}\geq\sqrt{2\lambda_{\max,t+1}}
we can use the bounds (
4.92
)(
4.96
) and it suffices to check

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t}+\frac{2\omega(V_{t})}{1+\lambda_{\min,t}}\geq\sqrt{2\left(\lambda_{\max,t}+2\omega(V_{t})\frac{\lambda_{\min,t}}{1+\lambda_{\min,t}}\right)}.

(4.99)

Squaring both sides and rearranging we obtain

\displaystyle\underbrace{\lambda^{2}_{\min,t}-2\lambda_{\max,t}}_{(i)}+\underbrace{\frac{4\omega^{2}(V_{t})}{(1+\lambda_{\min,t})^{2}}}_{(ii)}\geq 0,

(4.100)

where
(i)
is positive because we assume
\lambda_{\min,t}\geq\sqrt{2\lambda_{\max,t}}
to hold at time step
t
and
(ii)
is always positive. This concludes the case
\phi_{t}\geq 2\omega(V_{t})
induction.

Case 2
:
\phi_{t}\leq 2\omega(V_{t})

From the definition of
\lambda_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{0}\geq 4\sqrt{2}C+2.

(4.101)

Multiplying both sides by
\lambda_{\min,t}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq 4\sqrt{2}C\lambda_{\min,t}+2\lambda_{\min,t}

(4.102)

\displaystyle\geq 2\left(4C\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,t}}+\lambda_{\min,t}\right)\hskip 56.9055pt

(4.103)

\displaystyle\geq 2\left(\lambda_{\min,t}+4\omega(V_{t})\right)

(4.104)

\displaystyle\geq 2\left(\lambda_{\min,t}+\phi_{t}+2\omega(V_{t})\right)

(4.105)

\displaystyle=2\left(\lambda_{\max,t}+2\omega(V_{t})\right),

(4.106)

where in the second inequality we used the induction hypothesis
\lambda_{\min,t}\geq\sqrt{2\lambda_{\max,t}}
, the third inequality
\omega(V_{t})\leq C\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,t}}
, the fourth inequality
\phi_{t}\leq 2\omega(V_{t})
and the last equality the definition
\phi_{t}=\lambda_{\max,t}-\lambda_{\min,t}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t}\geq\sqrt{2(\lambda_{\max,t}+2\omega(V_{t}))},

(4.107)

and the inequality at time step
t+1
,
\lambda_{\min,t}\geq\sqrt{2\lambda_{\max,t+1}}
follows from the bounds

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,t+1}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\min,t},

(4.108)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,t+1}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{\max,t}+2\omega(V_{t}),

(4.109)

where we used (
4.69
) with
\lambda_{\min}(a_{t}a_{t}^{\mathsf{T}})=\lambda_{\min}(b_{t}b_{t}^{\mathsf{T}})=0
and
\lambda_{\max}(a_{t}a_{t}^{\mathsf{T}})=\lambda_{\max}(b_{t}b_{t}^{\mathsf{T}})=1
.

4.4.2
LinUCB
on the circle without elliptical potential lemma

Before presenting the full algorithm, we illustrate how the previously described action selection rule works and how it can already achieve sublinear regret. We focus on the circle example where the action set is
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{1}
and the unknown parameter is
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
, without making further assumptions on the noise model beyond a bounded subgaussian parameter. In Theorem
4.4.1
we will set the weights
\omega(X)=1
. Thus, we will will not weight the design matrix and work with the usual least squares estimator (
4.8
). The algorithm operates in batches of 2 actions, with a total of
\tilde{T}
batches, leading to a time horizon of
T=2\tilde{T}
. At each batch
\tilde{t}\in[\tilde{T}]
, the algorithm selects the actions
A^{+}_{\tilde{t}}
and
A^{-}_{\tilde{t}}
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t}}:=\frac{\tilde{A}^{\pm}_{\tilde{t}}}{\|\tilde{A}^{\pm}_{\tilde{t}}\|},\quad\tilde{A}^{\pm}_{\tilde{t}}:=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}}{\|\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}\|_{2}}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}}v_{\tilde{t}-1}

(4.110)

where
v_{\tilde{t}-1}
is the normalized eigenvector of minimum eigenvalue
\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})
of the design matrix

\displaystyle V_{\tilde{t}}=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{s=1}^{\tilde{t}}\left(A^{+}_{s}(A^{+}_{s})^{\mathsf{T}}+A^{-}_{s}(A^{-}_{s})^{\mathsf{T}}\right),

(4.111)

and the least squares estimator is

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}}=V^{-1}_{\tilde{t}}\sum_{s=1}^{\tilde{t}}\left(X_{s}^{+}A_{s}^{+}+X_{s}^{-}A_{s}^{-}\right),

(4.112)

where
X_{s}^{\pm}
are the rewards sampled for actions
A^{\pm}_{s}
. The pseudocode can be found in Algorithm
5
. In order to prove the regret bound we are going to use the confidence region of
LinUCB
stated in Theorem
4.1.3
, which in our case will have the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}}=\{\theta^{{}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\|\theta^{\prime}-\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}}\|^{2}_{V_{\tilde{t}}}\leq\beta_{\tilde{t},\delta}\},

(4.113)

with

\displaystyle\beta_{\tilde{t},\delta}=\left(1+\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{\delta}+2\log\left(1+\tilde{t}\right)}\right)^{2}.

(4.114)

Algorithm 5

LinUCB
on
\mathbb{S}^{1}

1:

Set initial design matrix
V_{0}\leftarrow\mathbb{I}_{2\times 2}

2:

Choose initial estimator
\hat{\theta}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
for
\theta
at random

3:

for

\tilde{t}=1,2,\cdots

do

4:

Select actions
A_{\tilde{t}}^{+}
and
A_{\tilde{t}}^{-}
according to Eq. (
4.110
)

5:

Receive associated rewards
X^{+}_{\tilde{t}}
and
X^{-}_{\tilde{t}}

Update design matrix and LSE

6:

V_{\tilde{t}}\leftarrow V_{\tilde{t}-1}+A^{+}_{\tilde{t}}(A^{+}_{\tilde{t}})^{\mathsf{T}}+A^{-}_{\tilde{t}}(A^{-}_{\tilde{t}})^{\mathsf{T}}

7:

\widetilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{w}}\leftarrow V^{-1}_{\tilde{t}}\sum_{s=1}^{\tilde{t}}\left(X_{s}^{+}A_{s}^{+}+X_{s}^{-}A_{s}^{-}\right)

8:

end

for

Before proving the regret scaling we state a Lemma that will quantify how far are the actions from the environment.

Lemma 4.4.3
.

Given two normalized vectors
c,v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, a positive constant
\lambda>1
and the following vectors

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}^{\pm}=c\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}v,\quad{a}^{\pm}=\frac{\widetilde{a}^{\pm}}{\|\widetilde{a}^{\pm}\|_{2}}.

(4.115)

Then we have

\displaystyle\|{a}^{\pm}-c\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{\lambda}.

(4.116)

Proof 4.4.4
.

We are going to give the proof for
{a}^{-}
. The one for
{a}^{+}
follows from an identical calculation.

First, we can relate the distance to the inner product of the vectors as

\displaystyle\|{a}^{-}-c\|_{2}^{2}=\langle{a}^{-}-c,{a}^{-}-c\rangle=2-2\langle{a}^{-},c\rangle.

(4.117)

Using the normalization factor is

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{a}^{-}\|_{2}^{2}=1-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\langle c,v\rangle+\frac{1}{\lambda},

(4.118)

then

\displaystyle\langle{a}^{-},c\rangle=\frac{1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha}{\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha}},\quad\text{where}\quad\alpha:=\langle c,v\rangle\in[-1,1].

(4.119)

In order to study the behavior of (
4.117
) in terms of the overlap
\langle c,v\rangle
we define

\displaystyle f(\alpha,\lambda):=2\left(1-\frac{1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha}{\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha}}\right)

(4.120)

and we are going to check the maximum in the range
\alpha\in[-1,1]
. Note that
f(\alpha,\lambda)=\|{a}^{-}-c\|_{2}^{2}
.
Taking the derivative respect to
\alpha
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f(\alpha,\lambda)}{\partial\alpha}=2\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha}-\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha}}}{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha}

(4.121)

Then we can find the extremal points of
f(\alpha)
as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f(\alpha,\lambda)}{\partial\alpha}

\displaystyle=0\Leftrightarrow 1+\frac{1}{\lambda}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha=1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\alpha

(4.122)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\alpha=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}

(4.123)

Using that
\lambda>1
we have the following inequalites

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f(\alpha,\lambda)}{\partial\alpha}\bigg|_{\alpha=1}=-\frac{2}{\lambda\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}\right)^{2}}<0,\quad\frac{\partial f(\alpha,\lambda)}{\partial\alpha}\bigg|_{\alpha=0}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda}\sqrt{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}}}\left(1-\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)>0.

(4.124)

Thus,
\alpha=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}
(
4.120
) is a maximum and it is achieved at

\displaystyle f\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}},\lambda\right)=2\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right).

(4.125)

Using the original definition of
f(\alpha,\lambda)
we can conclude that

\displaystyle\|{a}^{-}-c\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{\lambda}}\right)\leq\frac{2}{\lambda},

(4.126)

where the last inequality follows from
\lambda>1
, and
1-x\leq\sqrt{1-x}
for
0\leq x<1
.

This above Lemma actually holds for any dimension and we will also apply it when we study higher dimensional cases. We are now ready to prove a Theorem showing that selecting actions by projecting the extremal points of the principal axis of
\mathcal{C}_{\ttilde}
onto the circle is enough to achieve the same regret bound as
LinUCB
. The initial part of the proof will also be useful for later Theorems when we upper bound the instantaneous regret in the full setting with vanishing noise.

Theorem 4.4.5
.

Let
T=2\tilde{T}
for
\tilde{T}\in\mathbb{N}
. Then if we apply Algorithm
5
to a linear bandit with action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{1}
and reward model
X_{t}=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle+\epsilon_{t}
where
\epsilon_{1}
is 1-subgaussian we have that with probability at least
1-\delta

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\theta,\pi)\leq\frac{9\sqrt{2}}{4}\sqrt{T}\left(1+\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{\delta}+2\log\left(1+2T\right)}\right)^{2}.

(4.127)

Proof 4.4.6
.

First we note that since
\theta,A_{t}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
we can express the regret as

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\theta,\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\theta-A_{t}\|_{2}^{2}

(4.128)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\tilde{t}=1}^{\tilde{T}}\left(\|\theta-A^{+}_{\tilde{t}}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\theta-A^{-}_{\tilde{t}}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(4.129)

For now, we will assume that
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}}
holds for all
\tilde{t}\in[\tilde{T}]
where
\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}}
has the form (
4.113
).

Then from triangle inequality we have

\displaystyle\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}\|_{2}+\|\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}-\frac{\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}}{\|\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}\|_{2}}\|_{2}+\|\frac{\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}}{\|\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}\|_{2}}-A^{\pm}_{t}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{\tilde{t}-1,\delta}}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}}}_{(i)}+\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{\tilde{t}-1,\delta}}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}}}_{(ii)}+\underbrace{\sqrt{\frac{2}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}}}_{(iii)}

\displaystyle\leq 3\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{\tilde{t}-1,\delta}}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}},

(4.130)

where for the above bounds we use:

•

(i).
Since
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{t-1}
, by definition of
\mathcal{C}_{t-1}
(
4.113
) we have
\|\theta-\tilde{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{t-1}\|_{V_{t-1}}\leq\sqrt{\beta_{t-1,\delta}}
and then the inequality follows from
\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})\mathbb{I}_{2\times 2}\leq V_{\tilde{t}-1}
.

•

(ii).
Since
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}-1}
and
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
this implies that
\mathbb{B}^{2}_{r}(\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1})\cap\mathbb{S}^{1}
is non-empty with
r=\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{\tilde{t}-1,\delta}}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}}
(the longest axis of the ellipsoid). Then using that
\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}-1}\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{2}_{r}(\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1})
and
\frac{\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}}{\|\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}\|_{2}}
is the normalized vector of
\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}
which is the center of
\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}-1}
we get
\frac{\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}}{\|\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}\|_{2}}\in\mathbb{B}^{2}_{r}(\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1})
.

•

For
(iii).
We apply Lemma
4.4.3
using the expression of
A^{\pm}_{t}
(
4.110
) with
c=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}}{\|\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}\|_{2}}
and
\lambda=\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})
.

We sketch this bounds in Figure
4.4
.

\begin{overpic}[figures/cartoon_regret_distances.png]
\put(6.0,34.0){ $A^{+}_{\tilde{t}}$}
\put(78.0,12.0){ $A^{-}_{\tilde{t}}$}
\put(67.0,25.0){ $\theta$}
\put(54.0,46.0){ $\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}-1}$}
\put(46.0,32.0){ $\frac{\hat{{\theta}}_{\tilde{t}-1}}{\|{\hat{{\theta}}_{\tilde{t}-1}\|_{2}}}$ }
\put(52.0,63.0){\rotatebox{70.0}{\small$r=\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{t-1,\delta}}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}}$}}
\put(67.0,65.0){ $\mathbb{B}_{r}^{2}(\hat{{\theta}}_{\tilde{t}-1})$}
\put(35.0,55.0){ $\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}-1}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 4.4
:
Sketch for the triangle inequality used to bound
\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t}}\|_{2}
in (
4.4.6
). The red lines represent the distances
(i),(ii)
and
(iii)
. Under the event
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}-1}
we can use
\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}-1}\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{r}^{2}(\hat{{\theta}}_{\tilde{t}-1})
with
r
being the longest axis of the ellipsoid and bound all distances by the diameter
2r
.

To finalize the proof we need to study the eigenvalues of
V_{\tilde{t}}
. From the definition of
V_{\tilde{t}}
we have

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{\tilde{t}})=2+2\tilde{t}\Rightarrow\lambda_{\max}(V_{\tilde{t}})\geq\tilde{t},

(4.131)

where we used that
A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t}}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Then using Theorem
4.4.1
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}})\geq\sqrt{2\lambda_{\max}(V_{\tilde{t}})}\geq\sqrt{2\tilde{t}}

(4.132)

Thus combining the above bound with (
4.4.6
) and
\beta_{\tilde{t},\delta}
is an increasing sequence in
\tilde{t}
leads to

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{1},\theta,\pi)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9\beta_{\tilde{T},\delta}}{2}\sum_{\tilde{t}=1}^{\tilde{T}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{t}}}\leq\frac{9\beta_{\tilde{T},\delta}}{2}\sqrt{\tilde{T}},

(4.133)

and the result follows using
T=2\tilde{T}
, and applying the probabilistic bound from Theorem
4.1.3
.

4.4.3
General case

In the previous section, we saw how the action selection rule described in Section
4.4.1
can be used to minimize the regret. However, so far this rule only works for
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. In this section, we generalize the same rule to work on any sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. Instead of proving the ratio
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})=\Omega\left(\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(V_{t})}\right)
by induction and directly computing the eigenvalues, we will use matrix perturbation bounds. Specifically, we will use a proof technique based on the following minimax characterization of eigenvalues for Hermitian matrices.

Corollary 4.4.7

( Corollary III.1.2 in
[
22
]
)
.

Let
A\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times d}
be a Hermitian matrix, then

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(A)=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}\\
\dim(\mathcal{M})=d-k+1\end{subarray}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{M}\\
\|x\|_{2}=1\end{subarray}}\langle x,Ax\rangle=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{C}^{d}\\
\dim(\mathcal{M})=k\end{subarray}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{M}\\
\|x\|_{2}=1\end{subarray}}\langle x,Ax\rangle.

(4.134)

In particular, if
A\geq B
then
\lambda_{k}(A)\geq\lambda_{k}(B)
.

The following Theorem is the generalization of Theorem
4.4.1
.

Theorem 4.4.8
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
\{c_{t}\}_{t=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
be a sequence of normalized vectors and
\omega:\textup{P}^{d}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
a function such that

\displaystyle\omega(X)\leq C\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(X)}\quad\forall X\in\textup{P}^{d}_{+},

(4.135)

for some constant
C>0
. Let
\lambda_{0}\geq\max\big\{2,\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}}2dC+\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\big\}
, and define a sequence of matrices

\{V_{t}\}_{t=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
as

\displaystyle V_{0}:=\lambda_{0}\mathbb{I}_{d\times d},\quad V_{t+1}:=V_{t}+\omega(V_{t})\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}P_{t,i},

(4.136)

where

\displaystyle P_{t,i}:=a^{+}_{t+1,i}(a^{+}_{t+1,i})^{\mathsf{T}}+a^{-}_{t+1,i}(a^{-}_{t+1,i})^{\mathsf{T}},\quad a^{\pm}_{t+1,i}:=\frac{\tilde{a}^{\pm}_{t+1,i}}{\|\tilde{a}^{\pm}_{t+1,i}\|_{2}},\quad\tilde{a}^{\pm}_{t+1,i}:=c_{t}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}}v_{t,i},

(4.137)

with
\lambda_{t,i}=\lambda_{i}(V_{t})
the eigenvalues of
V_{t}
with corresponding normalized eigenvectors

v_{t,1},...,v_{t,d}\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{\max}(V_{t})}\quad\text{for all}\quad t\geq 0.

(4.138)

Proof 4.4.9
.

We start giving an upper bound of the Euclidean norm of
\tilde{a}^{\pm}_{t+1,i}
(
4.137
) with the following calculation

\displaystyle\|\tilde{a}^{\pm}_{t+1,i}\|^{2}_{2}

\displaystyle=1\pm\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1,t}}}\langle c_{t},v_{t,i}\rangle+\frac{1}{\lambda_{t,1}}

(4.139)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{t,1}}=\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}}\right)^{2},

(4.140)

where we used that
c_{t},v_{t,i}\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
. Thus using the definition of
P_{t,i}
(
4.137
),

\displaystyle P_{t,i}\geq\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}}\right)^{-2}\big(\tilde{a}^{+}_{t+1,i}(\tilde{a}^{+}_{t+1,i})^{\mathsf{T}}+\tilde{a}^{-}_{t+1,i}(\tilde{a}^{-}_{t+1,i})^{\mathsf{T}}\big).

(4.141)

From the definition of
\tilde{a}^{\pm}_{t+1,i}
(
4.137
) we have

\displaystyle\tilde{a}^{\pm}_{t+1,i}(\tilde{a}^{\pm}_{t+1,i})^{\mathsf{T}}=c_{t}c_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{t,1}}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}}\left(c_{t}v_{t,i}^{\mathsf{T}}+v_{t,i}c_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\right).

(4.142)

The above allow us to bound
P_{t,i}
as

\displaystyle P_{t,i}

\displaystyle\geq 2\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}}\right)^{-2}\left(c_{t}c^{\mathsf{T}}_{t}+\frac{1}{\lambda_{t,1}}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i}\right)

(4.143)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i},

(4.144)

where we used
c_{t}c^{\mathsf{T}}_{t}\geq 0
and
\frac{1}{\lambda_{t,1}}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}}\right)^{-2}=(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,i}})^{-2}
. Thus, from the abound bound and the definition of
V_{t}
(
4.136
) we obtain the following matrix inequality

\displaystyle V_{t+1}\geq V_{t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i}.

(4.145)

We want to prove the result using induction. The case
t=0
is immediately satisfied since
\lambda_{0,d}=\lambda_{0,1}\geq\frac{2}{3(d-1)}
.
Now we will assume that

\displaystyle\lambda_{t,1}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{t,d}},

(4.146)

is satisfied and we want to prove the same inequality for
t+1
. We distinguish cases depending on the growth of the maximum eigenvalue of
V_{t}
.

Case 1
:
\lambda_{t,d}\geq\lambda_{t,d-1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}
.

Using the hypothesis
\lambda_{t,d}\geq\lambda_{t,d-1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}
, the fact that
V_{t}
diagonalizes in the
\{v_{t,i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
basis and the ordering
\lambda_{t,1}\leq....\leq\lambda_{t,d-1}\leq\lambda_{t,d}
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\left(V_{t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{t,i}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,...,d-1

(4.147)

\displaystyle\lambda_{d}\left(V_{t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{t,d}.

(4.148)

Then combining with the mini-max principle for eigenvalues
4.4.7
and using that both sides of (
4.145
) are real and symmetric, we arrive at

\displaystyle\lambda_{i,t+1}\geq\lambda_{i,t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,...,d-1.

(4.149)

From the expression for
V_{t+1}
(
4.136
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{t+1})=\big(\lambda_{t+1,d}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\lambda_{t+1,i}\big)=\lambda_{t,d}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\lambda_{t,i}+2(d-1)w(V_{t}).

(4.150)

Also from (
4.149
)

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{t+1})\geq\lambda_{t+1,d}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\lambda_{t,i}+\frac{2(d-1)w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}.

(4.151)

Combining the above we can bound the maximum eigenvalue as

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,d}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{t,d}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\lambda_{t,i}+2(d-1)w(V_{t})-\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\lambda_{t,i}+\frac{2(d-1)w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\right)

(4.152)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{d,t}+2(d-1)w(V_{t})\frac{\lambda_{t,1}+2\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}.

(4.153)

Recall that we want to check

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,1}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{t+1,d}}.

(4.154)

Using (
4.149
) and (
4.152
) we can square the above and see that it suffices to check

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{1,t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{d,t}+\frac{4w(V_{t})(\lambda_{t,1}+2\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})}{3(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}.

(4.155)

Multiplying out the terms, this is equivalent to the condition

\displaystyle\underbrace{\lambda^{2}_{1,t}-\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{d,t}}_{(i)}+\underbrace{\frac{4w^{2}(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{4}}}_{(ii)}+\underbrace{\frac{4w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}(\lambda_{t,1}-\frac{1}{3}(\lambda_{t,1}+2\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})}_{(iii)}\geq 0.

(4.156)

It remains to observe that
(i)
is positive by induction at time step
t
(cf. (
4.146
)),
(ii)
is always positive and
(iii)
is positive for
\lambda_{t,1}\geq 1
and this is true since
\lambda_{t,1}\geq\lambda_{0}\geq 2
,
\lambda_{t,1}
is non-decreasing in
t
and
f(x)=x-\sqrt{x}
is positive for
x\geq 1
.

Case 2.1
:
\lambda_{t,1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}<\lambda_{t,d}<\lambda_{t,d-1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}
.

First we find
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
1<k\leq d-1
such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{t,k-1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\leq\lambda_{t,d}\leq\lambda_{t,k}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}.

(4.157)

Then using that
\{v_{t,i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
are the eigenvectors of
V_{t}
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\left(V_{t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{t,i}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,...,k-1

(4.158)

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}\left(V_{t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{t,d}

(4.159)

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}\left(V_{t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}v_{t,i}v^{\mathsf{T}}_{t,i}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{t,i-1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\quad\text{for}\quad i=k+1,...,d.

(4.160)

Again using the mini-max principle for eigenvalues
4.4.7
and that both sides of (
4.145
) are real and symmetric

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,i}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{t,i}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\quad\text{if}\quad i\in\{1,...,k-1\},

(4.161)

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,i}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{t,d}\quad\text{if}\quad i=k,

(4.162)

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,i}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{t,i-1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\quad\text{if}\quad i\in\{k+1,...,d\}.

(4.163)

Thus, using the above inequalities we can bound the trace of
V_{t+1}
as

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\lambda_{t+1,i}+\lambda_{t+1,k}+\sum_{i=k+1}^{d-1}\lambda_{t+1,i}+\lambda_{t+1,d}

(4.164)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\lambda_{t,i}\right)+\frac{2(k-1)w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}+\lambda_{t,d}+\left(\sum_{i=k}^{d-2}\lambda_{t,i}\right)+\frac{2(d-k-1)w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}+\lambda_{t+1,d}

(4.165)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{t+1,d}+\lambda_{t,d}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-2}\lambda_{t,i}+\frac{2(d-2)w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}

(4.166)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{t+1,d}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\lambda_{t,i}+\frac{2(d-2)w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}},

(4.167)

where in the last bound we used simply
\lambda_{t,d}\geq\lambda_{t,d-1}
.
From the expression of
V_{t+1}
(
4.136
)

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{t+1})=\lambda_{t,d}+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\lambda_{t,i}+2(d-1)w(V_{t}),

(4.168)

and combining with the previous bound we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,d}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{d,t}+2(d-1)w(V_{t})-\frac{2(d-2)w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}

(4.169)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{d,t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\left(\lambda_{t,1}(d-1)+2\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}(d-1)+1\right)

(4.170)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{d,t}+\frac{2(d-1)w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\left(\lambda_{t,1}+2\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}+1\right).

(4.171)

Again to check

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,1}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{t+1,d}},

(4.172)

we can square both sides and using the above bounds on
\lambda_{t+1,1}
and
\lambda_{t+1,d}
it suffices to check

\displaystyle\left(\lambda_{1,t}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{d,t}+\frac{4w(V_{t})(\lambda_{t,1}+2\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}+1)}{3(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}.

(4.173)

Multiplying out the terms, this is equivalent to the condition

\displaystyle\underbrace{\lambda^{2}_{1,t}-\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{d,t}}_{(i)}+\underbrace{\frac{4w^{2}(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{4}}}_{(ii)}+\underbrace{\frac{4w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}(\lambda_{t,1}-\frac{1}{3}(\lambda_{t,1}+2\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}}+1)}_{(iii)}\geq 0.

(4.174)

Finally, we observe that
(i)
is positive by induction at time step
t
,
(ii)
is always positive and
(iii)
is positive since
\lambda_{t,1}\geq 2
,
\lambda_{t,1}\geq 2
is non-decreasing and
f(x)=2x-2\sqrt{x}-1
is positive for
x\geq 2
.

Case 2.2
:
\lambda_{t,d}\leq\lambda_{t,1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}
.

From the statement of the theorem we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{t,1}\geq\lambda_{0}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}}2dC+\frac{2}{3(d-1)}.

(4.175)

Multiplying both sides by
\lambda_{t,1}
we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}_{t,1}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}}2dC\lambda_{t,1}+\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{t,1}

(4.176)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\left(2dC\sqrt{\lambda_{t,d}}+\lambda_{t,1}\right)

(4.177)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\left(2dw(V_{t})+\lambda_{t,1}\right)

(4.178)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\left(2(d-1)w(V_{t})+2w(V_{t})+\lambda_{t,1}\right)

(4.179)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\left(2(d-1)w(V_{t})+\lambda_{t,d}\right),

(4.180)

where the second inequality follows from the induction hypothesis
\lambda_{t,1}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{t,d}}
, the third inequality from
w(V_{t})\leq C\sqrt{\lambda_{t,d}}
and the fourth inequality from the assumption
\lambda_{t,d}\leq\lambda_{t,1}+\frac{2w(V_{t})}{(1+\sqrt{\lambda_{t,1}})^{2}}\leq\lambda_{t,1}+2w(V_{t})
. Thus taking the square root in both sides we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{t,1}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\left(\lambda_{t,d}+2(d-1)w(V_{t})\right)},

(4.181)

and the induction at time step
t+1
,
\lambda_{t+1,1}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{t+1,d}}
follows from the bounds

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,1}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{t,1},

(4.182)

\displaystyle\lambda_{t+1,d}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{t,d}+2w(V_{t})(d-1),

(4.183)

where we used the inequalities (
4.69
) and the definition of
V_{t}
(
4.136
).

4.5
Linear bandits with polylogarithmic minimax regret

Inspired by the shot noise in the PSMAQB setting, we now study a class of noise-dependent linear stochastic bandits which, by combining the previously developed tools, will allow us to achieve polylogarithmic regret in the time horizon.

4.5.1
Linear bandits with linearly vanishing subgaussian parameter

In this Section, we combine the weighted least squares estimator defined in (
4.57
) with the action selection rule developed in Theorems
4.4.1
and
4.4.8
to achieve polylogarithmic regret for a linear bandit with vanishing noise. Before tackling the variance condition (
4.52
) in the PSMAQB setting, we introduce a related model. We study a linear bandit with action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, unknown parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and reward model

\displaystyle X_{t}=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle+\epsilon_{t},

(4.184)

where
\epsilon_{t}
is conditionally
\eta_{t}
-subgaussian and satisfies

\displaystyle\eta^{2}_{t}\leq 1-\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle^{2}.

(4.185)

This noise model is inspired by the variance expression from the PSMAQB setting (
4.52
) in the qubit Bloch sphere, replacing the variance with the subgaussian parameter. Note that the noise decreases linearly as actions get closer to
\theta
, since
\eta_{t}\sim 1-\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle
. The extra factor
(1+\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle)
is not needed to achieve polylogarithmic regret, but we keep it to better match the behavior of the quantum model. We also note that solving this model does not directly solve the PSMAQB setting, as the subgaussian parameter is strictly smaller than the variance. However, assumption simplifies the algorithm and helps illustrate how polylogarithmic regret can be achieved.

Weighted confidence region

Before proving a regret bound we need to derive a confidence region based on the weighted least squares estimator (
4.57
). Our proof of is an adaptation from the one presented in
[
67
]
[Chapter 20] to our setting. We refer to the reference for detailed computations. The proof is based on supermartingles properties specifically the following Theorem.

Theorem 4.5.1

(Theorem 3.9 in
[
67
]
)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t=0}^{\infty}
be a supermartingale with
X_{t}\geq 0
almost surely for all
t
. Then for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left(\sup_{t}X_{t}\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\frac{\EX\left[X_{0}\right]}{\epsilon}.

(4.186)

Lemma 4.5.2
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
(A_{t})^{\infty}_{t=1}
be the actions selected by some policy with corresponding rewards
(X_{t})^{\infty}_{t=1}
given by
X_{t}=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle+\epsilon_{t}
, where
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is the unknown parameter and
\epsilon_{t}
is
\eta_{t}
-subgaussian. Let
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}
be an estimator of the form (
4.55
) and define the following event,

\displaystyle G_{t}:=\left\{\big((X_{s},A_{s})_{s=1}^{t-1},A_{t}\big):\sigma_{s}\leq\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A_{1},X_{1},\ldots,A_{s-1},X_{s-1},A_{s})\ \forall{s\in[t]}\right\}.

(4.187)

Then we can define the following confidence region

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\textup{w}}_{t}:=\{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\theta^{\prime}-\hat{\theta}^{\textup{w}}_{t}\|^{2}_{V_{t}(\lambda)}\leq\beta_{t,\delta}\},

(4.188)

where
V_{t}(\lambda)
is the weighted design matrix (
4.58
) and
\theta^{\textup{w}}_{t}
the weighted least squares estimator (
4.57
) both defined with with
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}
and

\displaystyle\beta_{t,\delta}=\left(\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{\delta}+\log\left(\frac{\det(V_{t}(\lambda))}{\det(V_{0}(\lambda))}\right)}+\sqrt{\lambda}\right)^{2}.

(4.189)

Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left[\forall s\in[t]:\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\textup{{w}}}\big|G_{t}\right]\geq 1-\delta.

(4.190)

Proof 4.5.3
.

To simplify notation we use
\hat{\sigma}_{s}^{2}=\hat{\sigma}_{s}^{2}(A_{s})
. From now we will condition all our calculations on the events
G_{t}
since we want to prove (
4.12
). First define the following random quantity

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{t}:=\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{s}^{2}}\epsilon_{s}A_{s},

(4.191)

and define the following process

\displaystyle\widetilde{M}_{t}(x):=\exp\left(\langle x,\widetilde{S}_{t}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|x\|^{2}_{{V}_{t}}\right),

(4.192)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
V_{t}=V_{t}(0)
. We want to check that
\widetilde{M}_{t}(x)
is a supermartingale, i.e
\EX\left[\widetilde{M}_{t}(x)|\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right]\leq\widetilde{M}_{t-1}(x)
where
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
is the history for all information up to time step
t
before reward
X_{t}
is observed (also contains
A_{t}
). From a direct calculation, we have

\displaystyle\EX\left[\widetilde{M}_{t}(x)|\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right]=\widetilde{M}_{t-1}(x)\EX\left[\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon_{t}}{\hat{\sigma}_{t}}\left\langle x,\frac{A_{t}}{\hat{\sigma}_{t}}\right\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}}A_{t}A^{\top}_{t}}\right)\big|\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\right].

(4.193)

Then, using that in the definition of
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}
(
4.55
) is defined only using the information up to time step
t-1
, the subgaussian property, and that the event
G_{t}
holds we have

\displaystyle\EX\left[\exp\left(\frac{\epsilon_{t}}{\hat{\sigma}_{t}}\left\langle x,\frac{A_{t}}{\hat{\sigma}_{t}}\right\rangle\right)\big|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\left\langle x,\frac{A_{t}}{\hat{\sigma}_{t}}\right\rangle^{2}\right)=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}}A_{t}A^{\top}_{t}}\right).

(4.194)

Inserting the above expression into (
4.193
) we immediately get
\EX\left[\widetilde{M}_{t}(x)|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\leq\widetilde{M}_{t-1}(x)
. Using Lemma 20.3 in
[
67
]
[Chapter 20] we have that

\displaystyle\bar{M}_{t}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{M}_{t}(x)dh(x)

(4.195)

is a supermartingale where
h
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular we choose
h=\mathcal{N}(0,H^{-1})
with
H=\lambda\mathbb{I}_{d\times d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and we get

\displaystyle\bar{M}_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{(2\pi)^{d}\det(H^{-1})}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(\langle x,\widetilde{S}_{t}\rangle-\frac{1}{2}\|x\|^{2}_{V_{t}}-\frac{1}{2}\|x\|_{H}\right)dx.

(4.196)

The above quantity can be exactly computed as

\displaystyle\bar{M}_{t}=\left(\frac{\det(V_{0})}{\det(V_{t}(\lambda))}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\widetilde{S}_{t}\|^{2}_{V^{-1}_{t}(\lambda)}\right),

(4.197)

where we used the solution for a Gaussian integral, we have completed the square inside the exponential term and we used that
V_{t}(\lambda)=H+V_{t}
. Then we can use Theorem
4.5.1
in order to get

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left(\sup_{t}\log\left(\bar{M}_{t}\right)\geq\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)=\mathrm{Pr}\left(\sup_{t}\bar{M}_{t}\geq\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\leq\delta,

(4.198)

where we used that by definition of
\widetilde{M}_{t-1}(x)
we have
\bar{M}_{t}\geq 0
almost surely and
\bar{M}_{0}=1
. Inserting (
4.197
) into the above equation we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left(\sup_{t}\|\widetilde{S}_{t}\|^{2}_{V^{-1}_{t}(\lambda)}\geq 2\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)+\log\frac{\det(V_{t}(\lambda))}{\det(V_{0}(\lambda))}\right)\leq\delta.

(4.199)

Finally using the expression for the weighted least squares estimator (
4.57
) we have

\displaystyle\|\hat{\theta}^{\text{wls}}_{t}-\theta\|_{V_{t}(\lambda)}\leq\|\widetilde{S}_{t}\|_{V^{-1}_{t}(\lambda)}+\sqrt{\lambda},

(4.200)

where we used triangle inequality and
\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}=1
. And the result follows by combining the above expression with (
4.199
) and conditioning under the event
G_{t}
.

LinUCB
vanishing noise

In this Section, we give the specific algorithm that minimizes the regret for the stochastic linear bandits with linear vanishing noise (
4.185
). The algorithm is based on the principle of “optimism in the face of uncertainty” (OFU) or upper confidence bounds (UCB). We name the algorithm
LinUCB-VN
, where VN stands for vanishing noise. The
LinUCB-VN
algorithm is designed to keep the relation
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})=\Omega(\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(V_{t})})
at each time step
t
following the general action selection rule from Theorem
4.4.8
combined with the weighted least squares estimator (
4.57
).

Figure 4.5:

We numerically test
LinUCB
and
LinUCB-VN
in a linear bandit with action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{1}
and reward model
X_{t}=\mathcal{N}(\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle,1-\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle^{2})
. Each point in the graphic is run independently and averaged over 100 instances for random environments
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
Left plot:
Scaling of the regret for
LinUCB
algorithm and
LinUCB-VN
. We fit the functions
R(t)=1.86\log^{2}t
for
LinUCB-VN
and
R(t)=0.88\sqrt{t\log t}
for
LinUCB
.

Right plots:
Scaling of the maximum and minimum eigenvalue of the matrix
V_{t}
for
LinUCB-VN
. The scaling shows the relation
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})=\Omega(\sqrt{V_{t}(\lambda_{\max}}))
. We fit the function
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})=0.2059t
for the minimum eigenvalue and
\lambda_{\max}(V_{t})=0.0012t^{2}
for the maximum eigenvalue. The behavior
\lambda_{\min}(V_{t})=\Theta(t)
is the one that gives us the theoretical guarantee of polylogarithmic scaling of the regret.

Our algorithm works in batches of
2(d-1)
actions for a total of
\tilde{T}\in\mathbb{N}
batches or
T=2(d-1)\tilde{T}
rounds. At each batch
\tilde{t}\in[\tilde{T}]
the algorithm selects the following actions,

\displaystyle A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}:=\frac{\widetilde{A}^{\pm}_{\ttilde,i}}{\|\widetilde{A}^{\pm}_{\ttilde,i}\|_{2}},\quad\textrm{where}\quad\widetilde{A}^{\pm}_{\ttilde,i}:=\frac{\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{\tilde{t}}}{\|\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{\tilde{t}}\|_{2}}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\tilde{t}-1,1}}}v_{\tilde{t}-1,i},

(4.201)

for
i\in[d-1]
, where
v_{\tilde{t}-1,i}
is the normalized eigenvector with eigenvalue
\lambda_{\tilde{t}-1,i}=\lambda_{i}(V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda))
of the weighted design matrix
V_{\tilde{t}}(\lambda)
(
4.58
) and
\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{\tilde{t}}
the weighted estimator (
4.57
). The design matrix
V_{\tilde{t}}(\lambda)
is updated at each batch
\tilde{t}
as

\displaystyle V_{\tilde{t}}(\lambda):=V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda)+\omega(V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda))\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(A^{+}_{\tilde{t},i}(A^{+}_{\tilde{t},i})^{\mathsf{T}}+A^{-}_{\tilde{t},i}(A^{-}_{\tilde{t},i})^{\mathsf{T}}\right),

(4.202)

and the weighted least squares estimator as

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{\tilde{t}}=V^{-1}_{\tilde{t}}(\lambda)\left(\sum_{s=1}^{\tilde{t}}\omega(V_{s-1}(\lambda))\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(X^{+}_{s,i}A^{+}_{s,i}+X^{-}_{s,i}A^{-}_{s,i}\right)\right)\right)

(4.203)

where
X^{\pm}_{s,i}
is the reward sampled with
A^{\pm}_{s,i}
and the weights (or noise estimator (
4.55
)) are set to

\displaystyle\omega(V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda))=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda))}}{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{\tilde{t}-1,\delta}},\quad\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\tilde{t}}({A}^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i})=\frac{1}{\omega(V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda))},

(4.204)

with
\beta_{\tilde{t},\delta}
defined as in Lemma
4.5.2
with input
V_{\tilde{t}}
(
4.202
). When is clear from the context we will denote
V_{\tilde{t}}(\lambda)
simply as
V_{\tilde{t}}
. We state the pseudo-code of
LinUCB-VN
below.

Algorithm 6

LinUCB-VN

1:

Require:
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
\omega:\text{P}^{d}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

2:

Set initial design matrix
V_{0}\leftarrow\lambda_{0}\mathbb{I}_{d\times d}

3:

Choose initial estimator
{\theta}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
for
\theta
at random

4:

for

\tilde{t}=1,2,\cdots

do

5:

Optimistic action selection

6:

for

i=1,2,\cdots d-1

do

7:

Select actions
A^{+}_{\ttilde,i}
and
A^{-}_{\ttilde,i}
according to Eq. (
4.201
)

8:

Receive associated rewards
X^{+}_{\ttilde,i}
and
X^{-}_{\ttilde,i}

9:

end

for
Update estimator of sub-gaussian noise for
a^{+}_{t,i}

\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\ttilde}\leftarrow\frac{1}{\omega(V_{\ttilde-1}(\lambda_{0}))}
for
\ttilde\geq 2
or
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\ttilde}\leftarrow 1
for
\ttilde=1
.

10:

Update design matrix and WLSE

11:

V_{\ttilde}(\lambda_{0})\leftarrow V_{t-1}(\lambda_{0})+\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{t}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(A^{+}_{\ttilde,i}(A^{+}_{\ttilde,i})^{\mathsf{T}}+A^{-}_{\ttilde,i}(A^{-}_{\ttilde,i})^{\mathsf{T}}\right)

12:

\widetilde{\theta}_{t}^{\text{w}}\leftarrow V_{\ttilde}^{-1}(\lambda_{0})\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{\ttilde}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(A^{+}_{s,i}X^{+}_{t,i}+A^{-}_{s,i}X^{-}_{s,i})

13:

end

for

Regret analysis for
LinUCB-VN

In the simplified proof of our action selection rule for the circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, one of the key steps was showing that
\lambda_{\max}(V_{t})
grows linearly with
t
. This followed from the fact that the actions were bounded and
\Tr(V_{t})=O(t)
. However, for the weighted version (
4.202
), this no longer holds, and the analysis becomes a bit more involved. To understand the scaling, we will use a differential inequality involving
\lambda_{\max}(V_{t})
, and the lemma below will help us solve it.

Theorem 4.5.4

(
[
84
]
)
.

If
u
satisfies the differential inequality
\frac{\text{d}u(t)}{\text{d}t}\lesseqgtr f(u(t),t)
, and
y
is the solution to the ordinary differential equation (ODE)
\frac{\text{d}y(t)}{\text{d}t}=f(y(t),t)
under the boundary condition
u(t_{0})=y(t_{0})
, then:

\displaystyle\forall t>t_{0},u(t)\lesseqgtr y(t).

(4.205)

The Theorem below formalized the regret scaling for
LinUCB-VN

6
.

Theorem 4.5.5
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
\delta\in(0,1)
and
T=2(d-1)\widetilde{T}
for some
\widetilde{T}\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\omega(X)
defined as in (
4.204
) using
\delta
and
\lambda_{0}
satisfy the constraints in Theorem
4.4.8
. Then if we apply Algorithm
6
to a
d
dimensional stochastic linear bandit with linear vanishing noise (
4.185
) with probability at least
1-\delta
the regret satisfies

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\leq 4(d-1)+\left(144d^{2}\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{T,\max}\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)}\right),

(4.206)

where

\displaystyle\beta_{T,\max}:=\left(\lambda_{0}+\sqrt{2\log\frac{T}{\delta}+d\log\left(\frac{1}{144\lambda_{0}}T^{2}+\frac{1}{6\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}}T+1\right)}\right)^{2}.

(4.207)

Proof 4.5.6
.

To simplify notation we use

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,\ttilde}:=\lambda_{\min}(V_{\ttilde}(\lambda_{0})),\quad\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}:=\lambda_{\max}(V_{\ttilde}(\lambda_{0}))

(4.208)

First, we fix

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}=\max\left\{2,\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}}\frac{d}{6\sqrt{d-1}}+\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\right\},

(4.209)

and later we will justify this choice.
Note that the regret can be written as

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ttilde=1}^{\widetilde{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(\|\theta-A^{+}_{t,i}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\theta-A^{-}_{t,i}\|_{2}^{2}\right),

(4.210)

so to upper bound the regret, we need to quantify the distance
\|\theta-A^{+}_{\ttilde,i}\|_{2}^{2}
between the unknown parameter and the actions we select at each batch
t
. The history up to batch
\ttilde\leq\widetilde{T}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\ttilde}:=\big(X^{+}_{s,1},A^{+}_{s,1},X^{-}_{s,1},A^{-}_{s,1},...,X^{+}_{s,d-1},A^{+}_{s,d-1},X^{-}_{s,d-1},A^{-}_{s,d-1}\big)_{s=1}^{\ttilde}.

(4.211)

Lemma
4.5.2
gives us this distance with a certain probability under the assumption that the event

\displaystyle G_{t}:=

\displaystyle\{\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},a^{\pm}_{s,i}\big):\sigma^{2}_{s}(a^{\pm}_{s,i}\big))\leq\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(\mathcal{H}_{s-1},a^{\pm}_{s,i})\ \forall{s\in[t]}\},

(4.212)

holds.
Using the definition of the subgaussian parameter for the noise
\epsilon_{t}
(
4.185
) we can upper bound it for our choice of actions as

\displaystyle\eta^{2}_{t}(A^{\pm}_{t,i})

\displaystyle\leq 1-\langle A^{\pm}_{t,i},\theta\rangle^{2}=1-(1-\frac{1}{2}\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{t,i}\|_{2}^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{t,i}\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{t,i}\|_{2}^{4}\leq\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{t,i}\|_{2}^{2}.

(4.213)

Thus, we need to use an estimator of the form (
4.55
) that upper bounds the distance
\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{t,i}\|_{2}^{2}
. This quantity depends on the unknown parameter
\theta
, thus we can not guarantee that
G_{t}
holds with probability one at each batch
\ttilde
. The other event that we will need to hold to quantify the distance is the following

\displaystyle E_{\ttilde}:=\{\mathcal{H}_{\ttilde}:\forall s\in\left[\ttilde\right],\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{s}\},

(4.214)

which is guaranteed to hold with probability at least
1-\delta
if
G_{t}
holds by Lemma
4.5.2
. We leave the probability computation that both events hold for the end of the proof.

Choosing
w(V_{T})
and instantaneous regret bound under
E_{t}

For now, we will assume that
E_{t}
always holds to justify our choice of weights through a bound on
\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{t,i}\|_{2}
. This will also allow us to bound the instantaneous regret. Using the same geometrical argument as in Theorem
4.4.5
we have that

\displaystyle\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{t,i}\|_{2}\leq 3\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{\ttilde-1,\delta}}{\lambda_{\min,{\ttilde-1}}}},

(4.215)

where
\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}
is defined as in Lemma
4.5.2
.
For
\ttilde=0
,

\displaystyle\beta_{0,\delta}=\left(\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}+\sqrt{2\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)}\right).

(4.216)

Note that at batch
\ttilde
we use that
E_{\ttilde-1}
holds instead of
E_{\ttilde}
since we want to define
\hat{\sigma}_{\ttilde}
such that only depends on past information up to batch
\\
tilde{t}-1
. Thus, a choice that will guarantee the event
G_{t}
to hold under the assumption that
E_{t-1}
holds is
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\ttilde}:=\frac{9\beta_{\ttilde-1,\delta}}{\lambda_{\min,\ttilde-1}}
. However if we apply Theorem
4.4.8
(later we will check that we are under the right assumptions to use it) to our particular update of
V_{\ttilde}
with the choices of actions
A^{+}_{\ttilde,i},A^{-}_{\ttilde,i}
we have that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,\ttilde}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}},

(4.217)

It is important to note that the above bound is independent of the events
G_{\ttilde}
and
E_{\ttilde}
and it is a consequence only of our particular choice of actions (
4.201
). Combining (
4.217
) and (
4.215
)

\displaystyle\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{t,i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 9\frac{\beta_{\ttilde-1,\delta}}{\lambda_{\min,\ttilde-1}}\leq\frac{9\sqrt{3}\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{\ttilde-1,\delta}}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{\lambda_{\min,t-1}}}.

(4.218)

Thus, using that
\frac{9\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\leq 12
we see that with our definitions of estimator (
4.57
