) and weights

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}(A^{\pm}_{t,i})=\frac{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{\ttilde-1,\delta}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,\ttilde-1}}},\quad\omega(V_{\ttilde-1})=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\ttilde}},

(4.219)

are well defined since only depends on the history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
, and

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}_{\ttilde}(A^{\pm}_{\ttilde,i})\leq\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\ttilde}(A^{\pm}_{\ttilde,i})\quad\text{if}\quad\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{\ttilde-1}.

(4.220)

To bound the regret our technique uses upper and lower bounds on the scaling of
\Tr(V_{t})
as a function of the number of rounds. In the standard
LinUCB
technique if the actions are bounded (
\|A_{t}\|_{2}=\Theta(1)
) and this immediately gives the linear scaling
\Tr(V_{t})=\Theta(t)
. Since we are updating
V_{\ttilde}
using
\omega(V_{\ttilde})
we need to do some extra work, and we will see that
\Tr(V_{\ttilde})=\tilde{\Theta}(\ttilde^{2})
. While Theorem
4.4.5
only required an upper bound now we will need both upper and lower bound since we will also need to characterize
\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}
that is defined through
V_{\ttilde}
and in Theorem
4.4.5
only depends on
t
.

Upper bound for
\Tr(V_{t})

The reason why we need an upper bound for
\Tr(V_{t})
is because we will need an upper bound for
\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}
that depends on
\Tr(V_{\ttilde})
. A direct calculation shows

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{\ttilde})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}+\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde}2(d-1)\omega(V_{s-1})

(4.221)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}+\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde}\frac{2(d-1)}{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{s-1,\delta}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s-1}}

(4.222)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6\beta_{0,\delta}}\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s}},

(4.223)

where we used
\beta_{0,\delta}\leq\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}
. Then using
\lambda_{\max,t}\leq\Tr(V_{\ttilde})
and
\beta_{0,\delta}\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}\leq\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}\sum_{s=0}^{\ttilde-1}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s}}.

(4.224)

Now we want to extend
\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}
to a monotonic function on the interval
[0,\widetilde{T}]
. In order to do that we define the linear interpolation
g_{1}:[0,\widetilde{T}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as

\displaystyle g_{1}(x):=(\ttilde+1-x)\lambda_{\max,t}+(x-\ttilde)\lambda_{\max,\ttilde+1}\quad\text{for}\quad x\in[\ttilde,\ttilde+1)\quad\text{and}\quad\ttilde\in\{0,...,\widetilde{T}\}.

(4.225)

Then using (
4.224
) and
g_{1}(\ttilde)=\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}
we have

\displaystyle g_{1}(\ttilde)\leq\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}\sum_{s=0}^{t-1}\sqrt{g_{1}(s)}\leq\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}\int_{0}^{\ttilde}\sqrt{g_{1}(x)}\dd x\quad\text{for}\quad\ttilde\in\{0,...,\widetilde{T}\}.

(4.226)

Now we want to prove the above inequality but for
g_{1}(x)
with
x\in[0,\widetilde{T}]
. From the above inequality and the definition of
g_{1}(x)
(
4.225
) we have that for
x\in[\ttilde,\ttilde+1)

\displaystyle g_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}\sum_{s=0}^{\ttilde-1}\sqrt{g_{1}(s)}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}(x-\ttilde)\sqrt{g_{1}(\ttilde)}

(4.227)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}\int_{0}^{\ttilde}\sqrt{g_{1}(x)}\dd x+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}(x-\ttilde)\sqrt{g_{1}(\ttilde)}.

(4.228)

Then we can use the above relation and

\displaystyle\int_{\ttilde}^{x}\sqrt{g_{1}(s)}\dd s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x-{\ttilde}}\sqrt{g_{1}(s+\ttilde)}\dd s=\int_{0}^{x-\ttilde}\sqrt{(1-s)g_{1}(\ttilde)+sg_{1}(\ttilde+1)}\dd s

(4.229)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{x-\ttilde}\sqrt{g_{1}(\ttilde)}\geq(x-\ttilde)\sqrt{g_{1}(\ttilde)},

(4.230)

where the second equality follows from the definition of
g_{1}(x)
(
4.225
) and the first inequality
g_{1}(\ttilde)=\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}\leq\lambda_{\max,\ttilde+1}=g_{1}(\ttilde+1)
. We conclude that

\displaystyle g_{1}(x)\leq\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}\int_{0}^{x}\sqrt{g_{1}(s)}\dd s.

(4.231)

Using the fundamental theorem of calculus

\displaystyle g_{1}(x)\leq\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}(G_{1}(x)-G_{1}(0))\quad\text{where}\quad\frac{\dd G_{1}}{\dd x}=\sqrt{g_{1}(x)}.

(4.232)

Fixing
G(0)=0
and rearranging

\displaystyle\frac{\dd G_{1}}{\dd x}\leq\sqrt{\lambda_{0}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{6}G_{1}(x)}.

(4.233)

Now solving for the equality with
G(0)=0
and using Theorem
4.5.4
we arrive at

\displaystyle G_{1}(x)\leq\frac{\sqrt{d}}{24}x^{2}+\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}x.

(4.234)

Finally inserting the above into (
4.232
) using that
g_{1}(\ttilde)=\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}=g_{1}(\ttilde)\leq\frac{d}{144}{\ttilde}^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{d\lambda_{0}}}{6}\ttilde+\lambda_{0},

(4.235)

and this gives the following bound on
\Tr(V_{\ttilde})

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{\ttilde})\leq d\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}\leq d\left(\frac{d}{144}{\ttilde}^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{d\lambda_{0}}}{6}\ttilde+\lambda_{0}\right).

(4.236)

Upper bound for
\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}

In order to give an upper bound for
\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}
(
4.189
) we have to give an upper bound for
\det(V_{\ttilde})
. The inequality of arithmetic and geometric means gives

\displaystyle\det(V_{\ttilde})\leq\left(\frac{\Tr(V_{\ttilde})}{d}\right)^{d},

(4.237)

which combined with (
4.236
) and (
4.189
) gives

\displaystyle\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}\leq\left(\lambda_{0}+\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{\delta}+d\log\left(\frac{d}{144\lambda_{0}}{\ttilde}^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}6}\ttilde+1\right)}\right)^{2}.

(4.238)

Lower bound for
\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}

In order to give an upper bound for regret our main technique is to control the growth of
\lambda_{\max,{\ttilde}}
through a lower bound for
\lambda_{\max,{\ttilde}}
. We will employ the same technique as in the previous part. For the lower bound we are only interested in the leading terms so we start by lower bounding
\Tr(V_{\ttilde})
as

\displaystyle\Tr(V_{\ttilde})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{s=2}^{\ttilde}2(d-1)\omega(V_{s-1})

(4.239)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=2}^{\ttilde}\frac{\sqrt{d-1}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s-1}}}{6\beta_{s-1,\delta}}

(4.240)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde-1}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s}}.

(4.241)

where we used
\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}\leq\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}
.
Then since our problem is restricted to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have that for the maximum eigenvalue of
V_{\ttilde}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}\geq\frac{\Tr(V_{\ttilde})}{d}.

(4.242)

Thus combining with the above expressions we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}\geq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6d\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde-1}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s}}.

(4.243)

Addding each side by
\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6d\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}}
and using the fact that
\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}\geq\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}}
since
\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}+\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6d\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,t}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6d\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde-1}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s}}+\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6d\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,t}},

(4.244)

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6d\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\right)\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}+\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6d\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}}\geq\frac{\sqrt{d-1}}{6d\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s}},

(4.245)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s}}.

(4.246)

As before, we want to extend
\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}
to a monotonic function on the interval
[0,\widetilde{T}]
and define the lower linear interpolation
g_{2}:[0,\widetilde{T}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as

\displaystyle g_{2}(x):=(\ttilde+1-x)\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}+(x-\ttilde)\lambda_{\max,\ttilde+1}\quad\text{for}\quad x\in[\ttilde,\ttilde+1)\quad\text{and}\quad\ttilde\in\{0,...,\widetilde{T}\}.

(4.247)

Then we have that

\displaystyle g_{2}(\ttilde)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde}\sqrt{g_{2}(s)}

(4.248)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{g_{2}(x)}\dd x\quad\text{for}\quad\ttilde\in\{0,...,\widetilde{T}\},

(4.249)

since
g_{2}(\ttilde)=\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}
for
\ttilde\in\{0,....,\widetilde{T}\}
. Then we can check that the above inequality also holds for
x\in[0,\widetilde{T}]
. If
x\in[\ttilde,\ttilde+1)
we have

\displaystyle g_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{\ttilde}\sqrt{g_{2}(s)}+(x-t)\sqrt{g_{2}(\ttilde+1)}\right)

(4.250)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\left(\int_{0}^{\ttilde}\sqrt{g_{2}(x)}\dd x+(x-\ttilde)\sqrt{g_{2}(\ttilde+1)}\right),

(4.251)

where the first inequality we applied the definition of
g_{2}(x)
(
4.247
) and the inequality for
\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}
(
4.243
). Then,

\displaystyle\int_{\ttilde}^{x}\sqrt{g_{2}(s)}\dd s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x-\ttilde}\sqrt{g_{2}(s+\ttilde)}\dd s=\int_{0}^{x-\ttilde}\sqrt{(1-s)g_{2}(\ttilde)+sg_{2}(\ttilde+1)}\dd s

(4.252)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{x-\ttilde}\sqrt{g_{2}(\ttilde+1)}\dd s\leq(x-t)\sqrt{g_{2}(\ttilde+1)},

(4.253)

where the second equality follows from the definition of
g_{2}(x)
and the first inequality from
g_{2}(\ttilde)=\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}\leq\lambda_{\max,t+1}\leq g_{2}(\ttilde+1)
. Combining with (
4.250
) we have

\displaystyle g_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\int_{0}^{x}\sqrt{g_{2}(s)}ds.

(4.254)

Using the fundamental theorem of calculus

\displaystyle g_{2}(x)\geq\frac{1}{1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}\left(G_{2}(x)-G_{2}(0)\right)\quad\text{where}\quad\frac{\dd G_{2}(x)}{\dd x}=\sqrt{g_{2}(x)}.

(4.255)

Fixing
G_{2}(0)=0
and rearranging

\displaystyle\frac{\dd G_{2}(x)}{\dd x}\geq\sqrt{\frac{1}{1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}}\sqrt{G_{2}(x)}.

(4.256)

Now solving the equality with
G_{2}(0)=0
and using Theorem
4.5.4

\displaystyle G_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4+24\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta}}x^{2}.

(4.257)

Using that
g_{2}(\ttilde)=\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}
and inserting the above result into (
4.255
)

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}=g_{2}(\ttilde)\geq\frac{1}{4(1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{\widetilde{T},\delta})^{2}}{\ttilde}^{2}.

(4.258)

Bounding the regret

From the weight update (
4.204
) we have

\displaystyle\omega(V_{\ttilde})=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,\ttilde-1}}}{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}}\leq\frac{1}{12\sqrt{d-1}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,\ttilde-1}},

(4.259)

so to use Therorem
4.4.8
we can choose
C=\frac{1}{12\sqrt{d-1}}
, our choice of
\lambda_{0}
(
4.209
), actions (
4.201
) and the sequence
\{\theta^{\text{w}}_{t}\}_{t=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{S}^{d}
. Then our update given by (
4.202
) satisfies all conditions of Theorem
4.4.8
and our choice of actions guarantees

\displaystyle\lambda_{\min,\ttilde}\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}}.

(4.260)

Then from (
4.238
) we can upper bound
\beta_{t,\delta}
as

\displaystyle\beta_{\ttilde,\delta}\leq\beta_{T,\max}\quad\text{where}\quad\beta_{T,\max}:=\left(\lambda_{0}+\sqrt{2\log\frac{1}{\delta}+d\log\left(\frac{d}{144\lambda_{0}}T^{2}+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}6}T+1\right)}\right)^{2}.

(4.261)

Finally, we can use the bound from (
4.5.6
)(
4.215
) (under the assumptions that
E_{t}
and
G_{t}
hold) combined with (
4.260
) to arrive at

\displaystyle\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{\ttilde,i}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9\beta_{T,\max}}{\lambda_{\min,\ttilde-1}}

(4.262)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{T,\max}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,\ttilde-1}}}

(4.263)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{24\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{T,\max}(1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{T,\max})}{\ttilde-1}

(4.264)

\displaystyle=\frac{144d\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{T,\max}}{\ttilde-1}

(4.265)

where we used the lower bound for
\lambda_{\max,\ttilde}
(
4.258
) and
\frac{9\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{2}}\leq 12
.
Then using the above result we can bound the regret as

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ttilde=1}^{\widetilde{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(\|\theta-A^{+}_{\ttilde,i}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\theta-A^{-}_{\ttilde,i}\|_{2}^{2}\right)

(4.266)

\displaystyle\leq 4(d-1)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ttilde=2}^{\widetilde{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(\|\theta-A^{+}_{\ttilde,i}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\theta-A^{-}_{\ttilde,i}\|_{2}^{2}\right)

(4.267)

\displaystyle\leq 4(d-1)+\left(144d^{2}\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{T,\max}\right)\sum_{\ttilde=2}^{\tilde{T}}\frac{1}{\ttilde-1}

(4.268)

\displaystyle\leq 4(d-1)+\left(144d^{2}\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{T,\max}\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)}\right).

(4.269)

Using that
\beta_{T,\max}=O(d\log(T))
we have that

\displaystyle\text{Regret}(T)=\widetilde{O}(d^{4}\log^{3}(T)).

(4.270)

Success probability analysis

From the computations in (
4.5.6
)(
4.215
)(
4.219
)(
4.220
) and
our choice of
\sigma^{2}_{t}(a_{t})
(
4.204
) we have that

\displaystyle\text{if}\quad\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{s-1}\Rightarrow\eta^{2}_{s}(A^{\pm}_{s,i})\leq\frac{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{t-1,\delta}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\max,s-1}}}=\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A^{\pm}_{s,i}),

(4.271)

thus,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(G_{t})\geq\mathrm{Pr}(E_{t-1})

(4.272)

where we have used the definitions of the events
G_{t}
(
4.212
) and
E_{t}
(
4.214
).

The initial choice
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{1}=1
implies
\eta^{2}_{1}\leq\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{1}
and using Lemma
4.5.2
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(G_{1})

\displaystyle=1,

(4.273)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(E_{1})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\delta.

(4.274)

Using Bayes theorem

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\ttilde})=\frac{\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\ttilde}|G_{\ttilde})\mathrm{Pr}(G_{\ttilde})}{\mathrm{Pr}(G_{\ttilde}|E_{\ttilde})}=\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\ttilde}|G_{\ttilde})\mathrm{Pr}(G_{\ttilde}),

(4.275)

where in the last equality we used

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(G_{\ttilde}|E_{\ttilde})=1.

(4.276)

From Lemma
4.5.2
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\ttilde}|G_{\ttilde})\geq 1-\delta.

(4.277)

Thus, applying recursively the above inequalities

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(G_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{Pr}(E_{1})\geq 1-\delta,

(4.278)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(E_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}(E_{2}|G_{2})\mathrm{Pr}(G_{2})\geq(1-\delta)^{2}

(4.279)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(G_{\ttilde})

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\ttilde-1})\geq(1-\delta)^{\ttilde-1}

(4.280)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\ttilde})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\ttilde}|G_{\ttilde})\mathrm{Pr}(G_{\ttilde})\geq(1-\delta)^{\ttilde}

(4.281)

Finally to bound the regret we used the assumption that
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{\ttilde}
for any
\ttilde\in\{1,...,\widetilde{T}-1\}
. Thus we can bound the probability that the obtained bound holds as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}

\displaystyle\left(R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\leq 4(d-1)+\left(144d^{2}\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{T,\max}\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)}\right)\right)

(4.282)

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\widetilde{T}}\cap G_{\widetilde{T}})=\mathrm{Pr}(G_{\widetilde{T}})\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\widetilde{T}}|G_{\widetilde{T}}).

(4.283)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\delta)^{\widetilde{T}-1}(1-\delta)=(1-\delta)^{\widetilde{T}}.

(4.284)

The result follows choosing
\delta=\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\widetilde{T}}
for some
0<\delta^{\prime}<1
, the inequality
\left(1-\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{\widetilde{T}}\right)^{\widetilde{T}}\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}
and
\frac{\sqrt{d}}{d-1}\leq 2
.

Corollary 4.5.7
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
4.5.5
, we can choose
\delta=\frac{1}{\widetilde{T}}
and Algorithm
6
achieves

\displaystyle\EX_{\theta,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)]\leq 8(d-1)+\left(144d^{2}\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{T,\max}\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)}\right),

(4.285)

or

\displaystyle\EX_{\theta,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)]=O(d^{4}\log^{3}(T)).

(4.286)

Proof 4.5.8
.

Defining the event

\displaystyle R_{T}=\bigg\{\mathcal{H}_{\widetilde{T}}:R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\leq 4(d-1)+\left(144d^{2}\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{T,\max}\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)}\right)\bigg\}

(4.287)

we have by Theorem
4.5.5
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(R_{T})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\delta=1-\frac{1}{\widetilde{T}},

(4.288)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(R_{T}^{C})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\widetilde{T}}.

(4.289)

Then

\displaystyle\EX_{\theta,\pi}[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)]

\displaystyle=\EX_{\theta,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\mathbbm{1}\{R_{T}\}\right]+\EX_{\theta,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\mathbbm{1}\{R_{T}^{C}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4(d-1)+\left(144d^{2}\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{T,\max}\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)}\right)

\displaystyle+4(d-1)\widetilde{T}\mathrm{Pr}\left(R_{T}^{C}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 8(d-1)+\left(144d^{2}\beta^{2}_{T,\max}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{T,\max}\right)\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)}\right),

(4.290)

and the results follows using that for
\delta=\frac{1}{\widetilde{T}}
,
\beta_{T,\max}=O(d\log(T))
.

4.5.2
Linear bandits with linearly vanishing variance

We conclude the algorithms Chapter by studying the linear bandit model in which the PSMAQB setting naturally fits, allowing us to address the questions posed in the Introduction. Specifically, we relax the subgaussian condition (
4.185
) considered in Section
4.5.1
, and study a linear bandit with action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, unknown parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and reward model
X_{t}=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle+\epsilon_{t}
where the noise
\epsilon_{t}
satisfies

\displaystyle\EX[\epsilon_{t}\mid A_{1},X_{1},\dots,A_{t-1},X_{t-1},A_{t}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[\epsilon_{t}\mid A_{1},X_{1},\dots,A_{t-1},X_{t-1},A_{t}]

\displaystyle\leq 1-\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle^{2}.

(4.291)

This setting corresponds to the variance condition (
4.52
) in the Bloch sphere representation of the PSMAQB problem. Although we still aim to use the weighted estimators introduced under the subgaussian noise assumption (
4.185
), the confidence region derived in Lemma
4.5.2
does not directly extend to the case of weighted variance estimation. We will need an additional tool on top of the weighted estimators.

Median of means for an online least squares estimator

We want to use the same idea as in the previous sections and, through a careful choice of actions, introduce weighted terms in the design matrix
1/\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(a_{s})
that overestimate the reward variances and “boost” the confidence along directions
a_{s}
close to
\theta
. However, this comes at a price: to obtain good concentration bounds for our estimator, we now have to deal with unbounded random variables and only finite variance. While the subgaussian property is strong enough to guarantee well-defined confidence regions, the variance condition is looser, and the random variables can have heavy tails with looser concentration. We address this issue using the recent ideas of median of means (MoM) for online least squares estimators introduced in
[
30
,
76
,
94
]
. The construction builds on the classical median of means method
[
68
, Chapter 3]
for real-valued random variables with unbounded support and bounded variance, but requires non-trivial adaptation to the online linear least squares setting. As in the classical case, we use the
k
independent estimators to construct a median of means estimator from which we can derive a confidence region with concentration bounds scaling as
1-\exp(-k)
.

First we discuss the medians of means method for the online linear least squares estimator introduced in
[
94
]
. We are going to use this estimator later in order to design a strategy that minimizes the regret for the noise model (
4.5.2
). In order to build the median of means online least squares estimator for linear bandits we need to sample
k
independent rewards for each action. Specifically given an action set
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, an unknown parameter
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, at each time step
t
we select an action
A_{t}\in\mathcal{A}
and sample
k
independent rewards using
A_{t}
where the outcome rewards are distributed as

\displaystyle X_{t,i}=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle+\epsilon_{t,i}\quad\text{for }i\in[k],

(4.292)

for some noise such that
\EX[\epsilon_{t,i}|A_{1},X_{1},...,A_{t-1},X_{t-1},A_{t}]=0
. We refer to
k
as the number of subsamples per time step. Then at time step
t
we define
k
least squares estimators as

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{t,i}=V_{t}^{-1}(\lambda)\sum_{s=1}^{t}X_{s,i}A_{s}\quad\text{for }i\in[k],

(4.293)

where
V_{t}
is the design matrix defined as

\displaystyle V_{t}(\lambda)=\lambda\mathbb{I}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}A_{s}A_{s}^{\mathsf{T}},

(4.294)

with
\lambda>0
being a parameter that ensures invertibility of
V_{t}
. We note that the design matrix is independent of
i
. Then the median of means for least squares estimator (MOMLSE) is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{t}^{\text{\tiny MoM}}:=\hat{\theta}_{t,k^{*}}\quad\text{where }k^{*}=\argmin_{j\in[k]}y_{j},

(4.295)

where

\displaystyle y_{j}=\text{median}\{\|\hat{\theta}_{t,j}-\hat{\theta}_{t,i}\|_{V_{t}}:i\in[k]/j\}\quad\text{for }j\in[k].

(4.296)

Using the results in
[
94
]
we have that the above estimator has the following concentration property around the true estimator.

Lemma 4.5.9

(Lemma 2 and 3 in
[
94
]
)
.

Let
\hat{\theta}_{t}^{\textup{\tiny MoM}}
be the MOMLSE defined in (
4.295
) with
k
subsamples with
\{X_{s,i}\}_{(s,i)\in[t]\times[k]}
rewards and corresponding actions
\{A_{s}\}_{s\in[t]}
. Assume that the noise of all rewards has bounded variance, i.e
\EX\left[\epsilon^{2}_{s,i}|A_{1},X_{1},...,A_{t-1},X_{t-1},A_{t}\right]\leq 1
for all
s\in[t]
and
i\in[k]
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left(\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}^{\text{\tiny MoM}}\|^{2}_{V_{t}}\leq 9\left(\sqrt{9d}+\lambda\|\theta\|_{2}\right)^{2}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(\frac{-k}{24}\right).

(4.297)

We will use a slight modification of the above result introducing the weights as in Section
4.5.1

,\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}:\mathcal{H}_{t-1}\times A\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0},

where
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}=\{X_{s,i}\}_{(s,i)\in[t-1]\times[k]}\cup\{A_{s}\}_{s\in[t-1]}
contains the past information of rewards and actions. As before for our purposes we will use only the information of the past actions and in order to simplify notation we will use
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}(A)
to denote an estimator of the variance for the reward associated action
A\in\mathcal{A}
with the information collected up to time step
t-1
. Then to define the weighted version of the median of means we need to sample
k
rewards for each action and define the following
k
linear least squares estimators

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{t,i}=V_{t}^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A_{s})}X_{s,i}A_{s}\quad\text{for }i\in[k],

(4.298)

with the weighted design matrix

\displaystyle V_{t}=\lambda\mathbb{I}+\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A_{s})}A_{s}A_{s}^{\mathsf{T}}.

(4.299)

Then the weighted version of the median of means linear estimator is defined analogously to (
4.295
) with the corresponding weighted versions (
4.298
)(
4.299
) and we will denote it as
\hat{\theta}_{t}^{\text{\tiny wMOM}}
.
In our algorithm analysis we will use the following analogous concentration bound under the condition that the estimators
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}
overestimate the true variance.

Corollary 4.5.10
.

Let
\hat{\theta}_{t}^{\textup{\tiny wMOM}}
be the weighted version of the MOMLSE with
k
subsamples,
\{X_{s,i}\}_{(s,i)\in[t]\times[k]}
rewards with corresponding actions
\{A_{s}\}_{s\in[t]}
and variance estimator
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}
. Define the following event

\displaystyle G_{t}:=\{\big(\mathcal{H}_{t-1},A_{t}\big):\textup{{Var}}[\epsilon_{s,i}]\leq\hat{\sigma}^{2}(A_{s})\ \forall s,i\in[t]\times[k]\}.

(4.300)

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left(\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}^{\textup{\tiny wMOM}}\|^{2}_{V_{t}}\leq\beta_{\textup{w}}\mid G_{t}\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(\frac{-k}{24}\right),

(4.301)

where

\displaystyle\beta_{\textup{w}}:=9\left(\sqrt{9d}+\lambda\|\theta\|_{2}\right)^{2}.

(4.302)

Proof 4.5.11
.

The result follows from applying Lemma
4.5.9
to the sequences of re-normalized rewards
\{\frac{X_{s,i}}{\hat{\sigma}_{s}(A_{s})}\}_{(s,i)\in[t]\times[k]}
and actions
\{\frac{A_{s,i}}{\hat{\sigma}_{s}(a_{s})}\}_{s\in[t]}
. We only need to check that the sequence
\{\frac{\epsilon_{s,i}}{\hat{\sigma}_{s}(A_{s})}\}_{(s,i)\in[t]\times[k]}
has finite variance. Conditioning with the event
G_{t}
and the fact that by definition
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A_{s})
only depend on the past
s-1
action and rewards we have that the re-normalized noise has bounded variance since

\displaystyle\EX\left[\left(\frac{\epsilon_{s,i}}{\hat{\sigma}_{s}(A_{s})}\right)^{2}\Bigg|\mathcal{H}_{s-1},A_{s}\right]=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A_{s})}\EX[\epsilon^{2}_{s,i}|\mathcal{H}_{s-1},A_{s}]=\frac{\mathrm{Var}[\epsilon_{s,i}]}{\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{s}(A_{s})}\leq 1.

(4.303)

LinUCB
variance vanishing noise algorithm

The algorithm that we design for linear bandits with linearly variance vanishing noise (
4.5.2
) is
LinUCB-VVN
(
LinUCB
vanishing variance noise) stated in Algorithm
7
. This algorithm combines all the tools we developed in this Chapter including the action selection rule from Theorem
4.4.8
, the weighted LSE from Section
4.5.1
and the recently introduced median of means (
4.295
). The algorithm runs in
\tilde{T}\in\mathbb{N}
batches, for a total time horizon of
T=2k(d-1)
time steps where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is a free parameter that controls the median of means. At each batch
\tilde{t}\in[\mathbb{T}]
the algorithm plays the actions

\displaystyle A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}:=\frac{\widetilde{A}^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}}{\|\widetilde{A}^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}},\quad\tilde{A}^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}=\frac{\hat{\theta}^{\text{wMoM}}_{\tilde{t}}}{\|\hat{\theta}^{\text{wMoM}}_{\tilde{t}}\|_{2}}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}}v_{\tilde{t}-1,i},\quad

(4.304)

for
i\in[d-1]
, where
v_{\tilde{t}-1,i}
is
i
-th eigenvector of the weighted design matrix
V_{\tilde{t}-1}
and where for each action
A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}
we sample
k
independent rewards
\{X^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i,j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
in order to build the weighted MOMLSE defined as in Section
4.5.2
. The design matrix
V_{\tilde{t}}
is updated as

\displaystyle V_{\tilde{t}}(\lambda)=V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda)+\omega(V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda))\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(A^{+}_{\tilde{t},i}(A^{+}_{\tilde{t},i})^{\mathsf{T}}+A^{-}_{\tilde{t},i}(A^{-}_{\tilde{t},i})^{\mathsf{T}}\right),

(4.305)

and the
k
weighted least squares estimators are

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\text{w}}_{\tilde{t},j}=V^{-1}_{\tilde{t}}(\lambda)\left(\sum_{s=1}^{\tilde{t}}\omega(V_{s-1})\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(X^{+}_{s,i,j}A^{+}_{s,i}+X^{-}_{s,i,j}A^{-}_{s,i}\right)\right)

(4.306)

where the weights
\omega(V_{\tilde{t}-1})
and variance estimator are chosen as

\displaystyle\omega(V_{\tilde{t}-1}):=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}}{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{w}},\quad\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}(A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}):=\frac{1}{\omega(V_{\tilde{t}-1})},

(4.307)

with
\beta_{w}
defined as in (
4.302
). We note that the definition for
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}(A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i})
fulfills the definition of variance estimator (
4.55
) stated in the previous section since it only depends on the past history
\mathcal{H}_{t-1}
.

Require:
\lambda_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\omega:\text{P}^{d}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

Set initial design matrix
V_{0}\leftarrow\lambda_{0}\mathbb{I}_{d\times d}

Choose initial estimator
{\theta}_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
for
\theta
at random

for

\tilde{t}=1,2,\cdots

do

Optimistic action selection

for

i=1,2,\cdots d-1

do

Select actions
A^{+}_{\tilde{t},i}
and
A^{-}_{\tilde{t},i}
according to Eq. (
4.304
)

Sample
k
independent rewards for each
a^{\pm}_{t,i}

for

j=1,...,k

do

Receive associated rewards
X^{+}_{\tilde{t},i,j}
and
X^{-}_{\tilde{t},i,j}

end

for

end

for

Update estimator of subgaussian noise for
A^{+}_{t,i}

\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\tilde{t}}\leftarrow\frac{1}{\omega(V_{\tilde{t}-1}(\lambda_{0}))}
for
\tilde{t}\geq 2
or
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{\tilde{t}}\leftarrow 1
for
\tilde{t}=1
.

Update design matrix

V_{\tilde{t}}\leftarrow V_{\tilde{t}-1}+\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{\tilde{t}}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(A^{+}_{t,i}(A^{+}_{t,i})^{\mathsf{T}}+A^{-}_{t,i}(A^{-}_{t,i})^{\mathsf{T}}\right)

Update LSE for each subsample

for

j=1,2,...,k
:
do

\hat{\theta}_{t,j}^{\text{w}}\leftarrow V_{\tilde{t}}^{-1}\sum_{s=1}^{\tilde{t}}\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{\tilde{t}}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(A^{+}_{s,i}X^{+}_{s,i,j}+A^{-}_{s,i}X^{-}_{s,i,j})

end

for

Compute
\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t}}^{\text{\tiny wMOM}}
using
\{\hat{\theta}_{\tilde{t},j}^{\text{w}}\}_{j=1}^{k}

end

for

Algorithm 7

LinUCB-VVN

Regret analysis for
LinUCB-VVN

Theorem 4.5.12
.

Let
d\geq 2
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\widetilde{T}\in\mathbb{N}
the number of batches and
T=2(d-1)k\widetilde{T}
the total time horizon. Let
\omega(X)
defined as in (
4.307
) using
\lambda_{0}
satisfying the constraints in Theorem
4.4.8
. Then if we apply Algorithm
7
(
\lambda_{0},k,\omega
) to a
d
dimensional stochastic linear bandit with variance as in (
4.5.2
) with probability at least
(1-\exp(-k/24))^{\widetilde{T}}
the regret satisfies

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\leq 4k(d-1)

\displaystyle+144d(d-1)k(\beta_{w})^{2}\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)k}\right)

\displaystyle+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}k\beta_{w}\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)k}\right),

(4.308)

and at each time step
t\in[T]
with the same probability it can output an estimator
\hat{\theta}_{t}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that

\displaystyle\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{576d^{2}(\beta_{w})^{2}k+96d\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{w}k}{t},

(4.309)

with
\beta_{w}
defined as in (
4.302
).

From the above Theorem we have that if we set
k=\lceil 24\log\left(\frac{\widetilde{T}}{\delta}\right)\rceil
for some
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
then with probability at least
1-\delta

LinUCB-VNN
achieves

\displaystyle\text{Regret}(T)=O\left(d^{4}\log^{2}(T)\right),\quad\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}=O\left(\frac{\log(T)}{t}\right).

(4.310)

Proof 4.5.13
.

The proof follows the lines of the proof in Theorem
4.5.5
with few modifications. From the expression of the regret using
\theta,A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
we have that

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\theta-A_{t}\|_{2}^{2}

(4.311)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\tilde{t}=1}^{\tilde{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(\|\theta-A^{+}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\theta-A^{-}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}^{2}\right)

(4.312)

\displaystyle\leq 4k(d-1)+\frac{k}{2}\sum_{\tilde{t}=2}^{\tilde{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(\|\theta-A^{+}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\theta-A^{-}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

(4.313)

Thus, it suffices to gives an upper bound between the distance of the unknown parameter
\theta
and the actions
A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}
selected by the algorithm (
4.304
). We denote the batches
\tilde{t}\in[\widetilde{T}]
. First we will do the computation assuming that the event

\displaystyle E_{\tilde{t}}:=\{\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{t}}:\forall s\in[\tilde{t}],\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{s}\},

(4.314)

holds where
\mathcal{C}_{s}=\{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\theta^{\prime}-\hat{\theta}^{\text{wMOM}}_{s}\|^{2}_{V_{s}}\leq\beta_{w}\}
. Here the history
\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{t}}
is defined with the previous outcomes and actions of our algorithm i.e

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{t}}:=\left(X^{+}_{s,i,j},A^{+}_{s,i},X^{-}_{s,i,j},A^{-}_{s,i}\right)_{(s,i,j)\in[\tilde{t}]\times[d-1]\times[k]}

(4.315)

Later we will quantify the probability that this event always hold. Using the definition of the actions
A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}
(
4.304
) and
\theta,\hat{\theta}^{\text{\tiny wMOM}}_{\tilde{t}}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and the arguments of Theorem
4.4.5
we have that

\displaystyle\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{9\beta^{\text{w}}}{\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}-1})}.

(4.316)

Then using that the design matrix
V_{\tilde{t}}
(
4.305
) is updated as in Theorem
4.4.8
and the choice of weights (
4.307
) we fix

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}\geq\max\left\{2,2\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}}\frac{d}{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{w}}+\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\right\}

(4.317)

and we have that
\lambda_{\min}(V_{\tilde{t}})\geq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3(d-1)}\lambda_{\max}(V_{\tilde{t}})}
applying Theorem
4.4.8
. Inserting this into the above we have

\displaystyle\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{12\sqrt{d-1}\beta^{\text{w}}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(V_{\tilde{t}})}}.

(4.318)

Thus, it remains to provide a lower bound on
\lambda_{\max}(V_{\tilde{t}})
. We note that in Theorem
4.5.5
we also had to provide an upper bound but this was because the constant
\beta_{w}
beta depended on the time step
t
. The update of the design matrix is the same as the algorithm analyzed in Theorem
4.5.5
, thus we can reuse the same computations and arrive at

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(V_{\tilde{t}})\geq\frac{\tilde{t}^{2}}{4(1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta)^{2}}.

(4.319)

Now we can insert the above into (
4.318
) and we have

\displaystyle\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{24\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{w}(1+6\frac{d}{\sqrt{d-1}}\beta_{w})}{\tilde{t}-1}

(4.320)

\displaystyle=\frac{144d(\beta_{w})^{2}+24\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{w}}{\tilde{t}-1}.

(4.321)

Thus, we can inserted the above bound into the regret expression and we have

\displaystyle R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)

\displaystyle\leq 4k(d-1)+(144d(d-1)k(\beta_{w})^{2}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}k\beta_{w})\sum_{\tilde{t}=2}^{\tilde{T}}\frac{1}{t-1}

(4.322)

\displaystyle\leq 4k(d-1)+144d(d-1)k(\beta_{w})^{2}\log\widetilde{T}+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}k\beta_{w}\log\widetilde{T}

(4.323)

\displaystyle=4k(d-1)+144d(d-1)k(\beta_{w})^{2}\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)k}\right)

(4.324)

\displaystyle+24(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}k(\beta_{w})\log\left(\frac{T}{2(d-1)k}\right).

(4.325)

It remains to quantify the probability that the event
E_{\tilde{t}}
holds. For that we will use the concentration bounds of the median of means for least squares estimator stated in Corollary
4.5.10
. From the variance condition of our model (
4.5.2
) we have that for the rewards
X^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i,j}=\langle\theta,A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\rangle+\epsilon^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i,j}
the variance of the noise satisfies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[\epsilon^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i,j}|\mathcal{H}_{\tilde{t}-1},A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}]\leq 1-\langle\theta,A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\rangle^{2}\leq 2(1-\langle\theta,A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}))=\|\theta-A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\|_{2}^{2},

(4.326)

where we used
1+\langle\theta,A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}\rangle\leq 2
.
Thus from our choice of weights (
4.307
) and (
4.320
) we have that

\displaystyle\text{if }\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{s-1}\Rightarrow\VX[\epsilon^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i,j}|\mathcal{F}_{\tilde{t}-1}]\leq\hat{\sigma}_{s}^{2}(A^{\pm}_{s,i}).

(4.327)

Then in order to apply Corollary
4.5.10
we note that from the choice
\hat{\sigma}_{s}^{2}(a^{\pm}_{1,i})=1
the event
G_{\tilde{t}}
at
\tilde{t}=1
is always satisfied i.e
\mathrm{Pr}(G_{1})=1
. Then applying Bayes theorem, union bound over the events
G_{1},E_{1},...,G_{t-1},E_{t}
and Corollary
4.5.10
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(E_{\widetilde{T}}\cap G_{\widetilde{T}})\geq\left(1-\exp(-k/24)\right)^{\widetilde{T}}.

(4.328)

This probability also quantifies the probability that (
4.320
) holds since the only assumption we used is
\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{\tilde{t}-1}
. Then we can take simply one of the actions
A^{\pm}_{\tilde{t},i}
as the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{t}
and the result follows using the relabeling
t=2(d-1)k\tilde{t}
and the inequality
1/(\tilde{t}-1)\leq 2/\tilde{t}
for
\tilde{t}\geq 2
. The more detailed computation of the above probability is identical as the on in Theorem
4.5.5
.

In the previous Theorem we did not set a specific value for the parameter
k
or the number of subsamples per action. We note that the regret scales linearly with
k
but since the success probability scales exponentially with
k
it will suffice to set
k\sim\log(T)
such that in expectation we get the
\log^{2}(T)
behaviour. We formalize this in the following Corollary.

Corollary 4.5.14
.

Under the same assumptions of Theorem
4.5.12
we can fix
k=\lceil 24\log(\widetilde{T}^{2})\rceil
and we have

\displaystyle\EX_{\theta,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\right]\leq 344(d-1)\log\left(T\right)+\left(3546d(d-1)(\beta_{w})^{2}+1152(d-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}\beta_{w}\right)\log^{2}\left(T\right)

(4.329)

and for
t\in[T]
,

\displaystyle\EX_{\theta,\pi}\left[\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq\frac{27648d^{2}(\beta_{w})^{2}\log(T)+4608d\sqrt{d-1}\beta_{w}\log(T)}{t}+\frac{4(d-1)\log(T)}{T}.

(4.330)

Using that
\beta_{w}=O(d)
gives

\displaystyle\EX_{\theta,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\right]=O(d^{4}\log^{2}(T)),\quad\EX\left[\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\right]=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{d^{4}}{t}\right).

(4.331)

Proof 4.5.15
.

The result of Theorem
4.5.12
holds with probability at least
(1-\exp(-k/24))^{\widetilde{T}}
. Setting
k=\lceil 24\log(\widetilde{T}^{2})\rceil
gives

\displaystyle(1-\exp(-k/24))^{\widetilde{T}}\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{\widetilde{T}^{2}}\right)^{\widetilde{T}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{\widetilde{T}}.

(4.332)

Then given the event
R_{T}
such that Algorithm
7
achieves the bounds given by Theorem
4.5.12
we have that the probability of failure is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}(R^{C}_{T})\leq\frac{1}{\widetilde{T}},

(4.333)

where we used
1=\mathrm{Pr}(R_{T})+\mathrm{Pr}(R^{C}_{T})
. Then the expectation of the bad events can be bounded as

\displaystyle\EX\left[\text{Regret}(T)\mathbbm{1}\{R^{C}_{T}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4(d-1)k\widetilde{T}\mathrm{Pr}(R^{C}_{T})\leq 4(d-1)k

(4.334)

\displaystyle\EX\left[\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}\{R^{C}_{T}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathrm{Pr}(R^{C}_{T})\leq\frac{4}{\widetilde{T}}

(4.335)

where we used
R_{T}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\theta,\pi)\leq 2T=4(d-1)k\widetilde{T}
,
\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 4
. Finally the result follows inserting the value of
k=24\log(\widetilde{T}^{2})
into the bounds of Theorem
4.5.12
and using
\widetilde{T}\leq T
.

Note.
While the above results apply for the qubit version of the PSMAQB setting, we will state the exact connection in Theorem
5.1.3
in the first section of the next Chapter
5
of applications.

Chapter 5
Applications

This chapter is based on the author’s works
[
73
,
26
,
72
]
, which explore applications of the multi-armed quantum bandit framework.

We begin with an application of the PSMAQB algorithm
7
to quantum state tomography, where we relate regret to cumulative disturbance. We show that it is possible to learn pure quantum states with minimal disturbance to the samples.

Next, we consider a specific setting of learning with minimal disturbance: quantum state-agnostic work extraction protocols. In this task, we study protocols for extracting work when given oracle access to an unknown pure qubit state, with the goal of transferring energy to a battery system. We analyze both discrete and continuous battery settings, and show that using the PSMAQB algorithm
7
, we can achieve polylogarithmic cumulative dissipation. Here, dissipation refers to the loss of extractable energy due to not knowing the underlying state.

As a final application, we modify the MAQB setting to study a recommender system for quantum data. In this setting, in each round, a learner receives an observable (the context) and must choose one quantum state to measure from a finite set of unknown states (the actions). The goal is to maximize the reward in each round, defined as the outcome of the measurement. Based on this model, we formulate the low-energy quantum state recommendation problem, where the context is a Hamiltonian and the objective is to recommend the state with the lowest energy. For this task, we study two families of contexts: the Ising model and a generalized cluster model.

5.1
Learning pure states without disturbing them

We start with one of the most immediate applications of the PSMAQB setting, a new approach for quantum state tomography. Given sequential access to a finite number of samples of a quantum state, our goal is not only to accurately learn a classical description of the state but also to use measurements that disturb the copies as little as possible. Generally, these two goals are incompatible, and we are thus interested in tomography algorithms that find an optimal balance between them.

Minimizing disturbance is important in many real-world scenarios where the samples that we use for tomography are in fact resources for another tasks —  and thus we want to learn the state in a way that is as non-intrusive as possible, ensuring that the post-measurement states remain useful for their intended purpose. An example of this occurs in quantum key distribution, where tomography can be used to keep reference frames aligned during a run, but any disturbance due to tomographic measurements will induce bit errors in the correlations used to extract a secret key. Disturbance is also relevant for state-agnostic resource distillation, where resourceful states might be destroyed by tomographic measurements, but learning the unknown state is crucial since optimal extraction protocols generally depend on its description.

In both cases, we encounter a fundamental trade-off between exploration (learning the state) and exploitation (using the samples for another purpose). This trade-off is exactly the one we find in a multi-armed bandit problem, and we are going to see how we can relate the disturbance to the samples to the figure of merit we studied during this thesis, the regret.

More formally, we consider a scenario where we have sequential access to
T
samples in an unknown pure qubit quantum state
|\psi\rangle
and at each round
t\in[T]
, we select a probe direction
|\psi_{t}\rangle
and perform a measurement sampling a result
r_{t}\in\{0,1\}
distributed according to Born’s rule, i.e.,
p_{t}=\Pr[R_{t}=1]=|\langle\psi|\psi_{t}\rangle|^{2}
. Here,
r_{t}=1
indicates that the post-measurement state is
\psi_{t}
and
r_{t}=0
indicates its collapse to the orthogonal complement,
\psi_{t}^{c}
. Specifically, the distribution of the post-selected state
\xi_{t}\in\mathcal{S}_{d}
after the measurement is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pr}\left(\xi_{t}|\psi_{t}\right)=\begin{cases}|\langle\psi|\psi_{t}\rangle|^{2}\quad\text{if }\quad\xi_{t}=|\psi_{t}\rangle\!\bra{\psi_{t}}\\
1-|\langle\psi|\psi_{t}\rangle|^{2}\quad\text{if }\quad\xi_{t}=|\psi^{c}_{t}\rangle\!\bra{\psi^{c}_{t}}\\
0\quad\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(5.1)

where

\displaystyle|\psi^{c}_{t}\rangle=\frac{\ket{\psi}-\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle\ket{\psi_{t}}}{\sqrt{1-|\langle\psi|\psi_{t}\rangle|^{2}}}.

(5.2)

Then the disturbance is quantified by the cumulative infidelity between the unknown state and the post-measurement state
\rho_{t}=p_{t}\psi_{t}+(1-p_{t})\psi_{t}^{c}
, i.e., and we define it as

\displaystyle\text{Disturbance}(T):=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}1-\bra{\psi}\!\rho_{t}\!\ket{\psi}

(5.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}2\,|\!\braket{\psi|\psi_{t}}\!|^{2}\big(1-|\!\braket{\psi|\psi_{t}}\!|^{2}\big)\,.

(5.4)

As can be seen from the second equation above, the disturbance is small when the probe direction is closely aligned with the unknown state. Hence, learning the state is, in fact, necessary to minimize disturbance. However, since we are interested in tomography, we also explicitly require that the algorithm outputs an estimate
\hat{\psi}_{T}
with high fidelity to the unknown state after
T
rounds, i.e., we aim to minimize the error

\displaystyle\text{Err}(T):=1-\big|\langle\psi|\hat{\psi}_{T}\rangle\big|^{2}\,.

(5.5)

5.1.1
Minimizing disturbance with the PSMAQB

We note that the setting described in the previous section is captured by the PSMAQB framework, as it corresponds to a sequential quantum state tomography problem where the unknown state (the environment) is pure and the measurements are all rank-1 projectors. The only difference lies in the form of the figures of merit: the disturbance (
5.3
) and the regret (
2.15
). However, these are technically equivalent, as we formalize in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Let
\pi
be a policy for a
d
-dimensional PSMAQB and let
T\in\mathbb{N}
be the time horizon. Then, minimizing the cumulative regret
R_{T}(\mathbb{S}_{d}^{*},\pi,\pi)
(
2.15
) is equivalent to minimizing the disturbance
\textup{Disturbance}(T)
(
5.3
).

Proof 5.1.2
.

Defining
\Pi_{a_{t}}=|\psi_{t}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{t}|
we have that

\displaystyle\text{Disturbance}(T)=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{t=1}^{T}\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle(1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle)

(5.6)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{t=1}^{T}(1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle)

(5.7)

\displaystyle=2R_{T}(\mathbb{S}_{d}^{*},\pi,\psi)

(5.8)

where we used
\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle\leq 1
. For the reverse inequality, we can use

\displaystyle\text{Disturbance}(T)\geq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\min\{1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle,\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle\},

(5.9)

combined with
\min\{1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle,\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle\}=1-\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle
where we used that for
\langle\psi|\Pi_{a_{t}}|\psi\rangle\leq\frac{1}{2}
we can just relabel the binary outcomes of the POVM
(\Pi_{a_{t}},\mathbb{I}-\Pi_{a_{t}})
.

With the above lemma, we can say that in fact the PSMAQB setting and the tomography setting are equivalent, and we can study policies that minimize regret since they will also minimize the disturbance (
5.3
). This is the main reason why we call this setting
quantum state tomography with minimal regret
. In the following theorem, we have the specific instance of
LinUCB-VNN
(Algorithm
7
) for
d=3
that applies to the qubit PSMAQB setting. We state the regret and the infidelity scaling of the estimator, since in quantum state tomography, we are also interested in minimizing the error (
5.5
).

\begin{overpic}[figures/psmaqb_regret_infidelity.png]
\put(-6.0,35.0){\rotatebox{90.0}{Regret($T$)}}
\put(50.0,-2.0){$T$}
\put(32.0,25.0){\rotatebox{90.0}{\tiny$\log\left(1-F(\Pi,\Pi_{t})\right)$}}
\put(60.0,8.0){\tiny$\log\left(\frac{t}{\log(t)}\right)$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 5.1:
Expected regret vs the number or rounds
T
for the
LinUCB-VNN
algorithm. We run
T=4\cdot 10^{4}
rounds with
k=10
subsamples for the median of means construction. We use
100
independents experiments and average over them. We obtain results for each round but only plot (red crosses) few for clarity of the figure. We fit the regression
\text{Regret}(T)=m_{1}\log^{2}T+b_{1}
with
m_{1}=3.2164\pm 0.0009
and
b_{1}=0.84\pm 0.016
. In the inset plot we plot the expected infidelity of the output estimator at each rounds
t\in[T]
versus the number of rounds
t
. We take
\Pi_{t}=\Pi_{\theta^{\text{wMoM}}_{t}}
as the estimator given by the median of means linear least squares estimator. We fit the regression
1-F(\Pi,\Pi_{t})=b_{2}\left(\frac{\log t}{t}\right)^{m_{2}}
and we obtain
m_{2}=-0.996\pm 0.002

b_{2}=0.112\pm 0.007
. We note that the number of subsamples of the theoretical results is very conservative in comparison with the value we take for the simulations.

\begin{overpic}[figures/algorithm_psmaqb.png]
\put(50.0,70.0){\rotatebox{80.0}{$|\psi^{+}_{a_{t}}\rangle$}}
\put(70.0,42.0){\rotatebox{0.0}{$|\psi^{-}_{a_{t}}\rangle$}}
\put(75.0,85.0){\rotatebox{0.0}{$\mathcal{C}_{t}$}}
\put(82.0,70.0){{$|\widehat{\psi}_{t}\rangle$}}
\put(90.0,60.0){{$|\psi\rangle$}}
\par\end{overpic}

Figure 5.2:
Sketch of Algorithm
7
that minimizes the disturbance. At each time step it computes an estimator
|\widehat{\psi}_{t}\rangle
and builds a high-probability confidence region
\mathcal{C}_{t}
(shaded region) around the unknown state
|\psi\rangle
on the Bloch sphere representation. Then uses the optimistic principle to output measurement directions
|\psi^{\pm}_{a_{t}}\rangle
that are close the unknown state
\ket{\psi}
projecting into the Bloch sphere the extreme points of the largest principal axis of
\mathcal{C}_{t}
. This particular choice allows optimal learning of
\ket{\psi}
(exploration) and simultaneously minimizes the regret or disturbance (exploitation).

Theorem 5.1.3
.

Let
\widetilde{T}\in\mathbb{N}
and fix the time horizon
T=\lceil 96\widetilde{T}\log(\widetilde{T}^{2})\rceil
. Then given a
PSMAQB
with qubit action set
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2}
and qubit environment
|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2}
, we can apply Algorithm
7
for
d=3
and it achieves

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi,\pi}\left[R_{T}(\mathbb{S}_{2}^{*},\pi,\psi)\right]\leq C_{1}\log\left(T\right)+C_{2}\log^{2}\left(T\right).

(5.10)

for some universal constants
C_{1},C_{2}\geq 0
. Also at each time step
t\in[T]
, it outputs an estimator
\hat{\Pi}_{t}\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2}
of
\psi
with infidelity scaling

\displaystyle\EX_{\psi,\pi}\left[1-\langle\psi|\hat{\Pi}_{t}|\psi\rangle\right]\leq\frac{C_{3}\log(T)}{t},

(5.11)

for some universal constant
C_{3}\geq 0
.

Proof 5.1.4
.

In order to apply Algorithm
7
to a PSMAQB, we set
d=3
(dimension for a classical linear stochastic bandit) and the actions that we select will be given by
\Pi_{A^{\pm}_{t,i}}\in\mathbb{S}^{*}_{2}
where
A^{\pm}_{t,i}
are the Bloch vectors of
\Pi_{A^{\pm}_{t,i}}\in\mathbb{S}^{*}_{2}
and are updated as in (
4.304
). Note that they are valid actions since
A^{\pm}_{t,i}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
implies
\Pi_{A^{\pm}_{t,i}}\in\mathcal{S}^{*}_{2}
. In order to update the action in the right form, we need to renormalize the rewards
X_{t}\in\{0,1\}
received by the algorithm as

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{t}=2X_{t}-1,

(5.12)

so we have the guarantee
\EX[\tilde{X}_{t}]=\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle
, where
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
is the Bloch vector of the unknown
|\psi\rangle
and
A_{t}
is the one of the measurements
\Pi_{A_{t}}
. Then we have that the variance of the statistical noise of
\tilde{X}_{t}
has the following form

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}[\tilde{\epsilon_{t}}]=1-\langle\theta,A_{t}\rangle^{2}.

(5.13)

Thus, this model fits into the linear bandit with linearly vanishing variance noise model explained in Section
4.5.2
, and we can apply the guarantees established in Theorem
4.5.12
and Corollary
4.5.14
.

The algorithm is set with
k=\lceil 24\log(\widetilde{T}^{2})\rceil
batches for the median of means construction. We set
\lambda_{0}=2
, and using
\|\theta\|_{2}=1

we have that the constant
\beta
given in (
4.302
) has the value

\displaystyle\beta=9\left(3\sqrt{3}+2\right)^{2}=279+108\sqrt{3}.

(5.14)

Then we can check that for
d=3
, the condition (
4.317
) for the input parameter
\lambda_{0}
for Theorem
4.5.12
to hold is satisfied since

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}=2\geq\max\left\{2,\frac{1}{3}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{6}(279+108\sqrt{3})}\right\}=2.

(5.15)

In the above, we just substituted all numerical values. Then we are under the assumptions of Theorem
4.5.12
and Corollary
4.5.14
, and the result follows by applying both results with the relation of regrets between the classical and quantum model. Then we also have

\displaystyle\|\theta-\hat{\theta}_{t}\|_{2}^{2}=4\left(1-F\left(\Pi_{\theta},\Pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}\right)\right),

(5.16)

where take the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{t}
given in Theorem
4.5.12
for
d=3
and we use it as the Bloch vector of
\Pi_{\hat{\theta}_{t}}
. We also use the bound
\widetilde{T}\leq T
and reabsorb all the constants into
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}
.

From the above theorem, we have the following remarks.

Remark 1.
The constant dependence can be slightly improved taking the estimator for
\Pi_{\theta}
as
\Pi_{\theta^{\text{wMoM}}_{t}}
with
\hat{\theta}^{\text{wMoM}}_{t}
defined in Section
4.5.2
.

Remark 2.
The result of Theorem
5.1.3
also holds with high probability. In particular for the choice of batches
k=24\log(\widetilde{T}^{2})
the guarantee holds with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{\widetilde{T}}
.

Remark 3.
The same notion of disturbance can also be extended to mixed states recovering the general expression of the regret for general bandits

R_{T}(\mathbb{S}_{d}^{*},\pi,\rho)=\EX_{\rho,\pi}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\lambda_{\max}(\rho)-\langle\psi_{t}|\rho|\psi_{t}\rangle\right]
. For more details, see
[
73
, Lemma 3]
.

5.2
Quantum state-agnostic work extraction (almost) without dissipation

As a specific task where learning with low disturbance is important, we consider work-extraction protocols from unknown quantum sources.

Given sequential access to finite copies of an identical, unknown quantum system, what is the optimal strategy for extracting work from them and charging a battery? A natural strategy might involve first performing quantum state tomography to estimate the unknown state, followed by work extraction based on this estimate
[
57
,
109
,
110
]
. Indeed, knowledge of the quantum state is essential for efficient work extraction; consider, for example, Szilard’s engine, where the information about the system’s configuration—such as the position of a particle—enables work to be extracted
[
100
]
. However, with only finitely many copies, any estimate of the true state has statistical uncertainty
[
82
,
50
]
, resulting in unavoidable heat dissipation during work extraction
[
86
]
. Furthermore, quantum systems that are measured during state tomography are no longer available for work extraction and must therefore be accounted for as a form of dissipation.

This raises a natural question: can adaptive strategies, which simultaneously learn the unknown state and extract work, offer better performance?
The extraction of useful work from available resources has been a central problem in classical thermodynamics and continues to attract significant attention in the quantum domain. While numerous protocols have been proposed
[
12
,
8
,
27
,
96
,
44
]
, the majority assume that the agent has full knowledge of the quantum state—these are so-called
state-aware
protocols. In practical scenarios, we may not always know how the state is prepared; in this case, how can an agent then design the protocol?

Figure 5.3:
Sketch of the work extraction protocols considered in this section. On the left, a source provides single copies of an unknown pure qubit state. A charging protocol (represented as a cartoon charger) uses each copy to attempt charging a battery. Since the state is unknown, a measurement is performed during the process to extract partial information. This measurement outcome provides feedback that can be used to improve the charging strategy in future rounds.

In this section, we investigate state-agnostic protocols for work extraction. Specifically, we are interested in how the cumulative dissipation scales with the number of available copies of an unknown quantum state. We show that adaptive strategies can simultaneously learn the unknown state and maximize the amount of work transferred to a battery system. Notably, such strategies can lead to an exponential improvement in cumulative dissipation: while tomography-based approaches yield a dissipation scaling of
\Omega(\sqrt{T})
in the number of available copies
T
, we demonstrate that a scaling of
O(\operatorname{polylog}(T))
is achievable for pure-state sources using suitable adaptive work extraction algorithms.

We consider a source of unknown pure qubit states used to charge batteries in two distinct models. First, we analyze a battery with discrete energy levels, modeled as a bosonic system where work is accumulated via a Jaynes–Cummings interaction
[
58
,
92
,
96
]
. Then, we turn to a semiclassical thermodynamic model that includes a thermal reservoir, with the battery described as a classical weight storing energy by being lifted or dropped.

The main technical contribution is an upper bound on the dissipation in both models. We establish this by connecting each setting to the PSMAQB framework and relating the cumulative dissipation to regret. Using the PSMAQB algorithm (Algorithm
7
), which achieves polylogarithmic regret, we show that the cumulative dissipation in both battery models similarly scales polylogarithmically with the number of available copies
T
.

5.2.1
Discrete battery

In this setting, we model the
battery
as a quantum harmonic oscillator with Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{B}=\omega a^{\dagger}a,

(5.17)

where
\omega>0
is the oscillator frequency, and
a^{\dagger}
,
a
are the creation and annihilation operators, respectively, defined as

\displaystyle a=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{n}\ket{n-1}\bra{n},\quad a^{\dagger}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{n+1}\ket{n+1}\bra{n},

(5.18)

where
|n\rangle
are the eigenvectors of
H_{B}
with eigenvalue
\omega n
for
n=0,1,2,....
.

We assume sequential access to
T
copies of an unknown pure qubit state
\ket{\psi}
. The goal is to design a protocol that uses these unknown states to charge the battery by increasing its energy level over time.

At each time step
t
, the battery is assumed to be in an energy eigenstate
\ket{n_{t}}
and the learner holds the state
|\psi\rangle\ket{n_{t}}
. The protocol proceeds as follows:

1.

Direction Selection:
Choose a direction
\psi_{t}
on the Bloch sphere. Apply a local Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{A}=\omega\ket{\psi_{t}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}}

(5.19)

to the incoming qubit
\ket{\psi}
, effectively projecting the qubit into the chosen basis.

2.

Interaction with the battery:
Use a Jaynes–Cummings-type interaction for a fixed time
\tilde{t}
:

\displaystyle H_{I}=\frac{\Omega}{2}\left(a\otimes\ket{\psi_{t}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}^{c}}+a^{\dagger}\otimes\ket{\psi_{t}^{c}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}}\right),

(5.20)

where
\Omega>0
controls the interaction strength.

After the interaction, a measurement is performed on the battery to determine whether it has successfully absorbed energy. If the interaction time is set to

\displaystyle\tilde{t}=\frac{\pi}{\Omega\sqrt{n_{t}+1}},

(5.21)

then the battery may transition to one of the following energy levels:

\ket{n_{t}+1},\quad\ket{n_{t}},\quad\text{or}\quad\ket{n_{t}-1}.

A transition to
\ket{n_{t}+1}
indicates a successful charging event, i.e., the battery has gained one quantum of energy. The task is to adaptively choose the direction
\psi_{t}
at each round to maximize the number of successful charges over
T
rounds.

We summarize this charging procedure in Algorithm
8
.

for

t=1,2,\cdots

do

Get new copy of
|\psi\rangle
.

Choose direction
\psi_{t}
.

Expose
|\psi\rangle
to a field induced by Hamiltonian
H_{A}=\omega\ket{\psi_{t}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}}
.

For a time
\tilde{t}=\pi\Omega^{-1}(n_{t}+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, turn on the interaction between the system and the battery with Hamiltonian is
H_{I}=\frac{\Omega}{2}(a\otimes\ket{\psi_{t}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}^{c}}+a^{\dagger}\otimes\ket{\psi_{t}^{c}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}})

Measure battery system and get output
n_{t+1}
.

end

for

Algorithm 8

Jaynes-Cummings work extraction

Dissipation

We now compute the expected energy transferred to the battery system when interacting with the unknown state using the chosen direction
\psi_{t}
. Based on this, we define the
dissipation
at each time step
t
as the difference between the maximum achievable energy input and the actual energy input obtained using
\psi_{t}
.

At each time step
t\in[T]
, the total Hamiltonian of the system is

\displaystyle H=\omega(\ket{\psi_{t}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}}+a^{\dagger}a)+\frac{\Omega}{2}(a\otimes\ket{\psi_{t}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}^{c}}+a^{\dagger}\otimes\ket{\psi_{t}^{c}}\!\bra{\psi_{t}}),

(5.22)

One can verify that the eigenstates of the total Hamiltonian are

\displaystyle\ket{0}\ket{\psi_{t}^{c}}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\ket{n,+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{n-1}\ket{\psi_{t}}+\ket{n}\ket{\psi_{t}^{c}})

(5.24)

\displaystyle\ket{n,-}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{n-1}\ket{\psi_{t}}-\ket{n}\ket{\psi_{t}^{c}}),

(5.25)

for
n=1,2,\ldots
and the eigenvalues are respectively

\displaystyle E_{0}=0,\quad E_{n+}=n\omega+\frac{\Omega}{2}\sqrt{n},\quad E_{n-}=n\omega-\frac{\Omega}{2}\sqrt{n}.

(5.26)

The state at time step
t\in[T]
is
\ket{n_{t}}\ket{\psi}
and can be decomposed into
4
eigenstates
\ket{n_{t},\pm}
and
\ket{n_{t}+1,\pm}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\ket{n_{t}}\ket{\psi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle\psi_{t}^{c}|\psi\rangle(\ket{n_{t},+}-\ket{n_{t},-})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle(\ket{n_{t}+1,+}+\ket{n_{t}+1,-}).

(5.27)

These eigenstates gain phase factors during the time evolution. After time
t_{k}=\pi\Omega^{-1}(n_{t}+1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, the state evolves to, up to an irrelevant global phase,

\displaystyle e^{-iHt_{k}}\ket{n_{t}}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\langle\psi_{t}^{c}|\psi\rangle(e^{i\theta_{k}}\ket{n_{t},+}-e^{-i\theta_{k}}\ket{n_{t},-})

(5.28)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}e^{i\frac{\omega\pi}{\Omega\sqrt{n_{t}+1}}}\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle(i\ket{n_{t}+1,+}-i\ket{n_{t}+1,-}),

(5.29)

which rearranged gives

\displaystyle e^{-iHt_{k}}\ket{n_{t}}\ket{\psi}

\displaystyle=\langle\psi_{t}^{c}|\psi\rangle(i\sin\theta_{k}\ket{n_{t}-1}\ket{\psi_{t}}+\cos\theta_{k}\ket{n_{t}}\ket{\psi_{t}^{c}})

(5.30)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}ie^{i\frac{\omega\pi}{\Omega\sqrt{n_{t}+1}}}\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle\ket{n_{t}+1}\ket{\psi_{t}^{c}},

(5.31)

where
\theta_{t}=\frac{\pi}{2}\sqrt{\frac{n_{t}}{n_{t}+1}}
. After the evolution we finally measure the battery energy. The measurement outcome is
n_{t+1}
. The probability distribution of
n_{t+1}
can be computed from
5.30
is given by

\displaystyle\Pr(n_{k+1})=\begin{cases}|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2},\quad&n_{t+1}=n_{t}+1,\\
|\langle\psi_{t}^{c}|\psi\rangle|^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{t},\quad&n_{k+1}=n_{t},\\
|\langle\psi_{t}^{c}|\psi\rangle|^{2}\sin^{2}\theta_{t},\quad&n_{t+1}=n_{t}-1.\end{cases}

(5.32)

The extracted work is defined as
\Delta W_{k}=\omega(n_{t+1}-n_{t})
. The expected extracted work is then given by

\displaystyle\EX[\Delta W_{t}]=\omega(|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2}-|\langle\psi_{t}^{c}|\psi\rangle|^{2}\sin^{2}\theta_{t})=\omega(|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2}(1+\sin^{2}\theta_{t})-\sin^{2}\theta_{t}),

(5.33)

where we have used
|\langle\psi_{t}^{c}|\psi\rangle|^{2}=1-|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2}
. Therefore, we can compute the maximal extracted work as

\displaystyle\max_{\ket{\psi_{t}}}\EX[\Delta W_{t}]=\omega,

(5.34)

where the maximum is achieved if
\psi_{t}=\psi
. The dissipation in this round is thus given by the difference between the maximal and the actual expected extracted work

\displaystyle\dissipation^{\text{jc},t}:=\max_{\ket{\psi_{t}}}\EX[\Delta W_{t}]-\EX[\Delta W_{t}]=\omega(1+\sin^{2}\theta_{t}^{2})(1-|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2})\leq 2\omega(1-|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2}).

(5.35)

Cumulative dissipation and regret

Recall that the goal is to maximize the energy transferred to the battery system, or equivalently, to minimize the cumulative dissipation. Using the expression for the dissipation at each time step
t
(
5.35
), we find that the cumulative dissipation over
T
rounds is bounded by

\displaystyle\dissipation^{\text{jc}}(T):=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\dissipation^{\text{jc},t}\leq 2\omega\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(1-|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2}\big).

(5.36)

It turns out that the right-hand side matches the regret expression in the PSMAQB setting. Moreover, given the measured battery energy level
n_{t}
at each time step
t
, we can define a reward as follows:

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }n_{t+1}=n_{t}+1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(5.37)

The probability distribution of this reward is

\displaystyle\Pr[X_{t}=x_{t}]=\begin{cases}|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2},&\text{if }x_{t}=1,\\
1-|\langle\psi_{t}|\psi\rangle|^{2},&\text{if }x_{t}=0,\end{cases}

(5.38)

which coincides with the reward distribution in the PSMAQB setting. Thus, we can adaptively select the directions
\psi_{t}
using Algorithm
7
(on the Bloch sphere representation) and we arrive to the following result.

Theorem 5.2.1
.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
be a finite time horizon. Then, there exists an update rule for the directions
\{\psi_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
in the work extraction protocol described in Algorithm
8
, based on the past rewards
\{x_{s}\}_{s=1}^{t-1}
as defined in (
5.37
), that guarantees, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\dissipation^{\text{jc}}(T)=O\left(\omega\log(T)\log\left(\frac{T}{\delta}\right)\right).

(5.39)

Note.
From the explore-then-commit algorithm studied in Section
4.2
, we have that an approach that allocates a fraction of the copies
\psi
to the learning and the remaining for extracting work at most can achieve
\dissipation^{\text{jc}}(T)=O(\omega\sqrt{T})
.

5.2.2
Continuous battery

In this section, we consider the work extraction model studied in
[
96
,
56
]
, where the goal is to extract energy from a qubit system in contact with a thermal bath and transfer it into a semi-classical battery, represented by a weight. The battery stores or releases energy through the raising or lowering of this weight, corresponding to changes in its potential energy. In our setting, we assume that the qubit system is initially unknown. The three physical subsystems involved in the process are:

•

System
A
(Unknown Pure State Source):
A qubit in a pure state
\psi_{A}=\ket{\psi}\!\bra{\psi}
with a degenerate Hamiltonian
H_{A}=\omega\mathbb{I}/2
(same eigenvalues). This is the system from which free energy is to be extracted.

•

System
B
(Battery):
A semi-classical weight described by a continuous-variable state
\varphi(x)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. The battery Hamiltonian is defined as
H_{B}\varphi(x-x_{0})=x\varphi(x-x_{0})
, where
x
represents the height of the weight. We will assume
\varphi(x-x_{0})
to be a battery state whose energy is sharply centered at
x_{0}
. The energy of the battery can be changed by translating the weight up by a certain height
x_{0}
, described by the translation operator
\Gamma^{B}_{x_{0}}\varphi(x)=\varphi(x-x_{0})
.

•

System
R
(Thermal Reservoir):
A heat bath at a fixed inverse temperature
\beta
, modeled as a supply of qubit states
\gamma_{\beta}(\nu)=Z_{R}(\nu)^{-1}e^{-\beta H_{R}(\nu)}
, where the Hamiltonian is
H_{R}(\nu)=\nu\ket{1}\!\bra{1}
and
Z_{R}(\nu)=\Tr(e^{-\beta H_{R}(\nu)})
is the partition function. Here,
\ket{0}
and
\ket{1}
are energy eigenstates, and
\nu
is the energy gap. This energy gap can be tuned.

We consider an agent with oracle access to the unknown system
A
over a finite number of rounds
T\in\mathbb{N}
. The goal is to extract the full free energy of
A
and store it in the battery
B
through interactions with a thermal reservoir
R
. However, since the state
\psi
of the system is unknown, the agent cannot extract work optimally from the outset. Instead, it must gradually improve its strategy by learning from each round, where the learning is done by measuring the battery.

The work extraction protocol is defined by two key components: a policy that updates the agent’s guess
\psi_{t}
of the unknown state
\psi
at each round, and a sequence
\{\epsilon_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
that determines the accuracy of these guesses. Both quantities can be chosen adaptively and together, as we will compute later, they determine the dissipation at each round. We first present the general structure of the protocol and later discuss how the dissipation depends on these choices and how to optimize them for best performance. The protocol proceeds as follows at each round
t\in[T]
:

1.

The agent receives a copy of the unknown qubit state
\psi
.

2.

Based on the outcomes from previous rounds, the agent selects a direction
\psi_{t}
on the Bloch sphere, sets an accuracy
\epsilon_{t}\in[0,1]
and defines a basis
\{\psi_{t},\psi_{t}^{c}\}
for system
A
. This computation is done using the previously selected directions and measured battery energies
\{\psi_{s},\mu_{s}\}_{s=1}^{t-1}
.

3.

We first implement a unitary on the system qubit in the form of

U_{t}=\ket{0}\!\bra{\psi_{t}}+\ket{1}\!\bra{\psi_{t}^{c}},

(5.40)

satisfying
[H_{A},U_{t}]=0
, which tries to diagonalize the system qubit in the computational basis.

4.

The agent then performs a thermal operation by repeatedly appending a reservoir qubit
R
, applying an energy-conserving unitary on the combined system
ABR
to transfer energy from
R
to the battery
B
, and discarding
R
. Specifically, the agent will do the following for
M\in\mathbb{N}
iterations (indexed by
\tau\in[M]
):

•

Sets the energy gap of the reservoir qubit to
\nu(\tau,\epsilon_{t})
(depends on repetition
\tau
and accuracy
\epsilon_{t}
, see (
5.47
)) and gets a fresh qubit state
\gamma_{\beta}(\nu(\tau,\epsilon_{t}))
.

•

Applies the following unitary

\displaystyle V_{\psi_{t},\tau}=\sum_{i,j}\ket{i}\!\bra{j}_{A}\otimes\ket{j}\!\bra{i}_{R}\otimes\Gamma^{B}_{(i-j)\nu(\tau,\epsilon_{t})},

(5.41)

satisfying
[H_{A}+H_{B}+H_{R},V_{\psi_{t},\tau}]=0
to
ABR
.

•

Discards the reservoir qubit.

5.

After completing the
M
steps, the agent measures the energy of the battery in its eigenbasis and records the energy
\mu_{t}
.

Going back to the protocol, we note that the unitary
V_{\psi_{t},\tau}
in (
5.41
) swaps the states of the unknown system
A
and the thermal reservoir
R
. This operation induces an energy exchange determined by the energy gap
\nu(\tau,\epsilon_{t})
between the two systems. The energy difference is transferred to the battery through a translation operation
\Gamma^{B}_{(i-j)\nu(\tau,\epsilon_{t})}
to conserve the total energy of the combined system.

To maximize the extractable non-equilibrium free energy from system
A
, it is essential to minimize dissipation during the energy transfer to the battery. This can be achieved by ensuring that the process is
quasi-static
— that is, slow and nearly reversible. Under such conditions, the system qubit remains close to the thermal state of the reservoir’s Hamiltonian, thereby suppressing heat flow during the interaction. Conversely, if the process is carried out rapidly, i.e., the system state deviates from the reservoir’s thermal state, the system will appear out of thermal equilibrium with the reservoir, resulting in heat exchange, which contributes to the entropy production during the protocol. The quasi-static limit can be approximated when
M\rightarrow\infty
, and in the next section, we are going to check that under this limit, at each round
t\in[T]
, the extracted work into the battery is

\displaystyle\EX[\Delta W_{t}]=\beta^{-1}\left[D(\psi\|\mathbb{I}/2)-D(\psi\|\Delta_{2\epsilon_{t}}(\psi_{t}))\right],

(5.42)

where
\Delta_{\epsilon}(\rho)=(1-\epsilon)\rho+\epsilon\id/2
denotes the depolarizing channel. The maximal expected work per round,
\beta^{-1}D(\psi\|\mathbb{I}/2)
, is achieved when the agent perfectly estimates the state, i.e., when
\psi_{t}=\psi
and
\epsilon_{t}=0
. Accordingly, we define the dissipation at round
t
as

\displaystyle\dissipation^{t}:=\max_{\psi_{t},\epsilon_{t}}\mathbb{E}[\Delta W_{t}]-\mathbb{E}[\Delta W_{t}]=\beta^{-1}D(\psi\|\Delta_{2\epsilon_{t}}(\psi_{t})).

(5.43)

This expression highlights a key trade-off: to reduce dissipation, the agent must align
\psi_{t}
with the true state
\psi
, but it cannot set
\epsilon_{t}
too small unless the estimate is sufficiently accurate, as otherwise the divergence becomes large. The parameter
\epsilon_{t}
thus plays a dual role, quantifying both the uncertainty in the estimate and its thermodynamic penalty. The agent’s objective is to extract the maximum amount of work into the battery using the
N
copies of the unknown system. Equivalently, the agent aims to minimize the cumulative dissipation over
N
rounds, which is given by

\displaystyle\dissipation(T)=\beta^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\dissipation^{t}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}D(\psi\|\Delta_{2\epsilon_{t}}(\psi_{t})).

(5.44)

Dissipation in the quasi-static limit
M\rightarrow\infty

In order to simplify the notation, we are going to compute the dissipation in a single round of the protocol. Thus we fix a direction
\hat{\psi}
and an accuracy
\epsilon\in[0,1]
,
and define the following quantities

\displaystyle p_{0}:=1-\epsilon,\quad|\phi_{0}\rangle=|\hat{\psi}\rangle,

(5.45)

\displaystyle p_{1}:=\epsilon,\quad|\phi_{1}\rangle=|\hat{\psi}^{c}\rangle.

(5.46)

We are going to use the following parametrization of the energy gap at repetition
\tau\in[M]

\displaystyle\nu(\tau,\epsilon)=\beta^{-1}\ln\left(\frac{1-\frac{\tau}{2M}-\left(1-\frac{\tau}{M}\right)\epsilon}{\frac{\tau}{2M}+\left(1-\frac{\tau}{M}\right)\epsilon}\right).

(5.47)

For
\tau\in[M]
and
i\in\{0,1\}
, we also define the following quantities

\displaystyle p_{i,\tau}:=p_{i}-(-1)^{i}\tau\delta p,\quad\delta p:=\frac{1}{M}(p_{0}-\frac{1}{2}).

(5.48)

We state the work extraction for a single repetition in Algorithm
9
.

Algorithm 9

Thermal work extraction

1:

Require: An unknown system state
\psi
, a direction
\hat{\psi}
, accuracy
\epsilon\in[0,1]
a battery state
\varphi(x)
with the battery energy
\mu
, a reservoir at inverse temperature
\beta
.

Unitary Rotation

2:

Apply unitary
U=\ket{0}\!\bra{\hat{\psi}}+\ket{1}\!\bra{\hat{\psi}^{c}}
to the unknown system
\psi

3:

for

\tau=1,2,\ldots,M

do

Prepare a fresh reservoir qubit and exchange it with the system

4:

Take a fresh thermal qubit
\gamma_{\beta}(\nu(\tau,\epsilon))=\frac{1}{Z_{R}(\nu(\tau,\epsilon))}e^{-\beta H_{R}(\nu(\tau,\epsilon))}
with
\nu(\tau,\epsilon)
defined as in (
5.47
)

5:

Apply the swap unitary
V_{\rho^{*},\tau}=\sum_{ij}\ket{i}\!\bra{j}_{A}\otimes\ket{j}\bra{i}_{R}\otimes\Gamma_{(i-j)\nu(\tau,\epsilon)}
on the system
A,B,R
.

6:

Discard the reservoir qubit.

7:

end

for
Measure the extracted work

Measure the battery energy, obtain the battery energy
\mu^{\prime}
, and compute the extracted work
\Delta W=\mu^{\prime}-\mu
.

In the first stage of the protocol, we rotate the unknown qubit via the unitary

U=\sum_{i}\ket{i}\!\bra{\phi_{i}}~.

(5.49)

This operation attempts to diagonalize the system qubit in the computational basis.
We then interact the system with the battery. The state of the system together with the battery is

\rho_{AB}=\sum_{ij}\bra{\phi_{i}}\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi\ket{\phi_{j}}\ket{i}\!\bra{j}_{A}\otimes\varphi(x)_{B}.

(5.50)

Then the unitary (
5.41
) swaps the system and the fresh qubit from the reservoir, extracts work into the battery due to the different energy gap between
\{\ket{i}_{A}\}_{i\in\{0,1\}}
and
\{\ket{i}_{R}\}_{i\in\{0,1\}}
and conserves energy of the system, the qubit from the reservoir and the battery. Finally, the qubit from the reservoir is discarded. At the end of each repetition
\tau
, the reduced state is

\displaystyle\rho_{AB,\tau}=\Tr_{R}\left(V_{\rho^{*},\tau}\left(\rho_{AB,\tau-1}\otimes\gamma_{\beta}(\nu(\tau,\epsilon))\right)V_{\rho^{*},\tau}^{\dagger}\right),

(5.51)

After the first repetition, we obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{AB,1}=\sum_{i}p_{i,1}\ket{i}\!\bra{i}_{A}\otimes\rho_{B,i,1},

(5.52)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{B,i,1}=\sum_{j}|\langle\phi_{j}|\psi\rangle|^{2}\varphi(x-(i-j)\nu(\tau,\epsilon)),

(5.53)

and after repetition
\tau
where
\tau\geq 2
, we obtain

\displaystyle\rho_{AB,\tau}=\sum_{i}p_{i,\tau}\ket{i}\!\bra{i}_{A}\otimes\rho_{B,i,\tau},

(5.54)

where

\displaystyle\rho_{B,i,\tau}=\sum_{j}p_{j,\tau-1}\Gamma_{(i-j)\nu(\tau,\epsilon)}\rho_{B,j,\tau-1}\Gamma_{(i-j)\nu(\tau,\epsilon)}^{\dagger}.

(5.55)

From (
5.54
), we observe that the reduced state of the system changes gradually, which resembles a quasi-static process in thermodynamics. This is the reason why we take the swap unitary in repetition.

We will need the following Lagrange mean value theorem and the first mean value theorem for definite integrals
[
98
]
as follows.

Theorem 5.2.2
.

Let
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function on the closed interval
[a,b]
and differentiable on the open interval
(a,b)
. Then there exists
c\in(a,b)
such that

\displaystyle f(b)-f(a)=f^{\prime}(c)(b-a).

(5.56)

Theorem 5.2.3
.

Let
f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function on the closed interval
[a,b]
. Then there exists
c\in(a,b)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}f(x)\textnormal{d}x=f(c)(b-a).

(5.57)

We will show the following theorem in the following subsection.

Theorem 5.2.4
.

Let
\hat{\psi}
be the input direction and
\epsilon\in[0,1]
the accuracy,
\Delta W
be the extracted work (which is a continuous random variable) and
M
be the number of repetitions as in Algorithm
9
. It holds that: if the extraction protocol is operated on a state
\psi
such that
\psi=\hat{\psi}
or
\psi=\hat{\psi}^{c}
, then the expected extracted work
\EX[\Delta W]
converges to a fixed value
w_{i}
and the extracted work
\Delta W
converges in probability to its expectation
\EX[\Delta W]
. To be precise, it means

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\EX[\Delta W]=w_{i},

(5.58)

where

\displaystyle w_{0}

\displaystyle:=\beta^{-1}(D(\psi\|\id/2)+\ln\epsilon),

(5.59)

\displaystyle w_{1}

\displaystyle:=\beta^{-1}(D(\psi^{c}\|\id/2)+\ln(1-\epsilon))

(5.60)

and for any
\tilde{\epsilon}>0

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\Pr[|\Delta W-\EX[\Delta W]|\geq\tilde{\epsilon}]=0.

(5.61)

Proof 5.2.5
.

We consider the notation we defined in (
5.45
). We assume that
p_{0}>\frac{1}{2}
as we deal with an estimate for a pure state in Algorithm
9
, although a similar proof holds for other cases. According to (
5.52
), the state after the first repetition can be viewed as a classical state described as follows: the state after repetition
1
is
\phi_{x_{1}}
where
x_{1}
is a random bit sampled from
\{0,1\}
according to the probability distribution
(p_{0,1},p_{1,1})
; the extracted work after the first repetition conditioned on
x_{1}
is
(x_{1}-i)\nu(1,\epsilon)
. According to (
5.54
), the evolution in repetition
\tau
where
\tau\geq 2
can be viewed as a classical process described as follows: the state after repetition
\tau
is
\phi_{x_{\tau}}
, where
x_{\tau}
is a random bit sampled from
\{0,1\}
according to the probability distribution
(p_{0,\tau},p_{1,\tau})
; the extracted work in repetition
\tau
conditioned on
x_{\tau-1}x_{\tau}
and is
(x_{\tau}-x_{\tau-1})\nu(\tau,\epsilon)
. Suppose that the random bits sampled during the above process is
x_{1}\ldots x_{M}
after
M
repetitions. The extracted work after repetition
M
conditioned on
x_{1}\ldots x_{M}
is

\displaystyle\Delta W

\displaystyle=(x_{1}-i)\nu(1,\epsilon)+\sum_{\tau=2}^{M}(x_{\tau}-x_{\tau-1})\nu(\tau,\epsilon)

(5.62)

\displaystyle=-i\nu(1,\epsilon)+\sum_{\tau=1}^{M-1}x_{\tau}(\nu(\tau,\epsilon)-\nu(\tau+1,\epsilon))+x_{M}\nu(M,\epsilon).

(5.63)

We use the expression of the gap (
5.47
) which can be expressed as

\displaystyle\nu(\tau,\epsilon)=\beta^{-1}\ln\frac{p_{0}-\tau\delta p}{p_{1}+\tau\delta p}.

(5.64)

where
\delta p=\frac{1}{M}(p_{0}-\frac{1}{2})
.
The expected extracted work is

\displaystyle\EX[\Delta W]=-i\nu(1,\epsilon)+\sum_{\tau=1}^{M-1}\EX[x_{\tau}](\nu(\tau,\epsilon)-\nu(\tau+1,\epsilon))+\EX[x_{M}]\nu(M,\epsilon).

(5.65)

Notice that, from the definition of
x_{\tau}
,
\EX[x_{\tau}]=p_{1,\tau}=p_{1}+\tau\delta p
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\EX[\Delta W]

\displaystyle=-i\beta^{-1}\ln\frac{p_{0}-\delta p}{p_{1}+\delta p}+\beta^{-1}\sum_{\tau=1}^{M-1}\left(\ln\frac{p_{0}-\tau\delta p}{p_{1}+\tau\delta p}-\ln\frac{p_{0}-(\tau+1)\delta p}{p_{1}+(\tau+1)\delta p}\right)(p_{1}+\tau\delta p).

(5.66)

We now use the definition of
\nu(\tau,\epsilon)
in (
5.64
) as well as the Lagrange mean value theorem in Theorem
5.2.2
to obtain
